From Many-body Quantum Dynamics to the Hartree–Fock and Vlasov Equations with Singular Potentials
Jacky J. Chong, Laurent Lafleche, Chiara Saffirio

Abstract
. We obtain the combined mean-field and semiclassical limit from the N N -body Schrödinger equation for fermions interacting via singular potentials. To obtain the result, we first prove the uniformity in Planck’s constant h h propagation of regularity for solutions to the Hartree–Fock equation with singular pair interaction potentials of the form ± | x − y | − a \pm|x-y|^{-a} , including the Coulomb and gravitational interactions. In the context of mixed states, we use these regularity properties to obtain quantitative estimates on the distance between solutions to the Schrödinger equation and solutions to the Hartree–Fock and Vlasov equations in Schatten norms. For a ∈ ( 0 , 1 / 2 ) a\in(0,1/2) , we obtain local-in-time results when N − 1 / 2 ≪ h ≤ N − 1 / 3 N^{-1/2}\ll h\leq N^{-1/3} . In particular, it leads to the derivation of the Vlasov equation with singular potentials. For a ∈ [ 1 / 2 , 1 ] a\in[1/2,1] , our results hold only on a small time scale, or with an N N -dependent cutoff.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
82C10, 35Q41, 35Q55 (82C05,35Q83).

Part I
Introduction

1.
Background

1.1.
The Equations

We consider a system of
N
identical fermions with unit mass interacting through a pair potential
K(x-y)
. The state of the system is described by an
N
-body anti-symmetric wave function
\psi_{N}=\psi_{N}(t,x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N})
belonging to the Hilbert space of square-integrable complex-valued functions
\mathfrak{h}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3N},\mathbb{C})
, with evolution given by the
N
-body Schrödinger equation

(1)

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\psi_{N}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\Delta_{x_{k}}\psi_{N}+\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq N}K(x_{k}-x_{l})\,\psi_{N},

where
h
is the Planck constant and
\hbar=\frac{h}{2\,\pi}
is the reduced Planck constant. In applications, one is typically interested in systems where the number of particles
N
is large, thus making the microscopic description given by the solution to Equation (
1
) not suitable for studies. In fact, the high dimensionality of the problem presents a formidable barrier for understanding qualitative behaviors of the many-body dynamics from the wave function at the microscopic scale. Instead, one could consider the problem at a macroscopic scale and look at the classical phase space distributions of particles
f=f(t,x,\xi)
, where
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
are the spatial and momentum variables. In particular, we consider scales where the dynamics of a large number of interacting particles can be approximated by the Vlasov equation

(2)

\partial_{t}f+\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}f+E_{f}\cdot\nabla_{\xi}f=0,

where
E_{f}=-\nabla V_{f}
is the force field corresponding to the mean-field potential

V_{f}(x)=(K*\rho_{f})(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}K(x-y)\,\rho_{f}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

and
\rho_{f}
is the spatial distribution of particles defined by

(3)

\rho_{f}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(x,\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

To explore the connection between the microscopic and the macroscopic scales of the system, we consider an intermediate mean-field quantum equation. Roughly speaking, we approximate the many-body effects exerted by the system on each particle by an
effective interaction potential obtained by averaging the pair potential

K
with the underlying spatial density of the system. To draw a parallel with classical mechanics, one could consider the mean-field equation called the Hartree equation which is the quantum analogue of the Vlasov equation.
More precisely, let us take a positive self-adjoint trace class operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}
acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C})
, which can be seen as a positive linear convex combination of projections onto one-particle wave functions. We use the same notation to denote both the operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}
and its integral kernel
\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y)
. Here,
\boldsymbol{\rho}
plays the role of the quantum one-particle phase space distribution of particles. Moreover, the effective one-particle Hamiltonian is given by
H=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\Delta+V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, called the Hartree Hamiltonian, where
V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is the mean-field potential
V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=K*\rho(x)
and
\rho(x)
is the quantum spatial distribution of particles defined by

(4)

\rho(x)=\diag\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)(x):=h^{3}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,x).

With these notations, the Hartree equation reads

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}=\left[{H,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right],

where
\left[{A,B}\right]:=AB-BA
is the commutator of the operators
A
and
B
. If the particles obey the Fermi statistics, a more accurate description of their evolution is given by the Hartree–Fock equation

(5)

\displaystyle i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}

\displaystyle=\left[{H_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right],

\displaystyle H_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\Delta+V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-h^{3}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,,

where the exchange term
\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is the operator with integral kernel

(6)

\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x,y)=K(x-y)\,\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y).

1.2.
Mean-field and Semiclassical Scalings

Our goal in this paper is to study simultaneously the mean-field limit, corresponding to the approximations made when the number of particles
N
is large and each pair interaction is weak, and the semiclassical limit, corresponding to a change of scales where the Planck constant
h
becomes negligible. Let us elaborate more on the two scalings.

To understand of the dynamics generated by the many-body Schrödinger equation (
1
) at different scales, it is convenient to recast the equation in its dimensionless form. Suppose
L
is some characteristic length of the problem and
T
is some characteristic timescale, then we define the following dimensionless variables

\widetilde{x}:=x/L\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{T}:=t/T.

We also recast the interaction potential in its dimensionless form via the change of scale

\widetilde{K}(\widetilde{x}):=\frac{N\,T^{2}}{m\,L^{2}}K(x)=\frac{N\,T^{2}}{m\,L^{2}}K(L\,\widetilde{x})

where
m
denotes the mass which we have set to one. If we define the rescaled dimensionless parameter

\widetilde{\hbar}=\frac{\hbar\,T}{m\,L^{2}}

and the new rescaled wave function

\widetilde{\psi}_{N}(\widetilde{t},\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{N}):=L^{dN/2}\,\psi_{N}(t,x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}),

then multiplying Equation (
1
) by
\frac{T^{2}}{m\,L^{2}}
yields the dimensionless equation

i\,\widetilde{\hbar}\,\partial_{\,\widetilde{t}\,}\widetilde{\psi}_{N}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}-\frac{\widetilde{\hbar}^{2}}{2}\,\Delta_{\widetilde{x}_{k}}\widetilde{\psi}_{N}+\frac{1}{N}\,\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq N}\widetilde{K}(\widetilde{x}_{k}-\widetilde{x}_{l})\,\widetilde{\psi}_{N}.

Moreover, in the case of an homogeneous interaction of the form
K(x)=\kappa\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{-a}
for some parameter
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, this gives
\widetilde{K}(\widetilde{x})=\widetilde{\kappa}\,\left\lvert\widetilde{x}\right\rvert^{-a}
where
\widetilde{\kappa}=\kappa\,N\,T^{2}/(m\,L^{2+a})
. From here, we consider the case of space-time scales where
\widetilde{\kappa}
is of order 1 and we simply set
\widetilde{\kappa}=1
. This provides a
N^{-1}
prefactor in front of the interaction potential which is usually referred to as the mean-field scaling. In this class of scales, the dimensionless parameter
\widetilde{\hbar}
is of the order
L^{a}/(\kappa NT)
.
Furthermore, we shall refer to the scale where
\widetilde{\hbar}
becomes negligible as the semiclassical regime. For convenience, let us express
L
and
T
in terms of the parameters
N,\kappa
and
\widetilde{\hbar}
as follows

(7)

L=\,\left(\frac{\hbar^{2}}{m\,\kappa\,N\,\widetilde{\hbar}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2-a}},\quad\quad\quad T=\,\frac{\widetilde{\hbar}\,m}{\hbar}\left(\frac{\hbar^{2}}{m\,\kappa\,N\,\widetilde{\hbar}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{2}{2-a}}.

From here, we impose the condition that
N\,\widetilde{\hbar}^{2}\ll\kappa^{-1}
to guarantee that
L\gg 1
. In particular, we could set
\kappa=N^{-1}
.

While
\hbar
and
N
can a priori be considered as independent parameters, certain constraints arise when dealing with fermions. In Section
1.4
, it is explained that the Pauli principle imposes a limitation on
N\,h^{3}
, which must remain bounded to ensure convergence of the one-particle density operator to a nonzero function on the phase space. This is in contrast to bosonic systems, which are system of particles that have permutation symmetry as opposed the anti-permutation symmetry of fermions, where the Pauli principle does not apply.

Lower densities of

bosons or fermions

Higher densities of bosons
Maximal density of fermions

Newton
Vlasov

\left(h=1\right)

\left(N=\infty\right)

\left(h=0\right)

Figure 1.
The different scalings for the combined mean-field and semiclassical limits. The dashed (red) curve corresponds to the equation
h=N^{-1/2}
and the continuous (blue) curve to
h=N^{-1/3}
.

In addition, observe that the particle density, defined as the number of particles per unit of characteristic volume, scales as
N\,L^{-3}
, where
N
is the total number of particles. By using the scaling given by Formula (
7
), we can express the density as follows

\frac{N}{L^{3}}\simeq N^{\frac{5-a}{2-a}}\,h^{\frac{6}{2-a}}\,\kappa^{\frac{3}{2-a}}.

This explains why the region of Figure
1
closer to the Hartree–Fock equation corner corresponds to relatively higher densities, while the region below corresponds to relatively lower densities. Moreover, we should note that in this work, we consider
h
to satisfy the constraint
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll h\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{3}}
, which corresponds to the dark shaded region in the figure. It should be noted that the constraint
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll h
, meaning
N\,h^{2}\to\infty
, could be technical and it arises in the proof of the main result (See Proposition
10.1
).

With a little abuse of notation and language, we shall drop the tildes and study the equation

(8)

\displaystyle i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\psi_{N}

\displaystyle=H_{N}\,\psi_{N},

\displaystyle H_{N}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta_{x_{k}}+\frac{1}{N}\,\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq N}K(x_{k}-x_{l})

where
N
is large and
\hbar
is small, and with

(9)

K(x)=\frac{\kappa}{\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{a}},

where
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
is of order
1
and
a\in(0,1]
.

More precisely, we study the time evolution of
N
-body fermionic mixed states, which are self-adjoint, positive trace class operators of rank larger than one. By the spectral theorem, they can be expressed in the following way

(10)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\ket{\psi_{j}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{j}}\quad\text{ with }\quad\lambda_{j}\geq 0

where
\set{\psi_{j}}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathfrak{h}^{\otimes N}
is an orthonormal set of anti-symmetric wave functions. The operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
is called a pure state provided it is a rank one projection, that is,
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\ket{\psi_{N}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{N}}
. The time evolution equation for density operators is given by the Liouville–von Neumann equation

(11)

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\left[{H_{N},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}}\right]

where the Hamiltonian
H_{N}
is given in Equation (
8
), which is the quantum analogue of the classical Liouville equation, equivalent to the
N
-body Newton laws.

1.3.
State of the Art

Both the problems of the mean-field limits and the semiclassical limits are well-known questions that are largely addressed in the literature. However, the derivation of the Vlasov–Poisson equation, i.e. the case of the Coulomb and gravitational potentials, remains an open problem, both in the case of quantum mechanics and in the case of classical Newton’s laws.

1.3.1.
The Classical Mean-Field Limit

In the context of classical mechanics, the problem of justifying the Vlasov equation (
2
) starting from the dynamics of
N
-particles obeying Newton’s laws was first proven for twice differentiable potentials in the pioneering works by Neunzert and Wick
[
58
]
, Braun and Hepp
[
19
]
, and then by Dobrushin
[
27
]
using the Wasserstein–Monge–Kantorovich distance (see also
[
74
]
for an introduction to the topic). The class of potentials was then extended to less regular potentials but still locally Hölder continuous by Hauray and Jabin
[
42
,
43
]
, which was later improved by Jabin and Wang using entropy methods in
[
45
]
, where the potential is only required to be bounded.

From another point of view, it was also proved in
[
43
,
17
]
that it is possible to obtain the mean-field limit for potentials with a vanishing cutoff, converging to potentials almost as singular as the Coulomb potential when
N\to\infty
. This is in particular interesting from a numerical point of view. These results were then improved by Lazarovici
[
50
]
, allowing the cutoff potential to converge to the Coulomb potential, and by Lazarovici and Pickl
[
51
]
, with a
N
-dependent cutoff of the order of the inter-particle distance.

1.3.2.
Combined Mean-Field and Semiclassical Limits

The first rigorous derivation of the Vlasov equation (
2
) from the
N
-body Schrödinger equation (
1
) was proved by Narnhofer and Sewel
[
57
]
in the case of smooth potentials, with
\hbar=N^{-1/3}
. Subsequently, the restriction on the potential was substantially relaxed by Spohn
[
73
]
to twice differentiable potentials. For the same kind of potentials, a more explicit rate of convergence without assuming
\hbar=N^{-1/3}
was later obtained by Graffi, Martinez, and Pulvirenti
[
39
]
in the case of weak convergence, and more recently by Golse and Paul
[
36
]
in the quantum Wasserstein metrics, and by Chen, Lee and Liew for fermions
[
22
]
in the scaling
\hbar=N^{-1/3}
.

1.3.3.
Quantum Mean-Field Limit

It is also possible to first look at the mean-field limit with
\hbar=1
, i.e. without taking the semiclassical limit, leading to the Hartree and the Hartree–Fock equations. In this case, the situation is better understood, even for the Coulomb and gravitational potentials. For bosons, weak convergence was proved in
[
10
,
30
,
8
]
, and explicit rates in stronger norms were obtained in
[
66
,
40
,
62
,
24
,
46
,
56
,
23
,
59
]
. For fermions, weak convergence was proved in
[
9
]
for bounded potentials, and estimates in trace norm and singular potentials such as the Coulomb potential were obtained in
[
34
,
5
,
61
,
60
]
.

Some of these results have been extended by taking into account the semiclassical parameter
\hbar
. For fermions, taking
\hbar=N^{-1/3}
, convergence of the Husimi transform has been proven in
[
29
]
for analytic interactions and short times. Schatten norms estimates have been obtained in
[
13
,
11
,
61
]
for at least twice differentiable potentials. Assuming a certain semiclassical structure on the solution of the Hartree equation, a result was obtained in the case of pure states and singular potentials in
[
63
,
67
]
.

For bosons, results were obtained for at least twice differentiable potentials in
[
35
,
38
,
37
]
.

1.3.4.
Semiclassical Limit

Another possible direction is to look only at the semiclassical limit
\hbar\to 0
, either for the number of particles
N
fixed or in the mean-field regime. This last case corresponds to going from the Hartree or the Hartree–Fock equation to the Vlasov equation. In the case of the Hartree equation, this was proved in
[
54
,
55
]
in weak topology, but including singular potentials such as the Coulomb interaction (see also
[
32
]
for the case of quantum Liouville dynamics). Explicit rates in stronger norms were then obtained in
[
4
,
1
,
12
,
36
]
for at least twice differentiable potentials, and then in
[
47
,
68
,
69
,
48
,
49
]
for singular interactions.

To our knowledge our work is the first one addressing mixed states (see (
10
)) in the case of singular interactions of the form (
9
) and proving in this context the approximation of the mean-field dynamics with the Hartree–Fock equation on time scales of order one when
a\in(0,1/2)
and to time scales of order
\sqrt{\hbar}
when
a\in[1/2,1]
. See also Remark
3.6
.

1.4.
Constraints on the Scalings

The Fourier transform is defined by

(12)

\widehat{g}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-2\pi i\,x\cdot\xi}\,g(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

for
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. We also adopt the following conventions for the time-dependent operator solution
\boldsymbol{\rho}=\boldsymbol{\rho}(t)
to the Hartree–Fock equation (
5
)

(13)

\begin{split}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\infty}&=\mathcal{C}_{\infty},\vskip 3.0pt\\
\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)&=h^{-3}.\end{split}

for some constant
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
. In particular,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=h^{3}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)=1
. For such an operator, we define its Wigner transform by

f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x,\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-i\,y\cdot\xi/\hbar}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}(x+\tfrac{y}{2},x-\tfrac{y}{2})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

so that it is a function of the phase space with mass
\iint f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi=h^{3}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)=1
.
It is well known that, under some regularity assumptions, the Wigner transform of solutions
\boldsymbol{\rho}
to the Hartree–Fock equation (
5
) converge to solutions of the Vlasov equation (
2
) in the semiclassical limit
h\to 0
(see e.g.
[
54
]
). We refer to
[
54
]
for a listing of the properties of the Wigner transform. One of them is the fact that

(14)

\left\lVert f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}=h^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{2},

where we denote by

(15)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{p}=\left(\tr\!\left(\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rvert^{p}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

the Schatten norm of order
p
. Here, the absolute value of an operator
A
is defined by
\left\lvert A\right\rvert=\sqrt{A^{*}A}
. Since we want to address the case when
f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})
to a solution
f
of the Vlasov equation, this implies that
h^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{2}\underset{h\to 0}{\rightarrow}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}
, so that
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{2}
is of size
h^{-3/2}
.

For an
N
-particle density operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
, we look at its corresponding one-particle reduced density operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}
defined as the partial trace of
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
with respect to the variables
2
to
N
, that is,

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}=\tr_{2,\ldots,N}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}).

Since we also want the corresponding Wigner transform
f_{N:1}
of the operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}
of the
N
-particle density operator to converge to
f
, we have as well

(16)

\left\lVert f_{N:1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}=h^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}\right\rVert_{2}{\longrightarrow}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}\quad\mbox{as }N\to\infty\ \mbox{and }h\to 0.

However, in the case of fermions, we also know that (see for instance
[
53
, Equation 12.5.12]
, or
[
72
, Theorem 8.4]
)

(17)

0\leq\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}\leq\frac{\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}\right)}{N}=\frac{h^{-3}}{N}.

Therefore, by bounding the square of the Hilbert–Schmidt norm by the product of the trace norm and the operator norm, we deduce that

(18)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}\right\rVert_{1}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{h^{-6}}{N}.

Combining Inequality (
18
) and Formula (
14
) with
\boldsymbol{\rho}=\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}
, we obtain the bound

(19)

h\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-2/3}\,N^{-1/3},

where
\mathcal{C}_{2}=\left\lVert f_{N:1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}
converges to
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}
, which remains of order
1
. Hence, we are mainly interested in the case when
N\,h^{3}
is bounded above by a constant independent of
N
and
h
. In particular, the case when
N\,h^{3}
is of order one is called the critical scaling regime. This corresponds to the blue line in Figure
1
.

Notice that our analysis still makes sense if
N\,h^{3}\to\infty
. However, in this situation, even though the solution to the
N
-body Schrödinger equation and the solution to the Hartree–Fock equation are close, they will not converge in the semiclassical limit to a nontrivial solution of the Vlasov equation, but to zero.

2.
Functional Spaces

2.1.
Semiclassical Spaces

Since we want to look at the convergence in the semiclassical limit
\hbar\to 0
towards probability distributions of the phase space, we define the semiclassical versions of the Lebesgue norms of the phase space as the following scaled Schatten norms

(20)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}=h^{\frac{3}{p}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{p}=h^{\frac{3}{p}}\tr\!\left(\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rvert^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

More generally, given any positive operator
m
, we define the corresponding weighted spaces by the norm
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}(m)}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}
. With this choice of scaling of the norm, notice that for any operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}\geq 0
satisfying the scaling assumptions (
13
), one obtains

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}}=1,\qquad\quad\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}=\left\lVert f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})},\qquad\quad\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}=\mathcal{C}_{\infty}.

One useful property of the norm defined by (
20
) is that it is compatible with taking powers of the operator, in the sense that for any
c>0
,
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}^{c}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{pc}}^{c}
. In particular, in the rest of the paper we will often work with the operator
\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, which satisfies, as one would expect,
\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}=1
and
\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}=\sqrt{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}
.

The fact that these norms are good analogues of the classical Lebesgue norms can be better understood in light of particular examples. One class of examples is when the density operator has the form
f(x)\,g(\boldsymbol{p})
, where
\boldsymbol{p}=-i\hbar\nabla
is the momentum operator. Then the Kato–Seiler–Simon inequality
[
71
, Theorem 4.1]
reads

(21)

\left\lVert f(x)\,g(\boldsymbol{p})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\ \text{ if }p\in[2,\infty),

with equality when
p=2
, and where
L^{p}=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. It is the analogue of the identity
\left\lVert f(x)\,g(\xi)\right\rVert_{L^{p}_{x,\xi}}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{p}}
. Another class of examples is the class of Toeplitz operators, namely when
\boldsymbol{\rho}
is an averaging of coherent states, as presented in Remark
3.2
.

We also want to consider the semiclassical version of Sobolev spaces of the phase space. Thus, as in
[
49
]
, we introduce the following operators

(22)

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}:=\left[{\nabla,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}:=\left[{\frac{x}{i\hbar},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right],

which can be seen as an application of the correspondence principle of quantum mechanics. More precisely, one can observe that these operators correspond to the gradients of the Wigner transform, since

f_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\nabla_{x}f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\quad\text{ and }\quad f_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\nabla_{\xi}f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}.

In the rest of the paper, we shall refer to
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}
as the first-order quantum gradients or, simply, the quantum gradients.

We define the semiclassical analogues of the weighted kinetic homogeneous Sobolev norms by

\displaystyle\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{W}}^{1,p}(m_{n})}^{p}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\mathrm{j}=1}^{3}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}(m_{n})}^{p}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}(m_{n})}^{p},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{W}}^{1,\infty}(m_{n})}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathrm{j}\in\{1,2,3\}}\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{n})},\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{n})}\right),

and consider the particular case of the weight defined for
n\in\mathbb{N}
by

(23)

m_{n}:=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n}.

where
\boldsymbol{p}=-i\hbar\nabla
so
\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2}=-\hbar^{2}\Delta
. We also define the inhomogeneous version by

(24)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{W}^{1,p}(m_{n})}^{p}:=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}(m_{n})}^{p}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{W}}^{1,p}(m_{n})}^{p},

with the usual modification when
p=\infty
. In particular, for
p=2
, we have that
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{W}^{1,2}}=\left\lVert f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}
.

2.2.
Fermionic Fock Space

Let
\mathfrak{h}^{\wedge N}:=\mathfrak{h}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathfrak{h}
denote the
n
-fold anti-symmetric tensor product of
\mathfrak{h}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C})
. We define the fermionic (anti-symmetric) Fock space over
\mathfrak{h}
to be the closure of

(25)

\cF(\mathfrak{h})=\cF:=\mathbb{C}\oplus\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{h}^{\wedge n}

with respect to the norm induced by the endowed inner product

(26)

\Braket{\psi|\varphi}_{\cF}=\overline{\psi^{(0)}}\,\varphi^{(0)}+\sum_{n\geq 1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3n}}\overline{\psi^{(n)}(\underline{x}_{n})}\,\varphi^{(n)}(\underline{x}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{n},

for any pair of vectors
\psi=(\psi^{(0)},\psi^{(1)},\ldots)
and
\varphi=(\varphi^{(0)},\varphi^{(1)},\ldots)
in
\cF
where
\underline{x}_{k}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{3k}
. For simplicity of notation, we will also denote the closure by
\cF
. The vacuum, defined by the vector

\Omega_{\cF}=(1,0,\ldots)\in\cF,

describes the state with no particles.
We define the number of particles operator by

(27)

\mathcal{N}\psi=\left(n\,\psi^{(n)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

whose meaning can be interpreted as counting the number of particles in each sector of
\cF
. A class of operators on
\cF
that is important to our studies is the class of mixed states on
\cF
, which are high rank density matrices on
\cF
. More specifically, we are interested in operators of the form

(28)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}:=\sum_{\mathrm{j}\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{\mathrm{j}}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{j}}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{\mathrm{j}}},

for some orthonormal set
\psi_{\mathrm{j}}
of vectors of
\cF
with the normalization

(29)

\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right)=\sum_{\mathrm{j}}\lambda_{\mathrm{j}}=h^{-3}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ h^{3}\tr\!\left(\mathcal{N}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right)=N.

Here,
N
is the mean number of particles. Moreover, for each
(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we define the operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{(n,m)}
as the operator with the integral kernel

(30)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m})=\sum_{\mathrm{j}\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{\mathrm{j}}\,\psi_{\mathrm{j}}^{(n)}(\underline{x}_{n})\,\overline{\psi_{\mathrm{j}}^{(m)}(\underline{y}_{m})}.

As in the case of the one-particle operator given in Equation (
20
), we define the Fock space semiclassical Schatten norms by

(31)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}(\cF)}:=h^{\frac{3}{p}}\tr_{\cF}\left(\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right\rvert^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

so that
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)}=1
and
\left\lVert\mathcal{N}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)}=N
. We also define the one-particle reduced density matrix, i.e. the analogue of the classical one-particle marginal, by

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{n}{N}\,\tr_{2..n}\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{(n,n)}\right),

where
\tr_{2..n}
indicates the partial trace with respect to all variables except the first.

3.
Main Results

3.1.
Propagation of Regularity

Our first result gives the local-in-time and uniform in
\hbar
propagation of the regularity of the solution to the Hartree–Fock equation (
5
). Let us notice that there are no constraints on the scaling here since we are only considering the mean-field equation. Moreover, this result also holds uniformly in
\hbar
in the case of the Coulomb potential.

Recall that we work exclusively with the singular interaction potential
K(x)=\kappa\,|\cdot|^{-a}
for
0<a\leq 1
. We define the parameter

(32)

\mathfrak{b}:=\frac{3}{a+1}

which corresponds to the integrability of the force field since
\nabla K\in L^{\mathfrak{b},\infty}
.

Theorem 3.1

(Propagation of regularity)
.

Let
a\in(0,1]
,
m_{n}=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n}
with
n\in 2\mathbb{N}
satisfying
n\geq 6
and
\boldsymbol{\rho}
be a solution to the Hartree–Fock equation (
5
) with initial condition
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{n})
satisfying (
13
) and such that

(33)

\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{1,4}(m_{n-2}).

Then there exists
T>0
such that

(34)

\boldsymbol{\rho}\in L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{1,4}(m_{n-2})),

uniformly in
\hbar\in(0,1)
.

Remark 3.1
.

In the case when
a\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
, we further extend the local-in-time and uniform-in-
\hbar
propagation of regularity result of Theorem
3.1
to a global-in-time result in
[
25
]
.

Remark 3.2

(On the initial data of the Hartree equation)
.

Define
\varphi(x)=e^{-\pi\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}/2}
and
\varphi_{x,\xi}(y):=\frac{1}{h^{9/4}}\varphi\!\left(\frac{y-x}{\sqrt{h}}\right)e^{i\,y\cdot\xi/\hbar}
. Then one can define an approximation of the Dirac delta on the phase space by
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{x,\xi}:=\ket{\varphi_{x,\xi}}\!\!\bra{\varphi_{x,\xi}}
. Now for any
g:\mathbb{R}^{6}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
g\in W^{1,\infty}(1+\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{n})\cap W^{1,2}(1+\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{n})\cap L^{2}
, one can define the averaging of coherent states, also called Toeplitz operator (see e.g.
[
35
,
36
]
) or Wick quantization (see e.g.
[
52
]
), as the operator

\,\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{g}:=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}g(x,\xi)\,\boldsymbol{\rho}_{x,\xi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

This defines a positive compact operator such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\,\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{g}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{6})},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\,\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{g}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})},

and more generally, as proved for example in
[
54
]
, such that for any convex function
\Phi
such that
\Phi(0)=0
, it holds

h^{3}\tr\!\left(\Phi\!\left(\,\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{g}\right)\right)\leq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\Phi(g)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

In particular, in Theorem
3.1
, we can take
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}=\,\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{g}
with
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}=1
and
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}=\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{1/2}
, and then
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}
satisfies the assumptions (
13
).

However, we can consider more general operators than simply the averaging of coherent states. Given a function
g
on the phase space, one can perform the inverse of the Wigner transform, called the Weyl quantization, to define the operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{g}
as the operator with integral kernel

(35)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{g}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-2i\pi\left(y-x\right)\cdot\xi}\,g(\tfrac{x+y}{2},h\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

This operator satisfies the hypotheses of the initial condition of Theorem
3.1
if
g
is sufficiently smooth and decays at infinity, as proved for example in
[
49
, Section 3]
.

3.2.
Mean-Field and Semiclassical Limits

To state our mean-field results, we assume there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
N
and
\hbar
such that

(36)

N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll h\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{3}},

where
a\ll b
means that
\frac{a}{b}\to 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. We also assume that the constant
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
defined in Formula (
13
) is independent of
N
and
\hbar
, and satisfies

(37)

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}<(N\,h^{3})^{-1}.

We define the following trace class norm over the Fock space weighted by the number operator

(38)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}_{k}(\cF)}:=\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)^{k}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)}.

In what follows, for technical reasons related to the well-posedness of the auxiliary dynamics given in Appendix
A
, we will assume that the initial quantum spatial distribution of particles (
4
) satisfies

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho^{\mathrm{in}}(x)\left(1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert\right)^{3}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq C,

where
C
may depend on
h
.

Theorem 3.2

(Mean-field limit)
.

Let
a\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and assume conditions (
36
) and (
37
) are satisfied. Let
n\in 2\mathbb{N}
satisfying
n\geq 6
. Let
\boldsymbol{\rho}
be a solution to the Hartree–Fock equation (
5
) with initial condition
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{n})
satisfying (
13
) and such that

(39a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{W}^{2,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{2,4}(m_{n-2})

(39b)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{1,q}(m_{n-2})

with
q\in[\tfrac{6}{1-2a},\infty]
. Then, there exist
T>0
,
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)
,
\lambda>0
and
C>0
such that for any
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
solution of the second quantized version of (
11
) (see Equation (
51
) below) with initial condition
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)
commuting with
\mathcal{N}
, for any
t\in[0,T]
and
p\in[1,\infty]
,

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C\,e^{\lambda\,t}}{\min(N^{1/2},N\,h^{3/p^{\prime}})}\left(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}-\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\mathrm{in}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}_{k}(\cF)}\right)

for any
k\geq\frac{1}{2\,p}+\frac{3}{2}\lceil\frac{\ln N}{p\ln\left(Nh^{2}\right)}\rceil
where
p^{\prime}=p/(p-1)
is the Hölder conjugate of
p
.

Remark 3.3
.

The
N
-body operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\mathrm{in}}
is explicitly created from
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}
via the Bogoliubov transformation (see Equation (
77
) in Section
4.3
). In particular, one should note that
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\mathrm{in}}
is constructed so that its one-particle reduced density matrix coincides with the initial data
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}
of the Hartree–Fock equation.

Remark 3.4
.

In the particular case when
h=N^{-\frac{1}{3}}
,
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}-\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\mathrm{in}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)}\leq CN^{-4}
, and
\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{4}\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)}\leq C
, then for any
t\in[0,T]
, one obtains

\left\lVert f_{N:1}-f_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq\frac{C_{T}}{N^{1/2}}.

where
f_{N:1}
denotes the Wigner transform of
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}
.

One can combine the above theorem with the result proved in
[
49
]
by two of the authors to obtain an estimate directly between the solution of the
N
-body Schrödinger equation (
1
) and the Vlasov equation (
2
). To simplify, we restrict our attention to the case when
p\leq 2
.

Theorem 3.3

(Combined mean-field and semiclassical limits)
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
3.2
and that
f
is a positive solution of the Vlasov equation (
2
) with initial condition satisfying

\left(1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{8}+\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{8}\right)\nabla_{x}^{\ell_{0}}\nabla_{\xi}^{\ell}f^{\mathrm{in}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{6})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})\ \text{ where }\ \ell_{0}+\ell\leq 9.

Moreover, assume
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)
is such that
[\mathcal{N},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}]=0
. Then, for any
p\in[1,2]
, there exists
T>0,C_{T}>0
and an operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,f}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)
such that for any solution
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
to the second quantized version of (
11
) (see Equation (
51
) below) with initial condition
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}
, the estimate

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}_{f}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq C_{T}\left(\frac{1}{N\,h^{3/p^{\prime}}}+h\right)\left(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}-\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,f}^{\mathrm{in}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}_{k}(\cF)}\right),

holds for any
t\in[0,T]
and any
k\geq\frac{1}{2\,p}+\frac{3}{2}\lceil\frac{\ln N}{p\ln\left(Nh^{2}\right)}\rceil
.

Remark 3.5
.

In particular, when
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}-\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,f}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}_{k}(\cF)}\leq C
and
p=2
, then, by Identity (
14
), we obtain again a
L^{2}
convergence result with the quantitative bound

\left\lVert f_{N:1}-f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{6})}\leq C_{T}\left(\frac{1}{N\,h^{3/2}}+h\right),

where
f_{N:1}
is the Wigner transform of
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}
.

In our result, it is interesting to notice that the semiclassical error
h
is larger than the mean-field error when
N\gg h^{-5/2}
, and smaller when
N\ll h^{-5/2}
. When the two are of the same order, one obtains an error of order
h=N^{-2/5}
, which is the best of the rates in term of the number of particles, while the rate is of order
h=N^{-1/3}
in the critical scaling. However, we do not claim our results yield the optimal rates.

Remark 3.6
.

In the particular case of the Coulomb potential, we can still obtain an estimate for small times or with a
N
dependent cutoff (see Theorem
4.1
and Remark
4.2
). Our results are summarized in the following table.

Time of Validity

a\in(0,1/2)

a\in[1/2,1]

Semiclassical Regularity

t<T

t<T

Mean-field

t<T

t\ll h^{a-1/2}
or cutoff

Mean-field + semiclassical

t<T

t\ll h^{a-1/2}
or cutoff

4.
The Strategy and the General Result

4.1.
Second Quantization

The method of second quantization provides a mathematical framework for studying the notion of quantum fluctuations. The goal of this section is to recast the original Cauchy problem (
11
) with a mixed state initial data on
\mathfrak{h}^{\wedge N}
to a problem on the Fock space
\cF
. We briefly present the method of second quantization and state the corresponding Hamiltonian evolution problem on
\cF
. We refer the interested reader to
[
15
,
65
,
33
,
26
,
6
]
for a more complete presentation.

For every
f\in\mathfrak{h}
, we define the associated creation operator
a^{\ast}(f)
and its adjoint the annihilation operator
a(f)
on
\cF
by their actions on the
n
-sector of
\cF
as follows

\displaystyle(a^{*}(f)\,\psi)^{(n)}(\underline{x}_{n})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}(-1)^{j-1}f(x_{j})\,\psi^{(n-1)}(\underline{x}_{n\backslash\,j})

\displaystyle(a(f)\,\psi)^{(n)}(\underline{x}_{n})

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{n+1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\overline{f(x)}\,\psi^{(n+1)}(x,\underline{x}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,

where
\underline{x}_{n\backslash\,j}:=(x_{1},\ldots,\cancel{x}_{j},\ldots,x_{n})
. Moreover, the action of the annihilation operator on the vacuum of
\cF
is defined to be
a(f)\,\Omega_{\cF}=0
. Then, we extend the operators linearly to the whole
\cF
. It can easily be checked that the collection of creation and annihilation operators on
\cF
satisfies the canonical anti-commutation relations (CAR)

(40)

\left[{a(f),a^{*}(g)}\right]_{+}=\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}},\quad\left[{a(f),a(g)}\right]_{+}=\left[{a^{*}(f),a^{*}(g)}\right]_{+}=0

for all
f,g\in\mathfrak{h}
where
\left[{A,B}\right]_{+}=AB+BA
is the anti-commutator of the operators
A,B
. Moreover, from relation (
40
), we have the identity

(41)

\left\lVert a(f)\psi\right\rVert_{\cF}^{2}+\left\lVert a^{*}(f)\psi\right\rVert_{\cF}^{2}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathfrak{h}}^{2}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{\cF}^{2}\ \ \implies\ \ \left\lVert a^{\sharp}(f)\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathfrak{h}}

for all
f\in\mathfrak{h}
where
a^{\sharp}
is either
a^{*}
or
a
. Thus, both the creation and annihilation operators are bounded operators on
\cF
.

At times, it is more convenient to deal with creation and annihilation operators at a given position, say
x
, as opposed to
a^{*}(f)
and
a(f)
. Thus, it is useful to introduce, at least formally, the fermionic creation and annihilation operator-valued distributions at the position
x
, denoted respectively by
a^{*}_{x}
and
a_{x}
, as follows

(42a)

\displaystyle(a_{x}^{*}\psi)^{(n)}(\underline{x}_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(-1\right)^{j-1}\delta(x-x_{j})\,\psi^{(n-1)}(\underline{x}_{n\backslash j}),

(42b)

\displaystyle(a_{x}\psi)^{(n)}(\underline{x}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n+1}\,\psi^{(n+1)}(x,\underline{x}_{n}).

It is also straightforward to check that
a^{*}_{x}
and
a_{x}
satisfy the anti-commutation relations

(43)

\left[{a_{x},a_{y}^{*}}\right]_{+}=\delta(x-y),\qquad\left[{a_{x},a_{y}}\right]_{+}=\left[{a_{x}^{*},a_{y}^{*}}\right]_{+}=0,

and that the creation and annihilation operators can be rewritten as follows

(44)

a^{*}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(x)\,a_{x}^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\qquad a(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\overline{f(x)}\,a_{x}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

To every observable
O
on
\mathfrak{h}
corresponds an induced linear operator
\dG(O):\cF\rightarrow\cF
called the
second quantization
of
O
on
\cF
, defined as

(45)

\dG\!\left(O\right)=0\oplus\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\dG_{n}(O)\qquad

where
\dG_{n}(O)
is the
n
-particle operator

(46)

\dG_{n}(O)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}1_{\mathfrak{h}^{j-1}}\otimes O\otimes 1_{\mathfrak{h}^{n-j}}.

An important example of second quantized operator is the number operator which is simply the second quantization of the identity operator. Another relevant class of operators are the trace class operators. It is straightforward to check that the second quantization of trace class operators on
\mathfrak{h}
are also trace class operators on
\cF
.

If the observable
O
has the distributional kernel
O(x,y)
, then we could rewrite
\dG(O)
in terms of the operator-valued distributions
a^{*}_{x}
and
a_{x}
as follows

(47)

\dG(O)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}O(x,y)\,a_{x}^{*}\,a_{y}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

In particular, the number operator can be rewritten as

(48)

\mathcal{N}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}a_{x}^{*}\,a_{x}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

4.2.
State Purification and Time Evolution

We define the Fock space Hamiltonian by

(49)

\mathsf{H}_{N}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}a_{x}^{*}\left(-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta_{x}\right)a_{x}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K(x-y)\,a^{*}_{x}\,a^{*}_{y}\,a_{y}\,a_{x}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

By direct computation, we see that
\mathsf{H}_{N}
commutes with the number operator, which implies that the expectation of the number of particles is conserved under the Hamiltonian dynamics.
Moreover, its action on the
n
-sector is given for any
\psi\in\cF
by

(50)

(\mathsf{H}_{N}\psi)^{(n)}=\mathsf{H}_{N}^{(n)}\psi^{(n)}=\sum^{n}_{k=1}-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta_{x_{k}}\psi^{(n)}+\frac{1}{N}\sum^{n}_{k<l}K(x_{l}-x_{k})\,\psi^{(n)},

which, on the
N
-sector of
\cF
, coincides with the mean-field Hamiltonian defined in Equation (
8
). We consider the Cauchy problem

(51)

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\left[{\mathsf{H}_{N},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}}\right],\ \text{ with }\ \boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}(t=0)=\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}=\sum_{\mathrm{j}}\lambda_{\mathrm{j}}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{j}}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{j}}

where the data are defined as in (
28
). Following the idea of
[
11
]
, we reformulate Equation (
51
) as an evolution problem of a pure state
1
1

1

Here, we make the identification of
\ket{\Psi}\!\!\bra{\Psi}
with
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}
. In other words, pure state density matrices are simply vectors.
in the fermionic Fock space

(52)

\mathcal{G}:=\cF(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})

which hereinafter will be referred to as the double Fock space. This procedure is commonly known as purification of mixed states. For completeness, we devote the remainder of this section to review the state purification process.

For any operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
as defined in Equation (
28
) and any orthonormal basis
\phi_{\mathrm{j}}
of
\cF
, we construct the following Hilbert–Schmidt operator on
\cF

(53)

\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}:=\sum_{\mathrm{j}\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{j}}\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{j}}}\!\!\bra{\phi_{\mathrm{j}}},

where
\left\lvert\varepsilon_{\mathrm{j}}\right\rvert^{2}=\lambda_{\mathrm{j}}
. Then, we see that
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}\right\rvert^{2}
, which is called the Schmidt decomposition of
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
. In particular, its scaled Hilbert–Schmidt norm, defined by
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\cF)}^{2}=h^{3}\tr(\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\right\rvert^{2})
, is

(54)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\cF)}^{2}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)}=1.

It is important to observe that the decomposition is not unique. In fact, we will need to make a definite choice later.

Recall that the space of Hilbert–Schmidt operators
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\cF)
is isomorphic to the tensor product
\cF(\mathfrak{h})\otimes\cF(\mathfrak{h})
, as Hilbert spaces, via the linear mapping
\mathsf{J}_{h}=\mathsf{J}
that maps
\ket{\psi}\!\!\bra{\phi}\mapsto h^{-\frac{3}{2}}\,\overline{\phi}\otimes\psi
. One can then associate to
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}
an element of
\cF(\mathfrak{h})\otimes\cF(\mathfrak{h})
as follows

(55)

\mathsf{J}\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}=h^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sum_{\mathrm{j}\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{j}}\,\overline{\phi_{\mathrm{j}}}\otimes\psi_{\mathrm{j}}.

Furthermore, we can associate to every element (
55
) a vector in the double Fock space
\mathcal{G}
via the isomorphism
\mathsf{U}:\cF\otimes\cF\to\mathcal{G}
defined by the following: for
F\in\mathfrak{h}^{\wedge n}
and
G\in\mathfrak{h}^{\wedge m}

(56)

\mathsf{U}\left(F\otimes G\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\left(n+m\right)!}{n!\,m!}}\left(J_{l}^{\otimes n}F\right)\otimes_{a}\left(J_{r}^{\otimes m}G\right),

where
J_{l},J_{r}:\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
are respectively the canonical embedding of
\mathfrak{h}
into the
left
and the
right
coordinate of
\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
, and
\otimes_{a}
is the anti-symmetric tensor product. Then extend the mapping linearly to the entire
\cF\otimes\cF
. The unitary map
\mathsf{U}
is known as the exponential law for Fock spaces and it satisfies the following properties (see
[
26
, Theorem 3.43]
or
[
6
, Chapter 3]
)

(57a)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{U}(\Omega_{\cF}\otimes\Omega_{\cF}),

(57b)

\displaystyle a_{l}^{\sharp}(f):=a^{\sharp}(f\oplus 0)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{U}\left(a^{\sharp}(f)\otimes 1\right)\mathsf{U}^{*},

(57c)

\displaystyle a_{r}^{\sharp}(f):=a^{\sharp}(0\oplus f)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{U}\left((-1)^{\mathcal{N}}\otimes a^{\sharp}(f)\right)\mathsf{U}^{*},

with
a^{\sharp}
is either
a
or
a^{*}
and
f\in\mathfrak{h}
. The presence of the operator
(-1)^{\mathcal{N}}
ensures that the operators satisfy the CAR. It can also be readily checked that
a^{\sharp}_{l}(f)
anti-commutes with
a^{\sharp}_{r}(g)
for all
f,g\in\mathfrak{h}
.

Just like in the case of
\cF
, it is useful to define the left and right creation and annihilation operator-valued distributions at the position
x
by

\displaystyle(a_{x,l}^{*}\Psi)^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(-1\right)^{j-1}\delta(x-x_{j})\,\Psi^{(n-1,m)}(\underline{x}_{n\backslash j},\underline{y}_{m}),

\displaystyle(a_{x,l}\Psi)^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m})

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{n+1}\,\Psi^{(n+1,m)}(x,\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m}),

\displaystyle(a_{x,r}^{*}\Psi)^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\,\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(-1\right)^{n+j-1}\delta(x-y_{j})\,\Psi^{(n,m-1)}(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m\backslash j}),

\displaystyle(a_{x,r}\Psi)^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m})

\displaystyle:=\left(-1\right)^{n}\sqrt{m+1}\,\Psi^{(n,m+1)}(\underline{x}_{n},x,\underline{y}_{m}).

This allows us to express
a^{\sharp}_{\sigma}(f)
for
f\in\mathfrak{h}
in terms of operator-valued distributions, that is,

(58)

a_{\sigma}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\overline{f(x)}\,a_{x,\sigma}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\ \ \text{ and }\ \ a_{\sigma}^{*}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(x)\,a^{*}_{x,\sigma}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

where
\sigma\in\{l,r\}
. It is again straightforward to check the CAR relations:
\left[{a_{x,\sigma},a^{*}_{y,\sigma}}\right]_{+}=\delta(x-y)
and
[a^{\sharp}_{x,\sigma},a^{\sharp}_{y,\sigma^{\prime}}]_{+}=0
where
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{l,r\}
.

For every observable
O
on
\mathfrak{h}
, we can define the left or right induced linear operator
\dG_{l}\!\left(O\right),\dG_{r}\!\left(O\right):\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\dG_{l}\!\left(O\right)

\displaystyle:=\dG\!\left(O\oplus 0\right)=\mathsf{U}\left(\dG(O)\otimes 1\right)\mathsf{U}^{*}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}O(x,y)\,a^{*}_{x,l}\,a_{y,l}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

\displaystyle\dG_{r}\!\left(O\right)

\displaystyle:=\dG\!\left(0\oplus O\right)=\mathsf{U}\left(1\otimes\dG(O)\right)\mathsf{U}^{*}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}O(x,y)\,a^{*}_{x,r}\,a_{y,r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

The number operator on
\mathcal{G}
is defined by

(59)

\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{l}+\mathcal{N}_{r}=\mathsf{U}\left(\mathcal{N}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathcal{N}\right)\mathsf{U}^{*}.

We shall denote by

(60)

\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}:=\mathsf{U}\mathsf{J}

the transformation from
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\cF)
to
\mathcal{G}
mapping density operators to vectors of the double Fock space. Then for an operator
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\cF)
, the action of the operator
\mathcal{N}
in
\mathcal{G}
becomes
\mathcal{N}\,\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N})=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{N}\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}\mathcal{N})
.

With the above purification process, we can recast our Cauchy problem for mixed states to a Cauchy problem for pure states defined on the double Fock space
\mathcal{G}
. Recall the solution to the Cauchy problem (
51
) in the Schrödinger picture is given by

(61)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=e^{-i(t/\hbar)\,\mathsf{H}_{N}}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}\,e^{i(t/\hbar)\,\mathsf{H}_{N}}.

We define the time evolution of
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}
with initial state
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}
by

(62)

\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}=e^{-i(t/\hbar)\,\mathsf{H}_{N}}\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}\,e^{i(t/\hbar)\,\mathsf{H}_{N}}.

Then
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}\right\rvert^{2}
solves Equation (
11
) with initial data
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}=\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}}\right\rvert^{2}
. In the double Fock space
\mathcal{G}
, this corresponds to say that the evolution is given by
\Phi=\Phi(t)
with

(63)

\Phi:=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N})=e^{-i(t/\hbar)\,\mathsf{L}_{N}}\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}^{\mathrm{in}})=e^{-i(t/\hbar)\,\mathsf{L}_{N}}\Phi^{\mathrm{in}}

where the Liouvillian
\mathsf{L}_{N}
is defined by
\mathsf{L}_{N}=\mathsf{U}\left(\mathsf{H}_{N}\otimes 1-1\otimes\mathsf{H}_{N}\right)\mathsf{U}^{*}
. In particular, for any observable
\mathsf{O}
of
\cF
, we have the relation

(64)

\tr_{\cF}\left(\mathsf{O}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right)=\Braket{\Phi|\left(\mathsf{O}\otimes 1\right)\Phi}_{\mathcal{G}}=\tr_{\mathcal{G}}\left((\mathsf{O}\otimes 1)\ket{\Phi}\!\bra{\Phi}\right),

which allows us to compute the mean value of the observable
\mathsf{O}
with respect to the mixed state
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
in terms of the purified state
\Phi
. In particular, we could express the one-particle reduced density matrix of
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
in terms of
\Phi
, that is, the integral kernel of
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}
is given by

(65)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}(x,y)=\frac{1}{N\,h^{3}}\Braket{\Phi|a_{x,l}^{*}\,a_{y,l}\,\Phi}.

Notice that we are using the normalization
\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}\right)=h^{-3}
.

4.3.
Bogoliubov Transformation and Quasi-Free States

In general, we do not know if the evolution of the Cauchy problem (
51
) can be well-approximated by its mean-field dynamics. Therefore, it is natural to restrict our studies to a subclass of initial data. As stated in
[
11
]
, equilibrium states at finite positive temperature are believed to be well-approximated by mixed quasi-free states. In the particular case of non interacting fermions at positive temperature, equilibrium states are exactly described by mixed quasi-free states (see
[
18
]
). Furthermore, mixed quasi-free states have the important property that they can be represented by the action of a Bogoliubov transformation on the vacuum of the double Fock space
\mathcal{G}
, which is a key object in our study of the mean-field limit.

In this section, we give a brief overview of rudimentary facts about Bogoliubov transformation in the framework of the double Fock space
\mathcal{G}
and construct a class of quasi-free states exhibiting the structure of pure states in
\mathcal{G}
, with average number of particles
N
and pairing density equal to zero. We follow closely the presentation given in
[
72
]
.

4.3.1.
Bogoliubov Transformation

For the pairs
f=f_{1}\oplus f_{2},g=g_{1}\oplus g_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
, we define the corresponding field operators by

\displaystyle A(f,g)

\displaystyle:=a(f)+a^{*}(\overline{g})=a_{l}(f_{1})+a_{r}(f_{2})+a^{*}_{l}(\overline{g_{1}})+a^{*}_{r}(\overline{g_{2}})

\displaystyle A^{*}(f,g)

\displaystyle:=(A(f,g))^{*}=a_{l}(\overline{g_{1}})+a_{r}(\overline{g_{2}})+a^{*}_{l}(f_{1})+a^{*}_{r}(f_{2}).

Notice the field operator
A(f,g)
and its adjoint satisfy the relation

(66)

A^{*}(f,g)=A(C(f,g))

for all
f,g\in\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
where
C:(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\,\oplus\,(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\to(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\,\oplus\,(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})
is the anti-linear map defined by
C(f_{1}\oplus f_{2},g_{1}\oplus g_{2})=(\overline{g_{1}}\oplus\overline{g_{2}},\overline{f_{1}}\oplus\overline{f_{2}})
.
We can also readily check the collection of field operators satisfy the anti-commutation relations:

(67)

\left[{A(f,g),A^{*}(h,k)}\right]_{+}=\Braket{(f,g)|(h,k)}_{(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\oplus(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})},\quad\left[{A^{\sharp}(f,g),A^{\sharp}(h,k)}\right]_{+}=0

where
A^{\sharp}=A
or
A^{*}
and
f,g,h,k\in\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
.

A linear isomorphism

\nu:(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\,\oplus\,(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\,\rightarrow\,(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\,\oplus\,(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})
is called a Bogoliubov (canonical) transformation of
(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\,\oplus\,(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})
provided it preserves the anti-commutation relations (
67
), that is, we have that

(68)

\left[{A(\nu(f,g)),A^{*}(\nu(h,k))}\right]_{+}=\Braket{(f,g)|(h,k)}_{(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\oplus(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})}

for all
f,g,h,k\in\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
and, likewise, for the other relations. Hence, it follows from (
66
) and (
68
) that
\nu
is a Bogoliubov transformation provided it satisfies the conditions

(69)

\nu\,C=C\,\nu\,,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\nu^{*}\nu=\nu\nu^{*}=I\,,

where
I
is the corresponding identity map.

It is more convenient to express conditions (
69
) as follows:
\nu
is a Bogoliubov transformation on
(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\,\oplus\,(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})
if there exist operators
U
,
V:\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
satisfying the properties

(70)

U^{*}U+V^{*}V=I\qquad\text{ and }\qquad U^{*}\overline{V}+V^{*}\overline{U}=0

such that
\nu
has the form

(71)

\nu=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}U&\overline{V}\\
V&\overline{U}\end{array}\right).

Moreover, we say that the Bogoliubov transformation
\nu
is (unitarily) implementable on
\mathcal{G}
if there exists a unitary map
\mathsf{R}_{\nu}:\mathcal{G}\mapsto\mathcal{G}
such that

(72)

\mathsf{R}^{*}_{\nu}\,A(f,g)\,\mathsf{R}_{\nu}=A(\nu(f,g))

for all
f,g\in\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
. A necessary and sufficient condition for the transformation
\nu
to be implementable is given by Shale and Stinespring in
[
70
]
:
\nu
is implementable if and only if
V
is a Hilbert–Schmidt operator. In particular, if
\tr\!\left(V^{*}V\right)
is finite, then
\nu
is an implementable Bogoliubov transformation. It is common to refer to
\mathsf{R}_{\nu}
as the Bogoliubov transformation on
\mathcal{G}
.

4.3.2.
Quasi-Free States

A fermionic state
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
on
\cF
is said to be quasi-free provided it possesses the following factorization properties

(73a)

\displaystyle\tr_{\cF}\left(a^{\sharp_{1}}(f_{1})\cdots a^{\sharp_{2n+1}}\!\left(f_{2n+1}\right)\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right)=0,

(73b)

\displaystyle\tr_{\cF}\left(a^{\sharp_{1}}(f_{1})\cdots a^{\sharp_{2n}}\!\left(f_{2n}\right)\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\tr_{\cF}\left(a^{\sharp_{\sigma(2j-1)}}(f_{\sigma(2j-1)})a^{\sharp_{\sigma(2j)}}(f_{\sigma(2j)})\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\right),

where
f_{k}\in\mathfrak{h}
and the sum is over all permutations
\sigma
of
\set{1,\dots,2n}
satisfying

\forall j\in\set{1,\dots,n},\sigma(2j-1)<\sigma(2j),\text{ and }\sigma(2j-1)<\sigma(2j+1)\text{ if }j<n.

In short, a state is said to be quasi-free if the higher-order reduced density matrices of
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}
are completely determined by the generalized one-particle reduced density matrix. We could also express Conditions (
73
) in terms of the purified state
\Phi
. This means that any quasi-free mixed state can be viewed as the partial trace of a quasi-free pure state. Moreover, using the fact that pure quasi-free states are completely characterized by their generalized one-particle reduced density matrix, it can be shown that a pure quasi-free state
\Phi
on
\mathcal{G}
can be written as
\Phi=\mathsf{R}_{\nu}\Omega
for some Bogoliubov transformation
\mathsf{R}_{\nu}
.

Let us now construct the Bogoliubov transformation and its corresponding class of quasi-free states that we will study in Part
III
of the paper. Let
\omega
be a one-particle density operator on
\mathfrak{h}
satisfying the properties:
0\leq\omega\leq 1
and
\tr\!\left(\omega\right)=N
. Define the map

\nu:(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\oplus(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\mapsto(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})\oplus(\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h})

given by Equation (
71
) with
U
and
V
having the explicit forms

(74)

U=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}u&0\\
0&\overline{u}\end{array}\right)\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad V=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\overline{v}\\
-v&0\end{array}\right)

with

(75)

u:=\sqrt{1-\omega}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad v:=\sqrt{\omega}\,.

Notice
U
and
V
satisfy Equation (
70
) which means
\nu
is a Bogoliubov transformation. Furthermore,
V
is a Hilbert–Schmidt operator. Indeed, since
\tr\!\left(V^{*}V\right)=2\tr\!\left(\omega\right)=2\,N
is clearly finite, then, by the Shale–Stinespring condition
[
70
]
,
\nu
is implementable.
Hence, there exists a unitary map
\mathsf{R}_{\nu}:\mathcal{G}\mapsto\mathcal{G}
implementing
\nu
. Consequentially, Equation (
72
) yields the relations

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}^{*}_{\nu}a_{x,l}\mathsf{R}_{\nu}=

\displaystyle\ a_{l}(u_{x})-a^{*}_{r}(\overline{v_{x}}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}^{*}_{\nu}a_{x,r}\mathsf{R}_{\nu}=

\displaystyle\ a_{r}(\overline{u_{x}})+a^{*}_{l}(v_{x}).

where we used the notation
u_{x}(y)=u(y,x),v_{x}(y)=v(y,x)
.

Let us now use the Bogoliubov transformation to represent quasi-free mixed states. The construction we present here is an example of the well-known Araki–Wyss representation, see
[
3
,
2
,
26
]
. More precisely, we are interested in constructing a quasi-free mixed state with one-particle reduced density
\boldsymbol{\rho}
on the double Fock space
\mathcal{G}
. To this end, we define
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
as the Bogoliubov transform with

\omega=N\,h^{3}\boldsymbol{\rho}

and let the unitary map
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
act on the vacuum
\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}
, i.e.

(76)

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathcal{G}\,.

We can now compute the integral kernel of the one-particle reduced density matrix associated with
the state
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
:

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}(x,y)&=\frac{1}{N\,h^{3}}\Braket{\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}|a_{l,y}^{*}\,a_{l,x}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}=\frac{1}{N\,h^{3}}\Braket{\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}|\mathsf{R}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}a_{l,y}^{*}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathsf{R}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}a_{l,x}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}}\\
&=\frac{1}{N\,h^{3}}\Braket{\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}|a_{l}(\overline{v_{y}})\,a^{*}_{r}(\overline{v_{x}})\,\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}}=\frac{1}{N\,h^{3}}\,(v^{*}v)(x,y)=\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y).\end{split}

Therefore, the one-particle reduced density matrix associated with
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
corresponds to the operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}
. Furthermore, the off-diagonal term associated with the state
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, referred to as pairing density, is zero. Indeed, since

\begin{split}\alpha_{\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}(x,y)&:=\Braket{\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}|a_{l,y}\,a_{l,x}\,\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}}=\Braket{\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}|a_{l}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(\overline{v_{x}})\,\Omega_{\mathcal{G}}}=0\end{split}

where we used that
[a_{l}(u_{y}),a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})]_{+}=0
. Undoing the purification process, we can now define the reference state (mean-field approximation)
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, associated to the solution
\boldsymbol{\rho}
of the Hartree–Fock equation (
5
), as stated in Theorem
3.2
by

(77)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\left\lvert\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})\right\rvert^{2}.

4.4.
The General Result

In this section, we state a more general result from which our main results would follow. Our result is obtained by controlling the growth of the weighted norm

\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}:=\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
a\in[0,1]
and assume Condition (
36
) is satisfied. Let
(k,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and
\alpha\in\left[0,1\right]
satisfying
n\geq 6
and
\alpha>a-\frac{1}{2}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\rho}
be a solution of the Hartree–Fock equation (
5
) with initial condition
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{n})
satisfying (
13
) and such that

(78)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{W}^{2,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{2,4}(m_{n-2})

(79)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{1,q}(m_{n-2})

with
q\in[2,\infty]
satisfying

(80)

\frac{3}{q}\in\left[2\left(\alpha-a-\frac{1}{4}\right),\alpha-a+\frac{1}{2}\right].

Let
\Psi^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{G}
. Then, there exists
T>0
and
C>0
such that for any
t\in[0,T]
and any
p\in[1,\infty)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C\,e^{\lambda\,h^{-\alpha}\,t}}{\min(N^{\frac{1}{2}},N\,h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}})}\left(\left\lVert\Psi^{\mathrm{in}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k+\frac{1}{2\,p}}}^{2}+\frac{h^{2k\left(\alpha-1\right)}}{N^{k-\frac{1}{p}}}t^{2}\left\lVert\Psi^{\mathrm{in}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k}}^{2}\right).

where
\lambda=C_{a,\alpha}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert C_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
for some constant
C_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
depending only on
T
and the initial condition of the Hartree–Fock equation.

In the above theorem, we assumed we know the perturbation of the vacuum,
\Psi^{\mathrm{in}}
. As done in (
77
) for the reference state
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, we can associate to
\Psi^{\mathrm{in}}
an operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\left\lvert\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\Psi)\right\rvert^{2}
which solves the Schrödinger equation (
51
).

Remark 4.1
.

In particular, notice that

\frac{h^{2k\left(\alpha-1\right)}}{N^{k-\frac{1}{p}}}\leq 1\iff k\geq\frac{\ln N}{p\,\ln\left(Nh^{2\left(1-\alpha\right)}\right)}.

More specifically, if
N=h^{-c}
, then this is equivalent to
k\geq\frac{c}{p\left(c+2(\alpha-1)\right)}
. For instance, take
c=3
. Then for any
a<1/2
, we can take
\alpha=0
and
k=3
, leading to

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C\,e^{\lambda\,t}}{N^{\min(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{p})}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{5}}^{2}.

In the case of the Coulomb potential
a=1
, we can take
k=2
and any
\alpha>1/2
, leading to

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C}{N^{\min(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{p})}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{4}}^{2}e^{\lambda\,t/h^{\alpha}},

which is small only for small times
t\ll N^{-1/6}=h^{1/2}
. This is an improvement in comparison to non semiclassical estimates which are valid only for
t\ll h
.

Remark 4.2
.

When
a\geq 1/2
, one can also consider the potential with a
h
-dependent cut-off. For example, a way to get a potential bounded at distance
\left\lvert x\right\rvert\leq R
is to take

(81)

K_{R}(x)=\frac{\omega_{a}\,\kappa}{2}\int_{0}^{R^{-2}}s^{\frac{a}{2}-1}e^{-\pi\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\,\underset{R\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\,\kappa\,\frac{1}{\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{a}},

which is a radial decreasing potential satisfying
K_{R}(x)\leq\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert\max\!\left(\frac{1}{\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{a}},\frac{\omega_{a}}{aR^{a}}\right)
. For the Coulomb interaction potential for example, assuming
R\leq 1
and
N=h^{-c}
and taking
c=3
and
p\leq 2
, this leads to

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C\,e^{\lambda\,t/\sqrt{R}}}{N^{1/2}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{5}}^{2}.

Thus, one obtains a quantitative convergence result as soon as
R>\frac{4\lambda^{2}t^{2}}{\left(\ln N\right)^{2}}
.

Remark 4.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}
be defined by Equation (
77
). Then the standard deviation of the number of particles
\sigma_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}:=h^{3}\tr\!\left(\mathcal{N}^{2}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)-\left(h^{3}\tr\!\left(\mathcal{N}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right)^{2}
is given by

\sigma_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}=\tr\!\left(\omega-\omega^{2}\right)=N\left(1-\mathcal{C}_{2}^{2}Nh^{3}\right).

In particular,
\sigma_{\mathcal{N}}\leq\sqrt{N}
.

Notice also that
\sigma_{\mathcal{N}}=0\iff\omega=\omega^{2}\iff\mathcal{C}_{2}^{2}Nh^{3}=1
. This implies that in order for the reference state
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}
to have a fixed number of particles, it has to be a pure state and the scaling has to be the critical scaling
Nh^{3}=\mathcal{C}_{2}^{-2}
. In this case, the regularity conditions (
33
) are not expected to hold. However, it is a good question to know whether it is possible to find a state
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\left\lvert\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\Psi)\right\rvert^{2}
with a fixed number of particles but still close to
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, in the sense that the associated
\Psi
satisfies
\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{5}}\ll N^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Part II
Propagation of Regularity

This part is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.1
about the propagation of the semiclassical regularity of the solutions of the Hartree–Fock equation (
5
), and also of higher regularity properties needed to obtain Theorem
3.2
.

5.
The Classical Case: Regularity for the Vlasov Equation

As a warm-up and an explanation of our strategy, we start by the analogue of our method in the classical case of the kinetic Vlasov equation. We define

N_{p,x}:=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\nabla_{x}f\right\rvert^{p}m\quad\text{ and }\quad N_{p,\xi}:=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\nabla_{\xi}f\right\rvert^{p}m.

Denoting
\mathsf{T}:=\xi\cdot\nabla_{x}+E\cdot\nabla_{\xi}
, then we have

(82)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}(\nabla_{x}f)&=-\mathsf{T}\nabla_{x}f-\nabla E\cdot\nabla_{\xi}f\\
\partial_{t}(\nabla_{\xi}f)&=-\mathsf{T}\nabla_{\xi}f-\nabla_{x}f.\end{split}

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
n>3
and
f
be a solution of Equation (
2
) with initial condition satisfying

\nabla_{x,\xi}f^{\mathrm{in}}\in L^{p}(1+\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{n})

for any
p\in[1,\infty)
. Then there exists a time
T>0
such that

\nabla_{x,\xi}f\in L^{\infty}((0,T),L^{p}(1+\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{n})).

Proof.

Let
m:=1+\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{np}
. To simplify the computations, we observe that
\mathsf{T}^{*}=-\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{T}(uv)=u\,\mathsf{T}(v)+\mathsf{T}(u)\,v
, so that by writing
u^{p}:=\left\lvert u\right\rvert^{p-1}u
, it holds

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\mathsf{T}(u)\cdot u^{p-1}m=-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}u\cdot\mathsf{T}(u^{p-1})\,m+\left\lvert u\right\rvert^{p}\mathsf{T}(m).

But

\displaystyle u\cdot(\mathsf{T}(u^{p-1}))

\displaystyle=u^{p-1}\cdot\mathsf{T}(u)+(p-2)\left(\mathsf{T}(u)\cdot u\right)\left\lvert u\right\rvert^{p-2}

\displaystyle=\left(p-1\right)u^{p-1}\cdot\mathsf{T}(u).

We deduce

-p\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\mathsf{T}(u)\cdot u^{p-1}m=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert u\right\rvert^{p}\mathsf{T}(m).

Therefore, differentiating the weighted
L^{p}
norms, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}N_{p,x}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\nabla_{x}f\right\rvert^{p}\mathsf{T}(m)-p\left(\nabla_{x}f\right)^{p-1}\cdot\nabla E\cdot\nabla_{\xi}f\,m\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}N_{p,\xi}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\nabla_{\xi}f\right\rvert^{p}\mathsf{T}(m)-p\left(\nabla_{\xi}f\right)^{p-1}\cdot\nabla_{x}f\,m\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

Then by Young’s inequality for the product

\mathsf{T}(m)=n\,p\,E\cdot\xi^{np-1}\leq np\left\lVert E\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}m.

We cut
\nabla K=F_{1}+F_{2}\in L^{\mathfrak{b}_{1}}+L^{\mathfrak{b}_{2}}
. The difficult term is

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}(\nabla_{x}f)^{p-1}\cdot\nabla E\cdot\nabla_{\xi}f\,m\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\leq\left\lVert\nabla K*\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\nabla_{x}f\right\rvert^{p-1}\left\lvert\nabla_{\xi}f\right\rvert m\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\\
\leq\left(\left\lVert F_{1}\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}_{1}}}\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{{\mathfrak{b}_{1}^{\prime}}}}+\left\lVert F_{2}\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}_{2}}}\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{{\mathfrak{b}_{2}^{\prime}}}}\right)N_{p,x}^{1-1/p}N_{p,\xi}^{1/p}\\
\leq B_{K}\left(\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{{\mathfrak{b}_{1}^{\prime}}}}+\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{{\mathfrak{b}_{2}^{\prime}}}}\right)N_{p,x}^{1-1/p}N_{p,\xi}^{1/p},

with
B_{K}=\left\lVert\nabla K\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}_{1}}+L^{\mathfrak{b}_{2}}}
and where we used three times Hölder’s inequality. Then, when
n\geq 3/\mathfrak{b}
, we get

\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}=\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\nabla_{x}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\leq\left\lVert\nabla_{x}f\,(1+\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{n})\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}_{x,\xi}}\leq C\,N_{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},x}^{1/\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}.

Therefore, taking respectively
p=\mathfrak{b}_{1}^{\prime}
and
p=\mathfrak{b}_{2}^{\prime}
and using the notation
u_{k}=u_{k}(t):=N_{\mathfrak{b}_{k}^{\prime},x}^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}_{k}^{\prime}}}
and
v_{k}=v_{k}(t):=N_{\mathfrak{b}_{k}^{\prime},\xi}^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}_{k}^{\prime}}}
, for
k=1
and
k=2
, it holds

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}u_{k}

\displaystyle\leq n\left\lVert E\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}u_{k}+B_{K}\left(u_{1}+u_{2}\right)v_{k}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}v_{k}

\displaystyle\leq n\left\lVert E\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}v_{k}+u_{k}v_{k},

so that defining
U:=u_{1}+u_{2}+v_{1}+v_{2}
we get

(83)

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}U\leq n\left\lVert E\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}U+\left(B_{K}+\frac{1}{2}\right)U^{2},

where we used the fact
2uv\leq u^{2}+v^{2}
, and we obtain by Grönwall’s inequality that
U
remains finite as long as
t<T
where
T
depends on the growth of
\left\lVert E(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
, which we can control by

\left\lVert E\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{K}\left(\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{c_{1}^{\prime}}}+\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{c_{2}^{\prime}}}\right)\leq C_{K}\left(N_{c_{1}^{\prime},x}^{1/c_{1}^{\prime}}+N_{c_{2}^{\prime},x}^{1/c_{2}^{\prime}}\right),

with
C_{K}=\left\lVert K\right\rVert_{L^{c_{1}}+L^{c_{2}}}
. In particular, for the Coulomb interaction, one can choose
c_{1}=\mathfrak{b}_{1}<3/2
and
c_{2}=\mathfrak{b}_{2}>3
.
∎

6.
The Quantum Case: Propagation of Regularity for the Hartree–Fock Equation

In this section, we prove the semiclassical analogue of the propagation of regularity for the Vlasov equation shown in Section
5
. The main difficulty is to close the Grönwall inequality, which we managed to do by propagating at the same time the
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{n})
, the
\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(m_{n})
, and the
\mathcal{W}^{1,q}(m_{n-2})
norms with
q\geq 2
and

m_{n}=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n},

where
n\in 2\mathbb{N}
. This first step allows us to prove that the
\mathcal{W}^{1,q}(m_{n})
norm remains bounded on some time interval for
q\in[2,q_{a})
with
q_{a}:=\infty
if
\mathfrak{b}:=\frac{3}{a+1}\geq 2
and

\frac{1}{q_{a}}:=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}-\frac{1}{2}

when
\mathfrak{b}<2
. It is the content of the following proposition, where we only consider
q\in[2,4]
for simplicity.

Proposition 6.1
.

Fix
a\in(0,1]
. If
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{1,4}(m_{n-2})\cap\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{n})
, then there exists
T>0
such that

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\rho}}

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{1,4}(m_{n-2})\cap\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{n})),

\displaystyle{\rho}

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}((0,T),H^{1}\cap W^{1,4}\cap L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}).

Now that we know that the first-order semiclassical Sobolev norms remain bounded for some finite time
T>0
for
q\in[2,q_{a})
, we can use this first result and a similar strategy to prove the propagation of higher Sobolev norms on the same time scale. This is done in the following proposition.

Proposition 6.2
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
6.1
and assuming moreover that
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{W}^{2,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{2,4}(m_{n-2})
, then

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\rho}

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{W}^{2,2}(m_{n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{2,4}(m_{n-2})\cap\mathcal{W}^{1,\infty}(m_{n})),

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}((0,T),H^{2}\cap W^{2,4}).

Remark 6.1
.

The propagation of the second-order Sobolev norms will allow us to remove the constraint
q\in[2,q_{a})
and to get the boundedness of first-order Sobolev norms also for
q\geq q_{a}
. This is relevant when
a\geq\tfrac{1}{2}
.

In order to perform the mean-field limit, we actually need to prove the propagation of these norms for
\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
instead of
\boldsymbol{\rho}
(Cf. Equations (
162a
)-(
162b
)), which works in a similar way.

Proposition 6.3
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
6.2
, if
\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}}\in\mathcal{W}^{1,q}(m_{n})
for some
q\in[2,\infty]
, then

\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{W}^{1,q}(m_{n})).

This proposition then implies also the regularity of
\boldsymbol{\rho}
as indicated in next lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\rho}\geq 0
be a compact operator. Then for any
q\in[1,\infty]
,

(84)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{W}}^{1,q}(m_{n})}\leq 2\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{n})}\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{W}}^{1,q}(m_{n})}.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

By the product rule for commutators and Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms, for any
\eta\in\set{x,\xi}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}(\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}})\,\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}+\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}(\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}})\right)m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}(\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},

which implies Inequality (
84
).
∎

6.1.
The Strategy

Both the Hartree and the Hartree–Fock equations can be written in the form

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\rho}=\left[{H,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]

with
H=\frac{\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2}}{2}+V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
(with
\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=0
in the case of the Hartree equation). If we look at the time derivatives of quantum gradients, since
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}H=\nabla V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=-E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
and
\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}H,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}
and since
\left[{x,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]=\mathsf{X}_{\left[{x,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]}
and
\left[{\nabla,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]=\mathsf{X}_{\left[{\nabla,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]}
(see Lemma
6.7
), we obtain

(85)
