\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{H,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{H,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}.

These equations are of the form

(86)

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\left[{\mathsf{A},\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right]+\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right],

with
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
self-adjoint. Our goal is to propagate the weighted Schatten norms for solutions of these equations, where we recall that Schatten norms were defined in Equation (
15
). Computing the time derivative of such quantities, we get the following result.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\rho}
,
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
be self-adjoint operators and
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}(t)
be a family of self-adjoint operators satisfying Equation (
86
). Then, formally, for any even integer
q\geq 2
we have

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}\leq\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{A},m_{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{q}+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}.

Applying this lemma for
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\rho}
solution of the Hartree–Fock equation or for
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}
or
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}
, and with
m_{n}=1
(for
n=0
) or
m_{n}=\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}
for some
\mathrm{j}\in\set{1,2,3}
, we obtain

(87)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}

(88)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{n}\right\rVert_{q},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}

(89)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{n}\right\rVert_{q},

where we used the fact that
\left[{H,m_{n}}\right]=\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{n}}\right]
since
[\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2},m_{n}]=0
. In the next sections, we will bound all the weighted Schatten norms of the commutators appearing on the right-hand side of inequalities (
87
), (
88
) and (
89
) in order to get a Grönwall-type inequality.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

First notice that

\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{\mathsf{A},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}}\right]+\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left(\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\mu}\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\right).

Therefore, using that
2p:=q
is even and the cyclicity of the trace, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{2p}^{2p}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\tr\!\left(\left(m_{n}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}\right)^{p}\right)=p\tr\!\left(m_{n}\left(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2})\right)m_{n}\left(m_{n}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}\right)^{p-1}\right)

(90)

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{i\hbar}\tr\!\left(m_{n}\left(\left[{\mathsf{A},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}}\right]+\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\mu}\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\right)m_{n}\left(m_{n}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}\right)^{p-1}\right)

\displaystyle=:I_{\mathsf{A}}+I_{\mathsf{B}}.

For
I_{\mathsf{A}}
, we use again the cyclicity of the trace to write

\displaystyle I_{\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{i\hbar}\tr\!\left(\mathsf{A}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2})^{p-2}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}\mathsf{A}m_{n}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2})^{p-2}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{i\hbar}\tr\!\left(m_{n}^{2}\mathsf{A}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2})^{p-2}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}-\mathsf{A}m_{n}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2})^{p-2}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{i\hbar}\tr\!\left(\left[{m_{n}^{2},\mathsf{A}}\right]\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}m_{n}^{2}\right)^{p-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{2}\right).

This can also be written as

\displaystyle I_{\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{i\hbar}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\left(\left[{m_{n},\mathsf{A}}\right]m_{n}+m_{n}\left[{m_{n},\mathsf{A}}\right]\right)\boldsymbol{\mu}\left\lvert m_{n}\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rvert^{2(p-1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2p}{i\hbar}\im\!\left(\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\left[{m_{n},\mathsf{A}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\left\lvert m_{n}\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rvert^{2(p-1)}\right)\right).

Therefore, by Hölder’s inequality for the trace, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert I_{\mathsf{A}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2p}{\hbar}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{2p}\left\lVert\left[{m_{n},\mathsf{A}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{2p}\left\lVert m_{n}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{2p}^{2(p-1)}

(91)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{q}{\hbar}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}^{q-1}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{A},m_{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{q},

where we used the fact that since
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
m_{n}
are self-adjoint, and since the Schatten norm is invariant by taking the adjoint, we have
\left\lVert m_{n}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{2p}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{2p}
. For the
\mathsf{B}
term, we get more easily

I_{\mathsf{B}}=\frac{2p}{i\hbar}\im\!\left(\tr\!\left(m_{n}\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\left\lvert m_{n}\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rvert^{2(p-1)}\right)\right)\,.

By using again Hölder’s inequality and the commutation in the Schatten norm, we obtain

(92)

\left\lvert I_{\mathsf{B}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{q}{\hbar}\left\lVert m_{n}\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\right\rVert_{q}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}^{q-1}.

We conclude the proof by combining inequalities (
91
) and (
92
) on Formula (
90
) and using the fact that
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}=\tfrac{1}{q}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}^{1-q}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}^{q}
.
∎

6.2.
Preliminary Inequalities

In order to simplify the computations, we will sometimes use weights of the form

m_{n}=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n}

and

\tilde{m}_{n}=1+\sum_{\mathrm{j}=1}^{3}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}.

Thanks to the following lemma, these weights define equivalent weighted Schatten norms.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be even. Then there exists
C>0
such that for any
p\in[1,\infty]
and any operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}

(93)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\tilde{m}_{n}\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{p}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\tilde{m}_{n}\right\rVert_{p}

(94)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{p}\leq C\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{p}+\sum_{\mathrm{j}=1}^{3}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\right\rVert_{p}\right).

Proof.

We observe that
\tilde{m}_{n}
and
m_{n}
commute,
m_{n}
is invertible, and
m_{n}^{-1}\tilde{m}_{n}=g(\boldsymbol{p})
with
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}<C
uniformly in
\hbar
. Therefore, we obtain

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\tilde{m}_{n}\right\rVert_{p}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\,g(\boldsymbol{p})\right\rVert_{p}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{p},

which proves the first inequality of Equation (
93
). The second one follows by reversing the role of
\tilde{m}_{n}
and
m_{n}
, and the first inequality of Equation (
94
) by replacing
\tilde{m}_{n}
by
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}
. The second inequality of Equation (
94
) follows from the second inequality of Equation (
93
) and the triangle inequality for Schatten norms.
∎

We will need the following
rearrangement inequality
similar to
[
49
, Equation (56)]
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
p\geq 1
and
(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. Then for any self-adjoint operators
A
and
B
, the following inequality holds

(95)

\left\lVert B^{n}AB^{m}\right\rVert_{p}\leq 2\left\lVert AB^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}.

Proof of Lemma (
6.4
).

Assume that
A\geq 0
. Then by Hölder’s inequality, we have

(96)

\left\lVert B^{n}AB^{m}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert B^{n}A^{\frac{n}{n+m}}\right\rVert_{\frac{n+m}{n}\,p}\left\lVert A^{\frac{m}{n+m}}B^{m}\right\rVert_{\frac{n+m}{m}\,p}.

Now observe that since by definition of the absolute value
\left\lvert BA\right\rvert=\left\lvert\left\lvert B\right\rvert A\right\rvert
, we get
\left\lVert B^{n}A^{\frac{n}{n+m}}\right\rVert_{\frac{n+m}{n}\,p}=\left\lVert\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{n}A^{\frac{n}{n+m}}\right\rVert_{\frac{n+m}{n}\,p}
, and since the Schatten norm is invariant by taking the adjoint

\left\lVert A^{\frac{m}{n+m}}B^{m}\right\rVert_{\frac{n+m}{m}\,p}=\left\lVert B^{m}A^{\frac{m}{n+m}}\right\rVert_{\frac{n+m}{m}\,p}=\left\lVert\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{m}A^{\frac{m}{n+m}}\right\rVert_{\frac{n+m}{m}\,p}\,.

Now, by the Araki–Lieb–Thirring inequality

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{n}A^{\frac{n}{n+m}}\right\rVert_{\frac{n+m}{n}\,p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{n+m}A\right\rVert_{p}^{\frac{n}{n+m}}=\left\lVert AB^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}^{\frac{n}{n+m}},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{m}A^{\frac{m}{n+m}}\right\rVert_{\frac{n+m}{m}\,p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{n+m}A\right\rVert_{p}^{\frac{m}{n+m}}=\left\lVert AB^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}^{\frac{m}{n+m}}.

Combining these inequalities with Inequality (
96
) leads to

(97)

\left\lVert B^{n}AB^{m}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert AB^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}.

In the more general case of a self-adjoint operator
A
possibly not nonnegative, we write
A=A_{+}-A_{-}
with
A_{+}=\frac{\left\lvert A\right\rvert+A}{2}
and
\frac{\left\lvert A\right\rvert-A}{2}
. Then by Inequality (
97
) and the triangle inequality for Schatten norms, we get

\left\lVert B^{n}AB^{m}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert A_{+}B^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}+\left\lVert A_{-}B^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}\\
\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lVert\left\lvert A\right\rvert B^{n+m}+AB^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}+\left\lVert\left\lvert A\right\rvert B^{n+m}-AB^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}\right)\\
\leq\left\lVert\left\lvert A\right\rvert B^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}+\left\lVert AB^{n+m}\right\rVert_{p}

and we conclude by using again the fact that
\left\lvert\left\lvert A\right\rvert B\right\rvert=\left\lvert AB\right\rvert
.
∎

Let us define the adjoint representation of
A
as

\adj_{A}\!\left(B\right):=\left[{A,B}\right].

Then, using the above Lemma, we can prove the following inequality.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for any self-adjoint operators
A
and
B
, we have

\left\lVert\adj_{B}^{n}(A)\right\rVert_{p}\leq 2^{n+1}\left\lVert AB^{n}\right\rVert_{p}.

Proof.

This follows from the expansion

\adj_{B}^{n}(A)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left(-1\right)^{k}B^{n-k}AB^{k},

together with the triangle inequality for the Schatten norms and the rearrangement inequality (
95
).
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
(p_{0},p_{1})\in[2,\infty]^{2}
and
(n_{0},n_{1})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
. Then for any
A
self-adjoint and

\theta\in[0,1]
,

(98)

\left\lVert A\,B^{n_{\theta}}\right\rVert_{p_{\theta}}\leq\left\lVert A\,B^{n_{0}}\right\rVert_{p_{0}}^{1-\theta}\left\lVert A\,B^{n_{1}}\right\rVert_{p_{1}}^{\theta}

where
\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}=\frac{1-\theta}{p_{0}}+\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}
and
n_{\theta}=\left(1-\theta\right)n_{0}+\theta\,n_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be the set of values of
\theta\in[0,1]
such that Equation (
98
) holds. Then
0
and
1
are in
S
. Moreover, if
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are in
S
, then for
\theta:=\left(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}\right)/2
, since the Schatten norms are invariant by taking the adjoint and
A
and
B
are self-adjoint,

\left\lVert A\,B^{n_{\theta}}\right\rVert_{p_{\theta}}=\left\lVert B^{n_{\theta}}\,A\right\rVert_{p_{\theta}}=\left\lVert A\,B^{2\,n_{\theta}}\,A\right\rVert_{p_{\theta}/2}^{1/2}

Hence, since
2\,n_{\theta}=n_{\theta_{1}}+n_{\theta_{2}}
,
p_{\theta}\geq 2
and
2/p_{\theta}=1/n_{\theta_{1}}+1/n_{\theta_{2}}
, by Hölder’s inequality,

\left\lVert A\,B^{2\,n_{\theta}}\,A\right\rVert_{p_{\theta}/2}^{1/2}\leq\left\lVert A\,B^{n_{\theta_{1}}}\right\rVert_{p_{\theta_{1}}}^{1/2}\left\lVert A\,B^{n_{\theta_{2}}}\right\rVert_{p_{\theta_{2}}}^{1/2}

where we used again the invariance of Schatten norms by taking the adjoint. Hence
\theta\in S
, and so we deduce finally that
S
is a dense subset of
[0,1]
.
∎

The next proposition allows us to control
\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p}}
by
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}
for some weight
m_{n}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty]
and
n>\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any compact self-adjoint operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}

\left\lVert\diag\!\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}},

with
m_{n}=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n}
.

Remark 6.2
.

In particular, since for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\nabla^{k}\rho=\diag\!\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{k}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)
, the above estimate implies

\left\lVert\nabla^{k}\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{k}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}.

Proof.

Let
\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x):=\diag\!\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)\!(x)=h^{3}\boldsymbol{\mu}(x,x)
. Then, using the dual formulation of the
L^{p}
norm and separating
\varphi
into the sum of its positive and negative parts
\varphi:=\varphi_{+}+\varphi_{-}
, we have

\left\lVert\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq\sup_{\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq 1}\left(\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\varphi_{-}\right\rvert+\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\varphi_{+}\right\rvert\right),

from which we deduce that we can actually restrict ourselves to nonnegative functions
\varphi
and identifying the function
\varphi
with the multiplication operator by the function
\varphi
, we get

(99)

\left\lVert\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq 2\,\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\geq 0\\
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\varphi\right\rvert=2\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\geq 0\\
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\lvert h^{3}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\varphi\right)\right\rvert.

Taking
m_{n}(y):=\sqrt{1+\left\lvert y\right\rvert^{n}}
and
w(y)=m_{n}(y)^{-1}
, we see that
\boldsymbol{m}:=m_{n}(\boldsymbol{p})
is a positive invertible operator and its inverse
\boldsymbol{w}:=w(\boldsymbol{p})
is a compact operator. By Hölder’s inequality for the trace, we have

(100)

h^{3}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\varphi\right)=h^{3}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{m}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\boldsymbol{m}\,\boldsymbol{w}\,\varphi\,\boldsymbol{w}\right)\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{m}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\boldsymbol{m}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{w}\,\varphi\,\boldsymbol{w}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p^{\prime}}}.

However, since
\varphi
is a nonnegative function, we get that it is also a positive operator. Hence

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{w}\,\varphi\,\boldsymbol{w}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p^{\prime}}}=\left\lVert\left\lvert\sqrt{\varphi}\,\boldsymbol{w}\right\rvert^{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p^{\prime}}}=\left\lVert\sqrt{\varphi}\,\boldsymbol{w}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2p^{\prime}}}^{2}\leq\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{2p^{\prime}}}^{2}

where to get the last inequality we used the Kato–Seiler–Simon Inequality (
21
) since
2p^{\prime}\geq 2
. Combining the above inequality with inequalities (
99
) and (
100
) yields

\left\lVert\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq C_{p,n}\,\left\lVert\boldsymbol{m}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\boldsymbol{m}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq C_{p,n}\,\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\boldsymbol{m}^{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}

where the second inequality is a consequence of Lemma
6.4
, and
C_{p,n}=2\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{2p^{\prime}}}^{2}
is finite because
n>\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}
by assumption.
∎

6.3.
Commutators Involving the Direct Term

In the semiclassical case, instead of
\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\cdot\nabla_{\xi}f
(see Equation (
82
)), the time derivative of the gradient let appear the term
\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]
(see Equation (
85
)). Hence we will need to get semiclassical estimates on this quantity, which is the purpose of the following proposition.

Proposition 6.5

(Commutator estimates)
.

Let
a\in(0,1]
,
\mathfrak{b}=\frac{3}{a+1}
and
(q,r)\in[2,\infty]^{2}
be such that
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
. Then for any compact operator
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}
it holds

(101)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}}\right]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\,\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{B^{1-3\left(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}_{r,1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.

When
q=2
and
r=\infty
, we also have

(102)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}}\right]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq C\,\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}

for
a\in\left[\frac{1}{2},1\right]
, and

(103)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}}\right]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq C\,\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\frac{3}{1-a},1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}

for
a\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
.

From the fact that
(L^{r},W^{1,r})_{s,1}=B^{s}_{r,1}
for any
r\in[1,\infty)
and
s\in(0,1)
, we deduce the following inequality in terms of more classical Sobolev spaces.

Corollary 6.1
.

Let
(q,r)\in(2,\infty]\times(\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},\infty)
be such that
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
. Then it holds

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}}\right]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{r}}^{1-s}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{W^{1,r}}^{s}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

with
s=1-3\left(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)
.

From the fact that
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
and the fact that
r>\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}
, when
a\geq 1/2
, the above results only work when
q<q_{a}
with
\frac{1}{q_{a}}=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}-\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

First observe that the integral kernel of the operator
\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}}\right]
can be written as

\displaystyle\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}}\right]\!(x,y)

\displaystyle=\left(E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x)-E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(y)\right)\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x)-E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(y)\right)\otimes\left(x-y\right)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{2}}\cdot\left(x-y\right)\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}(x,y).

Thus, we can explicitly compute its Hilbert–Schmidt norm by computing the
L^{2}
norm of the kernel, and since the kernel of the operator
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}
is
\frac{x-y}{i\hbar}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}(x,y)
, we get the following estimate

(104)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}}\right]\right\rVert_{2}=\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\frac{\left(E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(x)-E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(y)\right)\otimes\left(x-y\right)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}(x,y)\right\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
\leq\left\lVert\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}.

In particular, for
a\in\left[\frac{1}{2},1\right]
, since
\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\nabla K*\nabla\rho
with
\nabla K\in L^{\mathfrak{b},\infty}
, we deduce Inequality (
102
) using the fact that the dual of
L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},1}
is
L^{\mathfrak{b},\infty}
(see e.g.
[
44
]
)

If
a\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
, we use that
\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\nabla^{2}K*\rho
with
\nabla^{2}K\in L^{\frac{3}{a+2},\infty}
. Thus Inequality (
103
) follows from Hölder’s inequality for Lorentz norms.

A second possibility is to use the fundamental theorem of calculus for
E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
and then the Fourier inversion theorem to rewrite the integral kernel of the commutator as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{i\hbar}[E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}](x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}((1-\theta)x+\theta y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\cdot(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2})(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}(z)\,e^{2i\pi z\cdot(1-\theta)x}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2})(x,y)\,e^{2i\pi z\cdot\theta y}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z,

which implies that by writing
e_{\omega}
the operator of multiplication by
e^{2i\pi\omega\cdot x}
, it holds

\frac{1}{i\hbar}[E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}]=\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}(z)\,e_{(1-\theta)z}\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\right)e_{\theta z}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z.

Since the operators
e_{\omega}
are bounded operators of norm
\left\lVert e_{\omega}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
, we deduce the following estimate on the operator norm of the commutator

(105)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}}\right]\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\int_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lvert\widehat{\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}(z)\right\rvert\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\right\rVert_{\infty}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z=\left\lVert\widehat{\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

In order to get a result for a general
q\in[2,\infty]
, we proceed by complex interpolation. Defining the vector-valued Hilbert–Schmidt operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}:=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}
, we observe that
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}(x,y)=i\hbar\,\frac{x-y}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}(x,y)
and the commutator can be rewritten as the bilinear operator

\Lambda(E,\boldsymbol{\mu}):=\left[{E,\frac{x-y}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{2}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right]=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{E,\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}}\right].

Thus, using the fact that
B^{0}_{\infty,1}\subset L^{\infty}
and
B^{\frac{3}{2}}_{2,1}\subset\cF\!\left(L^{1}\right)
, inequalities (
104
) and (
105
) imply

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Lambda(E,\boldsymbol{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert E\right\rVert_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{2},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Lambda(E,\boldsymbol{\mu})\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert E\right\rVert_{B^{1+\frac{3}{2}}_{2,1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

By the same proof, one obtains the inequality for any vector-valued Hilbert–Schmidt operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}
. Finally, we use the fact that the complex interpolation space between the involved Besov spaces is given by
[B^{1}_{\infty,1},B^{1+\frac{3}{2}}_{2,1}]_{\frac{2}{r}}=B^{1+\frac{3}{r}}_{r,1}
(see for example
[
16
, Theorem 6.4.5]
), while the complex interpolation of Schatten spaces
\mathfrak{S}^{q}
gives
[\mathfrak{S}^{2},\mathfrak{S}^{\infty}]_{1-\frac{2}{q}}=\mathfrak{S}^{q}
(see for example
[
75
, Section 1.19.7]
), so that by bilinear interpolation (see
[
16
, Section 4.4]
), we obtain

\left\lVert\Lambda(E,\boldsymbol{\mu})\right\rVert_{q}\leq C\left\lVert E\right\rVert_{B^{1+\frac{3}{r}}_{r,1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{q}\quad\text{ with }\quad\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q}.

If we take
E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\nabla K*\rho
with
\rho\in L^{1}\cap L^{p}
for some
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\nabla K\in L^{\mathfrak{b},\infty}
, we know that
E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\in L^{\tilde{r}}
for some
\tilde{r}\in(1,\infty)
. Moreover,
E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is proportional to
(-\Delta)^{\frac{a-3}{2}}\nabla\rho
, therefore we can apply
[
7
, Proposition 2.30]
and deduce that

\left\lVert E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{B^{1+\frac{3}{r}}_{r,1}}\leq C\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{B^{\frac{3}{r}+a-1}_{r,1}}=\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{B^{1-3\left(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}_{r,1}}.

Taking
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}
yields the results.
∎

To get estimates with weights, notice that we can write
\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}
in the following form

\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]-\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\rho}.

To control the
\mathcal{L}^{q}
norm of the first term of the right-hand side we use Proposition
6.5
, which gives

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{B^{1-3\left(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}_{r,1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}(\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},

and we can also replace
\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{B^{1-3\left(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}_{r,1}}
by
\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},1}}
when
q=2
.

To bound the second term, we will write the potential
K(x)
as a sum of a singular part localized near
x=0
and a long-range part and use Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
6.7
below. More precisely, for some infinitely smooth and compactly supported function
\chi
verifying
\mathds{1}_{|x|\leq 1}\leq\chi(x)\leq\mathds{1}_{|x|\leq 2}
, we can write

(106)

K=K_{0}+K_{\infty}

with

K_{0}=\chi\,K\quad\text{ and }\quad K_{\infty}=\left(1-\chi\right)K.

Now the first part of
K
verifies
\nabla K_{0}\in L^{\mathfrak{b}}
for any
\mathfrak{b}<3/2
while
K_{\infty}\in L^{\infty}\cap C^{\infty}
. We start by the following proposition to control the singular part of the potential.

Proposition 6.6

(Weighted commutator estimate)
.

Let
E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0}=-\nabla K_{0}*\rho
with
K_{0}=\chi\,K
as described above and let
m_{n}:=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n}
. Take
(q,r,r_{1})\in\left[\frac{3}{2},\infty\right]\times[1,\infty]^{2}
, such that

(107)

\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{r_{1}}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{3}.

Then for any
n_{0}>3/\mathfrak{b}-1
,
k^{\prime}>3/r^{\prime}-2
and
k>3/r-1
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\\
\leq C\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}\right).

Replacing
E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0}
by
V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0}=K_{0}*\rho
just amounts to replacing
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}
by
\boldsymbol{\rho}
, hence by the same proof,

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}\right).

Proof of Proposition
6.6
.

To shorten the notation, let
E:=E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0}
. We notice that
\left[{E,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}\right]=E\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}-\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}\,E=i\hbar\,\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E
is the operator of multiplication by
x\mapsto i\hbar\,\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E(x)
, and since
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2}=\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}
, we get

\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{E,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2}}\right]=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left(\left[{E,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}\right]\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}+\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}\left[{E,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}\right]\right)=\left(\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E\right)\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}+\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}\left(\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E\right),

and more generally, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[{E,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2k}\left(\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}+\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}\,\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E\right)\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2\left(n-k-1\right)}=\sum_{k=0}^{2n-1}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{k}\,\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-k}.

From this formula and the triangle inequality for Schatten norms, we deduce

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{2n-1}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{k}\,\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-k}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.

We cannot directly apply Hölder’s inequality here since
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{k}E
is an unbounded operator, therefore we have to make some commutations between
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{k}
and
\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E
. By the Leibniz formula

\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{k}\,\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}g_{\ell}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{k-\ell},

where
g_{\ell}
is the function defined by
g_{\ell}(x)=(\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{\ell}(\partial_{\mathrm{j}}E))(x)
, as usual also identified with a multiplication operator. This yields

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{2n-1}C_{\ell}\left\lVert g_{\ell}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-\ell}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},

where
C_{\ell}=\sum_{k=\ell}^{2n-1}\binom{k}{\ell}
. We will now distinguish two cases to bound the terms of the sum depending on the values of
\ell
.

1. Case
\ell
small.

Take
\ell<n
. In this case, we use Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms and the fact that the norm of the operator of multiplication by a function is the
L^{\infty}
norm of this function to deduce that

\left\lVert g_{\ell}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-\ell}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-\ell}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},

where we used Inequality (
94
). Now, observe that

g_{\ell}=-\nabla K_{0}*(\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{\ell}\partial_{\mathrm{j}}\rho)=-\nabla K_{0}*\diag\!\left(\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\right).

Therefore, since
\nabla K_{0}\in L^{\mathfrak{b}}
with
\mathfrak{b}<3/2
, by Young’s inequality

\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{K}\left\lVert\diag\!\left(\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\right)\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}},

where
C_{K}=\left\lVert\nabla K_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}}}
. By Proposition
6.4
and Lemma
6.5
, we get that for any
n_{0}>3/\mathfrak{b}-1>0
,

\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{K,n_{0}}\left\lVert\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)m_{2+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\leq 2^{\ell+1}C_{K,n_{0}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}

where we used the fact that
\ell\leq n-1
.

2. Case
\ell
large.

Take
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cap[n,2n-1]
and define
\frac{1}{\tilde{q}}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{3}
. Then since
\tilde{q}\leq q
,

\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}=h^{3/q}\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{q}\leq h^{3/q}\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\tilde{q}}=h^{-1}\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{q}}}.

Since
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{\tilde{q}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}
, and multiplying and dividing by
m_{k}:=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{k}
, we deduce

\displaystyle\left\lVert g_{\ell}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-\ell}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq h^{-1}\left\lVert g_{\ell}\,m_{k}^{-1}m_{k}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-\ell}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{q}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}^{1/r}\,h^{-1}\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n+k-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}},

where
C_{m}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}{(1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{k})^{r}}
is finite because
k<3/r
and we used the fact that
\ell\geq n
. Note that since
\ell\geq 1

g_{\ell}=-\partial_{\mathrm{j}}\nabla K_{0}*\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{\ell}\rho=i\hbar\left(\partial_{\mathrm{j}}\nabla K_{0}\right)*\diag\!\left(\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell-1}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\right).

Hence, to control
g_{\ell}
, we can use the fact that the convolution by
\partial_{\mathrm{j}}\nabla K_{0}
is continuous from
L^{r}
to
L^{r}
by the Calderón–Zygmund Theorem (see e.g.
[
28
, Theorem 5.1]
) to obtain

\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{r}}\leq C_{K}\,h\left\lVert\diag\!\left(\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell-1}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\right)\right\rVert_{L^{r}}.

By Proposition
6.4
and Lemma
6.5
, it yields for any
\varepsilon=k^{\prime}+2-3/r^{\prime}>0
,

h^{-1}\,\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{r}}\leq C_{K,\varepsilon}\left\lVert\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell-1}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)m_{3/r^{\prime}+\varepsilon}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\leq 2^{\ell}\,C_{K,k^{\prime}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}.

∎

Thanks to Lemma
6.6
, we can modify Proposition
6.6
in a way depending only on the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
and
\mathcal{L}^{4}
norms.

Corollary 6.2
.

Assume
n\geq 3
, then there exists
\theta\in(0,1)
and
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1-\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1/3}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{2/3}

and

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1-\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1/3}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{2/3}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}

Proof.

In the case
q=2
, use Proposition
6.6
with
r=2
,
r_{1}=3
to get for any
n_{0}>3/\mathfrak{b}-1
and
k>1/2
,

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq C\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{3}}\right).

Since
\nabla K_{0}\in L^{\mathfrak{b}}
for any
\mathfrak{b}<3/2
, we can in particular take
\mathfrak{b}\in(4/3,3/2)
so that
\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}\in(3,4)
and we can apply Lemma
6.6
with
p_{0}=2
,
p_{1}=4
and
p_{\theta}=b^{\prime}
leading to

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1-\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{\tilde{n}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{\theta}

where
\theta=4\left(\frac{1}{b}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\in(\frac{2}{3},1)
and
\tilde{n}\geq\frac{\left(2\theta-1\right)n+n_{0}}{\theta}\in(\frac{n+3n_{0}}{2},n+n_{0})
. On the other hand, taking
p_{\theta}=3
yields

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{3}}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1/3}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{\tilde{n}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{2/3}

with
\tilde{n}\geq\frac{n+3k}{2}
. In particular, when
n\geq 3
, taking
\mathfrak{b}
close to
3/2
,
k
close to
1/2
and
n_{0}
close to
1
allows to take
\tilde{n}\leq 2n-2
.

In the case
q=4
, take
r=3
and
r_{1}=4
in Proposition
6.6
to get for any
n_{0}>3/\mathfrak{b}-1
and
k^{\prime}>0
,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\hbar}&\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{0},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}\\
&\ \ \ \leq C\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{3}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}\right).\end{split}

As previously, we interpolate the
\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}
norm between the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
and the
\mathcal{L}^{4}
norm leading to

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{\tilde{n}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1-\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{\theta}

with again
\theta=4\left(\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\in(\frac{2}{3},1)
but with
\tilde{n}\geq\frac{\left(1-2\theta\right)n+n_{0}}{\theta}
possibly negative. On the other hand, taking
p_{\theta}=3
yields

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{3}}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{\tilde{n}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1/3}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{2/3}

with
\tilde{n}\geq 2n+3k^{\prime}
. In particular, taking
b
close to
3/2
,
n_{0}
close to
1
and
k^{\prime}
sufficiently small allows to take
\tilde{n}\leq 2n+2
.
∎

Now we treat the long range part
K_{\infty}
of the potential.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\infty}=-\nabla K_{\infty}*\rho
and
V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\infty}=K_{\infty}*\rho
with
\rho=\diag\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)
and
n\geq 1
be an integer, then there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\hbar
such that for any
q\in[1,\infty]
and any positive compact operators
\boldsymbol{\rho}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\right)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\right)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.

Proof of Proposition
6.7
.

As in the previous case, i.e. the proof of Proposition
6.6
, and with the same notations, it holds

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{2n-1}C_{\ell}\left\lVert g_{\ell}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-\ell}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},

where
C_{\ell}=\sum_{k=\ell}^{2n-1}\binom{k}{\ell}
. We use Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms and the fact that the norm of the operator of multiplication by a function is the
L^{\infty}
norm of this function to deduce that

\left\lVert g_{\ell}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-\ell}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{2n-1-\ell}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},

where we used Inequality (
94
). Now, observe that

g_{\ell}=-\partial_{\mathrm{j}}\nabla K_{\infty}*(\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{\ell}\rho)=-\partial_{\mathrm{j}}\nabla K_{\infty}*\diag\!\left(\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\right).

Therefore, since
\partial_{\mathrm{j}}\nabla K_{\infty}\in L^{2}
, by Young’s inequality, which is just the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in this case

\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{K}\left\lVert\diag\!\left(\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\right)\right\rVert_{L^{2}},

where
C_{K}=\left\lVert\partial_{\mathrm{j}}\nabla K_{\infty}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
. By Proposition
6.4
, we get that

\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left\lVert\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)m_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}.

When
\ell=0
, since
2n\geq 2
, it implies that

\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}.

When
\ell>0
, we use the fact that
\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)=-i\hbar\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}
,
\ell\leq 2n-1
and Lemma
6.5
to get

\left\lVert g_{\ell}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\,\hbar\left\lVert\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{\ell-1}\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)m_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq 2^{\ell}\,C_{K,n_{0}}\,\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}.

In the case when
E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\infty}
is replaced by
V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{\infty}
, one obtains the same estimates with
\ell>0
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}
replaced by
\boldsymbol{\rho}
. The only remaining point is the case
\ell=0
, that is defining
g_{\ell}=-\partial_{\mathrm{j}}K_{\infty}*(\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{\ell}\rho)
, it remains to notice that since
\partial_{\mathrm{j}}K_{\infty}\in L^{\infty}
,

\left\lVert g_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}=\left\lVert\partial_{\mathrm{j}}K_{\infty}*\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{K}\,\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

with
C_{K}=\left\lVert\nabla K_{\infty}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
.
∎

6.4.
Preliminary Properties of the Exchange Operator

6.4.1.
Preliminary Identities

Let
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
be the operator of integral kernel
\mathsf{X}(x,y)=K(x-y)\,\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y)
with
K(x)=\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{-a}
and recall the notation of the adjoint representation of
A
,
\adj_{A}\!\left(B\right)=\left[{A,B}\right]
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
a\in(0,1]
. Then the following identities holds true

\displaystyle\left[{x,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{X}_{\left[{x,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]},

\displaystyle\left[{\nabla,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{X}_{\left[{\nabla,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]},

and more generally, with the adjoint notation,
\adj_{x}^{n}(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})=\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{x}^{n}(\boldsymbol{\rho})}
and
\adj_{\nabla}^{n}(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})=\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{\nabla}^{n}(\boldsymbol{\rho})}
. In particular, since
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}=\adj_{\nabla}\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\adj_{x}\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)
, it can be written
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{n}(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})=\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{n}\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{n}(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})=\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{n}\boldsymbol{\rho}}
.

Proof.

The first identity follows immediately by looking at the integral kernel of the operator

\left[{x,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right](x,y)=\frac{\left(x-y\right)\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{a}}=\mathsf{X}_{\left[{x,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]}(x,y).

To get the second we take
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}
, integrate by parts and use the fact that
\nabla_{x}K(x-y)=-\nabla_{y}K(x-y)
to get

\left[{\nabla,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]\!\varphi(x)=\nabla\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{a}}\,\varphi(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{a}}\,\nabla\varphi(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{y}\right)\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{a}}\right)\,\varphi(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\left(\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{y}\right)\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y)\right)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{a}}\,\varphi(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

and we conclude by noticing that
\left(\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{y}\right)\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y)\right)
is nothing but the integral kernel of the operator
\left[{\nabla,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]
.
∎

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\eta=1
,
\eta=\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}
or
\eta=x_{\mathrm{j}}
, then we have

(108)

\displaystyle\eta^{n}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{\eta}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\rho})}\,\eta^{n-k}

(109)

\displaystyle\left[{\eta^{n},\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{\eta}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\rho})}\,\eta^{n-k}.

Proof.

Since
A^{k}B=(A^{k-1}B)A+A^{k-1}\adj_{A}\!\left(B\right)
, we easily deduce the following commutator expansion

A^{n}B=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\adj_{A}^{k}(B)\,A^{n-k}.

Therefore, we deduce the result by taking
B=\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
and by Lemma
6.7
.
∎

6.4.2.
Preliminary Inequalities

We know from
[
49
, Equation (39a)]
that if
a\in[0,3/2)
and
q=2
, then

(110)

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{q}\leq C\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}\right\rVert_{2}.

By the fact that the Schatten norms of smaller order controls the Schatten norms of higher order, we deduce that this inequality actually holds for any
q\in[2,\infty]
. The next proposition will allow us to put the weight
\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}
on an other operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}
instead of
\boldsymbol{\rho}
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
be compact operators. Then for any
q\in[2,\infty]
and any
\theta\in\{0,1\}
,

(111)

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{q}\leq C_{a}\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a\left(1-\theta\right)}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{\theta a}\right\rVert_{\infty},

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}
is the adjoint operator of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

Proof.

Take
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
a compact but possibly not self-adjoint operator. Then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\tr\!\left(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}^{*}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}\right)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{*}(x,y)\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}(y,x)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{2a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\frac{\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}(x,y)\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{2a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\frac{\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}(x,y+x)\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert y\right\rvert^{2a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

so that by the Hardy–Rellich inequality,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{a}\,\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\Delta^{a/2}_{y}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}(x,y+x)\right\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{C}_{a}\,\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\Delta^{a/2}_{y}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}(x,y)\right\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq C_{a}\,h^{-2a}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}\right)(x,y)\right\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

where
\mathcal{C}_{a}
is the constant appearing in the Hardy–Rellich inequality and
C_{a}=\left(2\pi\right)^{a}\mathcal{C}_{a}
. From this we deduce the generalization of (
110
) for possibly not self-adjoint operators

(112)

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}\right\rVert_{2}\leq C_{a}\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}\right\rVert_{2}.

By Hölder’s inequality, taking
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, it implies (
111
) when
\theta=0
.
Now, noticing that we have the following integration by parts like formula

\tr\!\left(\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x,y)\,\boldsymbol{\mu}(y,x)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\tr\!\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right),

and using the cyclicity of the trace, Hölder’s inequality and Inequality (
112
) with
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}
, we get

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\tr\!\left(\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}}\,\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\right)=\tr\!\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\,\mathsf{X}_{(\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*})}\right)\\
\leq\left\lVert\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{(\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*})}\right\rVert_{2}\leq C_{a}\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}\right\rVert_{2}.

By Hölder’s inequality, this leads to

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq C_{a}\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

We deduce the result by dividing both sides by
\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}
and then using the fact that for
q\geq 2
,
\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{q}\leq\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}
.
∎

The following lemma will allow us to replace the Hilbert–Schmidt norm on the right-hand side of Inequality (
110
) by another Schatten norm with higher index at the expense of using a less sharp power on
\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mu}
be a compact operator. Then for any
\alpha>a
and any
q\in[2,\infty]
, it holds

(113)

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{q}\leq C\,h^{-\alpha}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\left(1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{\alpha}\right)\right\rVert_{q},

for a constant
C
depending only on
a
and
\alpha
.

Proof.

Take
(\varphi,\phi)\in(L^{2})^{2}
. Then one has

\Braket{\varphi|\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi}_{L^{2}}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mu}(x,y)\,\overline{\varphi(x)}\,\phi(y)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\left(2\pi\right)^{3-a}C_{a}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\varphi\left(-\Delta\right)^{\frac{a-3}{2}}\phi\right),

where
\varphi
and
\phi
are seen as multiplication operators and
C_{a}=\frac{\omega_{a}}{\omega_{3-a}}
. By the definition of
\boldsymbol{p}
, this can be written

\Braket{\varphi|\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi}_{L^{2}}=C_{a}h^{3-a}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\varphi\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a-3}\phi\right)=C_{a}h^{3-a}\tr\!\left(m_{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{\alpha}\,m_{\alpha}^{-1}\varphi\,g(\boldsymbol{p})\,\phi\,m_{\alpha}^{-1}\right),

with
g(x)=\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{a-3}
and
m_{\alpha}=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{\alpha}
. Now taking
1\leq\frac{3}{\alpha}<p_{0}^{\prime}<\frac{3}{a}<p_{1}^{\prime}\leq\infty
such that
\frac{1}{p_{0}^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p_{1}^{\prime}}=\frac{a}{3}
, we have
g\in L^{p_{0}}+L^{p_{1}}
, hence we can write
g=g_{0}+g_{1}
with
(g_{0},g_{1})\in L^{p_{0}}\times L^{p_{1}}
. Let
\tilde{g}=g_{0}
or
\tilde{g}=g_{1}
, or more generally, take
\tilde{g}\in L^{p}
for some
p\geq 1
satisfying
p^{\prime}>\frac{3}{\alpha}
. Then, by Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms, Lemma
6.4
and the Kato–Seiler–Simon Inequality (
21
), we have

\displaystyle h^{3}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\tr\!\left(m_{\alpha}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{\alpha}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,m_{\alpha}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\,\tilde{g}(\boldsymbol{p})\,\phi\,m_{\alpha}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert m_{\alpha}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{\alpha}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert m_{\alpha}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2p^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\varphi^{\frac{1}{p}}\tilde{g}(\boldsymbol{p})^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2p}}\left\lVert\tilde{g}(\boldsymbol{p})^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2p}}\left\lVert\phi^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}m_{\alpha}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\left\lVert\tilde{g}\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert_{L^{2}},

where we used the notation
z^{b}=\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{b-1}z
and with
C_{p}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}{\left(1+\left\lvert y\right\rvert^{\alpha}\right)^{p^{\prime}}}
. This constant is finite since
\alpha p^{\prime}>3
. This proves Inequality (
113
) when
q=\infty
. When
q=2
, the inequality follows from Formula (
112
). The other cases follow by complex interpolation.
∎

6.5.
Commutators Involving the Exchange Term

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
a\in[0,1]
. Then there exists
C>0
such that for any compact self-adjoint operators
\boldsymbol{\rho}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, any
q\in[1,\infty]
and any integer
n\geq 2a-1

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq 3^{n}\,h^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\,C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},

where
m_{n}=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n}
.

Proof.

By Formula (
109
), and the triangle inequality, we have

\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{q}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k}\!(\boldsymbol{\rho})}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n-k}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{q}.

Now, Lemma
6.9
gives the bound

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k}\!(\boldsymbol{\rho})}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n-k}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{q}\leq C_{a}\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k}\!(\boldsymbol{\rho})\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a\left(1-\theta\right)}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n-k}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{\theta a}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Using the fact that
\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}\!(\boldsymbol{\rho})=-i\hbar\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}
and expanding the
k-1
commutators in
\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k-1}
by Lemma
6.5
, we get

\left\lVert\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k}\!(\boldsymbol{\rho})\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a\left(1-\theta\right)}\right\rVert_{2}\leq 2^{k}\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a\left(1-\theta\right)+k-1}\right\rVert_{2}.

Now when
k\geq a
, we take
\theta=1
so that
n-k+\theta a\leq n
and
a\left(1-\theta\right)+k-1=k-1\leq n
. When
k<a
, we take
\theta=0
so that
n-k+\theta a=n-k\leq n
and
a\left(1-\theta\right)+k-1\leq 2a-1\leq n
. In all the cases, this leads to

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\right\rVert_{q}\leq C\,h^{-a}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}2^{k}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

We conclude using the fact that
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}
and the definition (
20
) of the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
norm.
∎

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
a\in[0,1]
,
\mathfrak{b}=\frac{3}{a+1}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
n\geq 2a
. Then for any
\alpha\in(a,n-a]
and any
q\in[2,\infty]

(114)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq 3^{n}\,C\,h^{3\left(\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}

(115)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq 3^{n}\,C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left(h^{\frac{3}{\beta^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+h^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\right),

where
m_{n}=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n}
and
\beta=\frac{3}{\alpha+1}
.

Note that the power of
h
in the first formula is nonnegative only for
q\leq q_{a}
with
\frac{1}{q_{a}}=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}-\frac{1}{2}
. In the second formula, this is true for every
q
but involves a semiclassical derivative of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.9
.

Since the exchange term is vanishing when
\hbar\to 0
, we can estimate the two parts of the commutator separately by writing

\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\right\rVert_{q}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\right\rVert_{q}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{q}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}.

The first term in the right-hand side can be bounded using Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms and Lemma
6.9
with
\theta=0
, leading to

(116)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{q}\leq C\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}\right\rVert_{2}\leq C\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{2}.

We can also use Lemma
6.10
with
\alpha\in(a,n]
, to get

(117)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rVert_{q}\leq C\,h^{-\alpha}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\left(1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{\alpha}\right)\right\rVert_{q}\leq C\,h^{-\alpha}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}.

To treat the second term, we want to put the first weight
m_{n}
either on
\boldsymbol{\mu}
either on
\boldsymbol{\rho}
. To obtain this effect, we use Formula (
108
) to get

(118)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k}\!(\boldsymbol{\mu})}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n-k}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\right\rVert_{q}.

Now we use Lemma
6.9
and then expand the commutators by Lemma
6.5
to get for any
\theta\in\{0,1\}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k}\!(\boldsymbol{\mu})}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n-k}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\right\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq C\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k}\!(\boldsymbol{\mu})\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a\left(1-\theta\right)}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n-k}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{\theta a}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{k}\,C\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\left(1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{k+a\left(1-\theta\right)}\right)\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\left(1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n-k+\theta a}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty},

and similarly as in the proof of Proposition
6.8
, if
k\geq a
, we take
\theta=1
and if
k\leq a
, we take
\theta=0
and use the fact that
2a\leq n
. In any cases, the power on
\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert
is smaller than
n
. Therefore, recalling Inequality (
118
), we obtain

(119)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}\leq 3^{n}\,C\,h^{-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{2}.

Combining inequalities (
116
) and (
119
) and using the definition (
20
) of
\mathcal{L}^{q}
norms yield Formula (
114
).

To get Formula (
115
), we start back from Inequality (
118
). If
k>a
, so that in particular
k\geq 1
, we use again Lemma
6.9
and Lemma
6.5
but we use first the fact that
\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{j}}\!(\boldsymbol{\mu})=-i\hbar\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\mu}
to get an additional
\hbar
. This yields

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k}\!(\boldsymbol{\mu})}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n-k}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\right\rVert_{q}\leq 2^{k}\,C\,h^{1-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\mu}\left(1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{k-1}\right)\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\left(1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n-k+a}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty},

If
k\leq a
, then we use Lemma
6.10
with
\alpha\in(a,n-a]
to get

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\adj_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}}^{k}\!(\boldsymbol{\mu})}\,\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n-k}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\right\rVert_{q}\leq 2^{k}\,C\,h^{-\alpha}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\left(1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{k+\alpha}\right)\right\rVert_{q}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\left(1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n-k}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Therefore, Inequality (
118
) implies

(120)

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}}^{n}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}\leq 3^{n}\,C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left(h^{-\alpha}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{q}+h^{1-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x_{\mathrm{j}}}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{2}\right),

and together with Inequality (
116
) and the definition of the
\mathcal{L}^{q}
norm, this implies Formula (
115
).
∎

6.6.
Proof of the Propagation of Regularity

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

The strategy to prove Proposition
6.1
is to look at Equations (
87
), (
88
) and (
89
) and find a Grönwall-type inequality on
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{W}^{1,q}(m_{2n})}
(observe that we renamed
n
by
2n
as we need the number of moments to be even). In particular we will see that to close the Grönwall argument for
q=2
, we need to estimate
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{W}^{1,q}(m_{2n})}
for
q\in\set{2,4}
. We will therefore proceed by interpolation and define

M_{2}(t):=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(m_{2n})},\quad M_{4}(t):=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{W}^{1,4}(m_{2n-2})},\quad M_{\infty}(t):=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}.

For
a\in\left[\frac{1}{2},1\right]
we will find a Grönwall-type inequality on
M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)+M_{\infty}(t)
, whereas for
a\in\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
it suffices to apply Grönwall’s Lemma to
M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)
.

We now look at Equation (
87
). Splitting the interaction
K
as in (
106
), by Proposition
6.7
and Proposition
6.6
we have that, for
1/r+1/r_{1}=1/q+1/3
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+C\left(\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\right)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

with

n_{0}>\frac{3}{\mathfrak{b}}-1,\quad\quad k^{\prime}>\frac{3}{r^{\prime}}-2,\quad\quad k>\frac{3}{r}-1.

The contribution given by the exchange term in the right-hand side of (
87
) can be bounded by Proposition
6.8
with
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\rho}
. Therefore, we obtain the following bounds on the right-hand side of Equation (
87
)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\big(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\qquad+h^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\big).

In particular, for
q=\infty
we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\big(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\qquad+h^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\big).

Note that in order to close the Grönwall inequality and we will need bounds on
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}
. To this end, we look at Equation (
88
) and Equation (
89
). We start bounding the right-hand side of Equation (
88
). By Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
6.7
we obtain

(121)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\hbar}&\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\\
&\quad\leq C\,\big(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}\\
&\qquad\quad+\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\big)\end{split}

with the usual constraints on
r,r_{1},n_{0},k,k^{\prime}
.

By writing
\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}=\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}}\right]+\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]
, applying Proposition
6.5
with
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}=\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}
, and Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
6.7
with
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\rho}
, we get

(122)

\begin{split}\dfrac{1}{\hbar}&\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\,\big(\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{r}}^{1-s}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{W^{1,r}}^{s}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\\
&\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}\\
&\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\big)\end{split}

for
q>2
and
s=1-3\left(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}\right)
, where we used the interpolation of Besov spaces stated in Corollary
6.1
.

For
q=2
, we have

(123)

\begin{split}\dfrac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}&\leq C\,\big(\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\\
&\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1-\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\\
&\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1/3}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{2/3}\\
&\qquad+{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\big)\end{split}

for
a\in\left[\frac{1}{2},1\right]
, and

(124)

\begin{split}\dfrac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}&\leq C\,\big(\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\frac{3}{1-a},1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\\
&\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1-\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{\theta}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\\
&\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{1/3}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}}^{2/3}\\
&\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}^{2}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\big)\end{split}

for
a\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
.

The contributions of the exchange term can be bounded using Proposition
6.8
and Proposition
6.9
. Combining them with (
121
) and (
122
) leads to, for
q>2
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\big(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\big)
