\displaystyle+C\,\big(\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{r}}^{1-s}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{W^{1,r}}^{s}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\big)

\displaystyle+C\,h^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle+C\,h^{3\left(\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}.

To bound the right-hand side of Equation (
89
), we use Proposition
6.6
for the contribution due to the direct term and Proposition
6.8
and Proposition
6.9
to estimate the contributions of the exchange term. Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\big(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\,h^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\,h^{3\left(\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}.

To get an estimate in
\mathcal{L}^{2}
we need a bound on the
\mathcal{L}^{q}
norm for
q\in(2,4)
. Therefore we look for a bound when
q=4
using Corollary
6.2
and proceed by interpolation.

To establish a Grönwall type inequality for
a\geq\frac{1}{2}
, we consider the sum
M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)+M_{\infty}(t)
and observe that it satisfies

(125)

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left(M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)+M_{\infty}(t)\right)\leq C\left(M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)+M_{\infty}(t)\right)\\
+C\left(1+h^{\frac{3}{2}-a}+h^{\frac{3}{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}+h^{3\left(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\right)\left(M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)+M_{\infty}(t)\right)^{2},

where we used interpolation inequality with
\theta\in(0,1)
and Young’s inequality for products to bound the norms
\mathcal{L}^{r},\,\mathcal{L}^{r^{\prime}},\,L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}
with
r,\,r^{\prime},\,\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}\in[2,4]
. Furthermore, we used the following simple inequality: for an operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
k\in(0,2n)
and
q\geq 2
,
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n-k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\left\lVert m_{k}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}
.

We observe that Equation (
125
) is a Grönwall-type inequality of the same form as Equation (
83
). Thus there exists a time
T>0
, depending only in the initial data, such that
M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)+M_{\infty}(t)
is bounded for all
t\in[0,T]
.

For
a<\frac{1}{2}
, we consider the quantity

M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)
and use that

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\leq Ch^{-\frac{3}{q}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.

Hence

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left(M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)\right)\\
\leq C\left(M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)\right)+C\left(1+h^{\frac{3}{2}-a}+h^{3\left(\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}\right)\left(M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)\right)^{2}.

Therefore there exists
T>0
, depending only in the initial data, such that
M_{2}(t)+M_{4}(t)
is bounded for all
t\in[0,T]
, thus
\boldsymbol{\rho}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(m_{2n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{1,4}(m_{2n})\cap\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(m_{2n}))
. Moreover,
{\rho}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),H^{1}\cap W^{1,4}\cap L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})
thanks to Proposition
6.4
and the bounds on
M_{2}(t),\,M_{4}(t)
and
M_{\infty}(t)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.2
.

Similarly to what we have done for the first-order quantum gradients, we can compute the time derivative of the second order quantum gradients of
\boldsymbol{\rho}

(126)

\begin{split}i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}&=\left[{H,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-2\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-\left[{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-2\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\\
i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}&=\left[{H,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-i\hbar\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}-2\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\\
i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}&=\left[{H,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-i\hbar\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}-\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]-\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right],\end{split}

that are of the form

(127)

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\left[{\mathsf{A},\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right]+\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]+\left[{\mathsf{C},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right],

with
\mathsf{A},\,\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{C}
being self-adjoint operators. The proof of Lemma
6.2
proves also the following statement.

Lemma 6.11

(
Lemma
6.2
bis).

Let
\boldsymbol{\rho},\,\mathsf{A},\,\mathsf{B},\,\mathsf{C}
be self-adjoint operators and
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}(t)
be a family of self-adjoint operators satisfying (
127
). Then, formally, for any even integer
q\geq 2
we have

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\leq\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{A},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{q}+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{B},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{C},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}.

We consider the Identities (
126
) and bound them by Lemma
6.11
. This yields

(128)

\begin{split}\hbar\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}&\leq C\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}+C\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\\
&+C\left\lVert\left[{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}+C\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}\\
&+C\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}+C\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\end{split}

(129)

\begin{split}\hbar\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}&\leq C\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}+C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\\
&+C\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}+C\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\\
&+C\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\end{split}

(130)

\begin{split}\hbar\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}&\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\leq C\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}+C\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\\
&\qquad\qquad+C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}+C\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{q}\\
&\qquad\qquad+C\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}+C\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}.\end{split}

We now estimate the right-hand side of Equation (
128
). The first three contributions are related to the direct term in the Hartree equation, whereas in the others the exchange operator appears. By Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
6.7
we have

(131)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\hbar}&\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}^{q}}\\
&\qquad+C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}+C\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\\
&\qquad+C\,\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\end{split}

As for the second term on the right-hand side of Equation (
128
), we rewrite it as follows:

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}=\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}}\right]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.

By Proposition
6.5
and Corollary
6.1
, we get

(132)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}}\right]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{r}}^{1-s}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{W^{1,r}}^{s}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},

for
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
and
s=1-3\left(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)
. By Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
6.7
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.

This, together with (
132
), controls the second term in the right-hand side of Equation (
128
).

The third term on the right-hand side of Equation (
128
) can be dealt in an analogously manner as to the second term by using that
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=E_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}
and Proposition
6.6
. This gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left[{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{r}}^{1-s}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{W^{1,r}}^{s}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.

We now turn to terms in which the contribution of the exchange term appears. By Proposition
6.8
we obtain

(133)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\hbar^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\,.

By Proposition
6.9
we get the bound

(134)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\hbar^{3\left(\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\,.

Finally, by noticing that
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, we apply Proposition
6.9
to the last term on the right-hand side in Equation (
128
):

(135)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\hbar^{3\left(\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\,.

Therefore, using Proposition
6.1
and estimates (
131
)–(
135
)
we obtain a bound on the time derivative of
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}
.

We now look at the right-hand side of Equation (
129
). By using Proposition
6.1
, Proposition
6.6
, Proposition
6.7
, Proposition
6.8
, Proposition
6.9
and by Proposition
6.9
with
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}}
we obtain a bound on the time derivative of
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}
.

As for the mixed term (
130
), its right-hand side can be bounded as follows. By Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
6.7
we get

(136)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}&\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\\
&+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n+k^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n+k}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}\\
&+\left(\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}+\hbar\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\right)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.\end{split}

As for the second term on the right-hand side of Equation (
130
), we rewrite it as

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}=\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}}\right]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.

and use again Proposition
6.5
and Corollary
6.1
for the first term on the right-hand side and Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
6.7
for the second term on the right-hand side. We turn now on the terms in which the contribution of the exchange term appears. By Proposition
6.8
we obtain

(137)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}\leq C\,\hbar^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}.

By Proposition
6.9
we get the bounds

(138)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\hbar^{3\left(\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}.

(139)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C\hbar^{3\left(\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}.

Therefore, using Proposition
6.1
and estimates (
136
)–(
139
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{r_{1}}}+C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}+C\,\hbar^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\,\hbar^{3\left(\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}+C\,\hbar^{3\left(\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}

for
s=1-3\left(\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)
and with the constraints

\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{r_{1}}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}
and
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{2}
. Now we define

N_{x,q}(t):=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},\quad N_{v,q}(t):=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}},\quad N_{xv,q}(t):=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}}

and denote by
N_{2n,q}(t)
the quantity

N_{2n,q}(t)=N_{x,q}(t)+N_{v,q}(t)+N_{xv,q}(t).

Then we proceed as for the first-order gradients. Using Proposition
6.1
, we obtain a bound on the time derivative of
N_{2n,2}(t)+N_{2n-2,4}
.

For
a\in\left[\frac{1}{2},1\right]
, we consider the quantity

F_{2n,\infty}(t):=N_{2n,2}(t)+N_{2n-2,4}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{W}}^{1,\infty}}
and look for a Grönwall type inequality. From Equation (
88
) and Equation (
89
) with
q=\infty
, we obtain an upper bound on the time derivative of
F_{2n,\infty}(t)
, using Equation (
105
) and Proposition
6.4
and standard interpolation allows to conclude by Grönwall’s Lemma.

For
a\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
, using that
\boldsymbol{\rho}\in\mathcal{W}^{1,4}(m_{2n-2})
by Proposition
6.1
and that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,h^{-\frac{3}{4}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{-\frac{3}{4}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{2n-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{4}},

we get an estimate on the time derivative of
N_{2n,2}(t)+N_{2n-2,4}(t)
. By Grönwall’s inequality we conclude that
\boldsymbol{\rho}\in\mathcal{W}^{2,2}(m_{2n})\cap\mathcal{W}^{2,4}(m_{2n-2})
for
a\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

We observe that analogously to (
87
), (
88
) and (
89
), the following bounds hold

\hbar\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\leq\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{q}+\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}X_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{q},

(140)

\begin{split}\hbar\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}&\leq\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{q}+\left\lVert\left[{E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}\\
&\quad+\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}X_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{q}+\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}X_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q},\end{split}

and

(141)

\displaystyle\hbar\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{q}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!x}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m_{2n}}\right]\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{q}+\left\lVert\left[{h^{3}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}},\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]m_{2n}\right\rVert_{q}.

As in Proposition
6.1
, we look for a Grönwall type inequality. To this end, we define

\widetilde{M}_{q}(t)=\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(m_{2n})}+\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q}(m_{2n})},

for
q\in[2,\infty]
and notice that, because of Propositions
6.6
, Proposition
6.7
and
6.8
, we have

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\widetilde{M}_{q}(t)\leq C\,M_{r}(t)\,\widetilde{M}_{{r_{1}}}(t)+C\,M_{2}(t)\,\widetilde{M}_{q}(t)\,,

that implies the boundedness of
\widetilde{M}_{q}(t)
for
q\in[2,\infty]
thanks to Proposition
6.1
.

We now define the quantity

\widetilde{N}_{q}(t)=\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{W}}^{1,2}(m_{2n})}+\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{W}}^{1,q}(m_{2n})},

for
q\in[2,\infty]
and using Equations (
140
) and (
141
) we compute

(142)

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\!\left(\widetilde{N}_{2}(t)+\widetilde{N}_{q}(t)\right).

The contributions due to the direct term in (
142
) can be estimated in terms of
M_{r}
and
\widetilde{N}_{r}
by Proposition
6.6
, in terms of
M_{r}^{1+\theta}
(for
\theta\in(0,1)
) and
\widetilde{N}_{q}
by Proposition
6.5
, together with
\widetilde{N}_{r}
by Proposition
6.6
. The contributions due to the exchange term in (
142
) can be estimated in terms of
M_{2}
and
\widetilde{N}_{q}
by Proposition
6.8
, and in terms of
\widetilde{M}_{q}
,
\widetilde{N}_{q}
and
N_{2}
by Proposition
6.9
. Hence, in the same spirit of the proofs of Propositions
6.1
and
6.2
, using the results of Proposition
6.1
and Proposition
6.2
, we conclude by Grönwall’s Lemma obtaining boundedness of
\widetilde{N}_{q}
for
q\in[2,\infty]
.
∎

Part III
Mean-Field Limit

7.
Scaling

In order to define the Bogoliubov rotation as explained in Section
4.3
, we define

(143)

\omega:=\lambda\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\ \text{ with }\lambda=N\,h^{3}

so that
\tr\!\left(\omega\right)=N
and
0\leq\omega\leq\lambda\,\mathcal{C}_{\infty}\leq 1
. Notice that in the critical scaling
N=C\,h^{-3}
,
\lambda
is a constant, while in the other cases when
N=h^{-c}
with
c<3
we have
\lambda\to 0
. We also define

v=\sqrt{\omega}\quad\text{ and }\quad u=\sqrt{1-\omega},

which are well defined bounded positive operators since
0\leq\omega\leq 1
. With these definitions, we obtain the following behavior for the Schatten norms for
p\in[1,\infty]

\displaystyle\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{p}\,N\,h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{p/2}^{1/2}\,N^{1/2}\,h^{3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)},

where
\mathcal{C}_{p}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}
and
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
. The operator
u
satisfies
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
, but of course
u
is not bounded in other Schatten norms. However, it is possible to prove that
0\leq 1-u\leq\omega
, hence
1-u
is of the same order of magnitude as
\omega
. Since
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}u=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}(1-u)
, it explains why we can expect the gradients of
u
to be of the same order as
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}\omega
, as indicated more precisely in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.1
.

Assume
\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{\infty}=\lambda\,\mathcal{C}_{\infty}<1
. Then

C\,\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}u\,m\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}\,\omega\,m\right\rVert_{p}+\left\lVert\omega\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}m\right\rVert_{p},

with
C=2\sqrt{1-\lambda\,\mathcal{C}_{\infty}}
. In particular, it implies that

C\,\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}u\,m\right\rVert_{p}\leq\mathcal{D}_{p}\,N\,h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}

where
\mathcal{D}_{p}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}m\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}
is of order
1
in the semiclassical limit.

Proof.

Since
\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{\infty}<1
, we can write
u=(1-\omega)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{1/2}{n}\left(-1\right)^{n}\omega^{n}
. Therefore, for
\eta\in\set{x,\xi}
, we obtain

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}u\,m\right\rVert_{p}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}(u-1)\,m\right\rVert_{p}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left\lvert\binom{1/2}{n}\right\rvert\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}\left(\omega^{n}\,m\right)\right\rVert_{p}.

Expanding the gradient with the product rule for commutators gives

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}\left(\omega^{n}\,m\right)=\omega^{n}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}m+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega^{k-1}\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}\omega\right)\omega^{n-k}

which leads to

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}u\,m\right\rVert_{p}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left\lvert\binom{1/2}{n}\right\rvert n\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{\infty}^{n-1}\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}\,\omega\,m\right\rVert_{p}+\left\lVert\omega\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\eta}m\right\rVert_{p}\right).

Moreover, for
n\geq 1
,
\left\lvert\binom{1/2}{n}\right\rvert=\left(-1\right)^{n-1}\binom{1/2}{n}
and
\binom{1/2}{n}=\frac{1}{2n}\binom{-1/2}{n-1}
, from which we deduce

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left\lvert\binom{1/2}{n}\right\rvert n\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{\infty}^{n-1}\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\binom{-1/2}{n-1}\left(-1\right)^{n-1}\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{\infty}^{n-1}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{1-\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{\infty}}}

and the proof follows by combining the two last inequalities.
∎

8.
Preliminary Inequalities

In this section, we provide estimates which are crucial for controlling the growth of the particle number operator with respect to the fluctuation dynamics in the subsequent sections.

As a preliminary, let us begin by defining some convenient notations.
For any pair
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\in\set{l,r}^{2}
and a bounded operator
O:\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma}
, we generalize the standard notation of the second quantization of the one-particle operator by

(144a)

\displaystyle\dG_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(O)=

\displaystyle\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}O(x,y)\,a^{*}_{x,\sigma}\,a_{y,\sigma^{\prime}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\,

(144b)

\displaystyle\dG_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}^{+}(O)=

\displaystyle\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}O(x,y)\,a^{*}_{x,\sigma}\,a^{*}_{y,\sigma^{\prime}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\,

(144c)

\displaystyle\dG_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}^{-}(O)=

\displaystyle\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}O(x,y)\,a_{x,\sigma}\,a_{y,\sigma^{\prime}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\

where the operators are expressed in terms of operator-valued distributions (
44
).
When
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
, we write
\dG^{\circ}_{\sigma}:=\dG^{\circ}_{\sigma,\sigma}
where
\circ
denotes either
+
,
-
, or null. Moreover, we have the relations

(145)

\dG_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(O)^{*}=\dG_{\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}(O^{*})\quad\text{ and }\quad\dG^{+}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(O)^{*}=\dG^{-}_{\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}(O^{*}).

We begin by extending
[
11
, Lemma 4.2]
to the case of Schatten class operators between different Hilbert spaces. See
[
76
, Chapter 7]
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
(\sigma^{\prime},\sigma)\in\set{l,r}^{2}
and
O:\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma}
be a compact operator. Then, for every
p\in[1,\infty]
, we have the estimate

(146)

\left\lVert\dG_{\sigma}(O)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p}\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

for every
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}
where
\mathcal{N}=\dG_{l}\!\left(1\right)+\dG_{r}\!\left(1\right)
. Moreover, for
p\in[1,2]
we have the estimates

(147a)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\dG^{-}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(O)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p}\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}},

(147b)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\dG^{+}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(O)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

for every
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

The case of inequality (
146
) with
p=\infty
and the case of inequalities (
147a
) and (
147b
) with
p=2
are proved in
[
11
, Lemma 4.2]
.

For any compact
O
, we can write down a singular value decomposition of
O
, that is,
O=\sum_{j}\mu_{j}\left\langle\phi_{j},\cdot\right\rangle\varphi_{j}
where
(\phi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
and
(\varphi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{\sigma}
are two orthonormal sets, and
\mu_{j}\geq 0
are the singular values of
O
(see e.g.
[
76
, Theorem 7.6]
). Thus, using the notation
a^{\sharp}
to denote either
a
or
a^{*}
, we have

\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}O(x,y)\,a^{\sharp}_{x,\sigma}\,a^{\sharp}_{y,\sigma^{\prime}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\sum_{j}\mu_{j}\left\lVert a^{\sharp}_{\sigma}(\tilde{\phi}_{j})\,a^{\sharp}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\tilde{\varphi}_{j})\right\rVert_{\infty}

where
\tilde{\phi}_{j}
is either
\phi_{j}
or
\bar{\phi}_{j}
. Since
\left\lVert a^{\sharp}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{2}}=1
, we obtain the estimate

\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}O(x,y)\,a^{\sharp}_{x,\sigma}\,a^{\sharp}_{y,\sigma^{\prime}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\ \right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\sum_{j}\mu_{j}=\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{1}.

Hence, for any
\circ\in\set{+,-,\ }
, we have the estimate
\left\lVert\dG^{\circ}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(O)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{1}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}
. Finally, we deduce the desired result by weighted interpolation.
∎

As an immediate application, we can bound the expectation values of the operators (
144
) in terms of the expectation values of powers of the number operator.

Lemma 8.2
.

For any
p\in[1,\infty]
, we have the estimate

(148)

\Braket{\Psi|\dG_{\sigma}(O)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p}\Braket{\Psi|\mathcal{N}^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

for every
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}
. Similarly, for any
p\in[1,2]
, we have the estimates

(149a)

\displaystyle\Braket{\Psi|\dG^{+}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(O)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p}\Braket{\Psi|\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

(149b)

\displaystyle\Braket{\Psi|\dG^{-}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(O)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p}\Braket{\Psi|\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

for every
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

For
\epsilon>0
, one has the equality

\displaystyle\Braket{\Psi|\dG\!\left(O\right)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\Braket{\left(\mathcal{N}+\epsilon\right)^{\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\Psi|\left(\mathcal{N}+\epsilon\right)^{-\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\dG\!\left(O\right)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\Braket{\left(\mathcal{N}+\epsilon\right)^{\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\Psi|\dG\!\left(O\right)\left(\mathcal{N}+\epsilon\right)^{-\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}.

Applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and Lemma
8.1
yields

\displaystyle\Braket{\Psi|\dG\!\left(O\right)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+\epsilon\right)^{\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\left(\mathcal{N}+\epsilon\right)^{-\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+\epsilon\right)^{\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Then inequality (
148
) follows by passing to the limit
\epsilon\to 0
. With a similar argument and the observation that for any nice function
g
,
g(\mathcal{N})\,a^{*}=a^{*}\,g(\mathcal{N}+1)
, we obtain

\Braket{\Psi|\dG^{+}_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(O)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p}\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2\right)^{\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

from which we deduce inequality (
149a
). Inequality (
149b
) follows immediately from Equation (
145
).
∎

9.
Quantum Fluctuations and the Mean-Field Limit

In this section, we prove how the error of the mean-field approximation of the fermionic system can be controlled by the mean number of particles of the fluctuation dynamics about a quasi-free state. To this end, it suffices for us to specialize our study to the state vector

(150)

\Psi_{\text{fluc}}=\mathsf{R}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\Phi_{t}=\mathsf{R}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}e^{-i(t/\hbar)\mathsf{L}_{N}}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}_{0}}\Psi^{\mathrm{in}}

and consider its mean number of particles

\Braket{\Psi_{\text{fluc}}|\mathcal{N}\Psi_{\text{fluc}}}_{\mathcal{G}}=\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Psi_{\text{fluc}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}.

More specifically, we control the error of the mean-field approximation by the norm

(151)

\left\lVert\Psi_{\text{fluc}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}:=\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k}\Psi_{\text{fluc}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

for
k>0
, which allows us to handle additional small error terms. For the rest of this section, we drop the subscript of the fluctuation vector and the dependence on time to reduce cumbersome notations.

One can see that quantity (
151
) controls the difference of the one-particle density operators in the sense of the following proposition.

Proposition 9.1
.

Define
\Psi
and
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}
as in the Theorem
4.1
. Then, for any
p\in[1,\infty]
, we have the estimate

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C_{p}}{\min(N^{\frac{1}{2}},N\,h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}})}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{2p}}}^{2}

where
C_{p}=2^{2+\frac{1}{2p}}
if
p\geq 2
and
C_{p}=2+2^{\frac{5}{4}}\mathcal{C}_{\frac{p}{2-p}}^{\frac{1}{2}}
if
p<2
.

Proof.

Following the proof of
[
14
, Proof of Theorem 2.1]
, we have

\displaystyle Nh^{3}\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}(x,y)-\omega(x,y)

\displaystyle=\Braket{\Psi_{N}|a_{y,l}^{*}\,a_{x,l}\Psi_{N}}_{\mathcal{G}}=\Braket{\Psi|\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\,a_{y,l}^{*}\,a_{x,l}\,\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\langle\Psi\,|\,\big(a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{x})-a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v}_{x})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-a_{r}(\overline{v}_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{x})-a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v}_{x})\,a_{r}(\overline{v}_{y})\big)\Psi\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}.

Since
\boldsymbol{\rho}=\frac{1}{Nh^{3}}\omega
, we deduce

(152)

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\!(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Nh^{3}}\langle\Psi\,|\,\big(a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{x})-a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v}_{x})

\displaystyle-a_{r}(\overline{v}_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{x})-a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v}_{x})\,a_{r}(\overline{v}_{y})\big)\Psi\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}.

In particular, pairing operator (
152
) with an observable
O
yields

\tr\!\left(O\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\right)\\
=\frac{1}{N\,h^{3}}\Braket{\Psi|\left(\dG_{l}\!\left(u\,O\,u\right)-\dG_{r}\!\left(\overline{v}\,\overline{O}^{*}v\right)-\dG^{+}_{l,r}(v\,O\,u)-\dG^{-}_{r,l}(v\,O\,u)\right)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}.

In the case
p\in[2,\infty]
, we apply the fact that
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1,\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
, and Lemma
8.2
to deduce the estimate

\tr\!\left(O\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\right)\leq\frac{2^{2+\frac{1}{2p}}}{N\,h^{3}}\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p^{\prime}}\Braket{\Psi|\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}.

Then, by duality and the fact that
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}=h^{\frac{3}{p}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\rVert_{p}
, we obtain the result when
p\geq 2
.

For
p\in[1,2]
, we can bound the terms with
\dG_{l}\!\left(u\,O\,u\right)
and
\dG_{r}\!\left(\overline{v}\,\overline{O}^{*}v\right)
as in the previous case. For the other two terms, we begin by applying Hölder’s inequality to get
\left\lVert v\,O\,u\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{r}\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p^{\prime}}
where
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}
. Then, by Lemma
8.2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Braket{\Psi|\left(\dG^{+}_{l,r}(v\,O\,u)+\dG^{-}_{r,l}(v\,O\,u)\right)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=2\left\lvert\Braket{\Psi|\dG^{-}_{r,l}(v\,O\,u)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{5}{4}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{r}\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p^{\prime}}\Braket{\Psi|\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}.

Since
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{r}=\mathcal{C}_{\frac{r}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{\frac{1}{2}}h^{3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{r}\right)}
and
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}
, this implies

\left\lvert\Braket{\Psi|\left(\dG^{+}_{l,r}(v\,O\,u)+\dG^{-}_{r,l}(v\,O\,u)\right)\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}\right\rvert\leq 2^{\frac{5}{4}}\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}_{\frac{r}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{\frac{1}{2}}h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}\Braket{\Psi|\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}.

So, we have the estimate

\tr\!\left(O\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)\right)\leq\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{p^{\prime}}\left(\frac{2}{N\,h^{3}}+\frac{2^{\frac{5}{4}}\mathcal{C}_{\frac{r}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h^{\frac{3}{p}}}\right)\Braket{\Psi|\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

which yields the desired result.
∎

To better understand what it means to have a small number of particles after having performed the Bogoliubov transformation, it is useful to see how this latter acts on the number operator. From the definition (
72
), we obtain the following formula for
\sigma\in\set{r,l}

(153)

\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}=\mathsf{A}_{\sigma}+\mathsf{C}+\mathsf{C}^{*},

where

\mathsf{A}_{\sigma}=\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}+N-\dG\!\left(\omega\oplus\overline{\omega}\right),\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathsf{C}=\dG^{+}_{r,l}\!\left(uv\right).

Since changing
v
by
-v
changes
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}
, we deduce similarly that

(154)

\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\,\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\mathsf{A}_{\sigma}-\mathsf{C}-\mathsf{C}^{*}.

From these formulas, we deduce the following interesting fact: the operator
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
acting on the single Fock space
\cF
and corresponding to the Bogoliubov transform of the vacuum in
\mathcal{G}
commutes with the number of particles operator .

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\Omega)
. Then

\left[{\mathcal{N},\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]=0.

This also implies that
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rvert^{2}
commutes with
\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

Let
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\Omega=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
. Then we obtain
\mathcal{N}_{l}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{N}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})
and
\mathcal{N}_{r}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathcal{N})
, therefore

\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}\left[{\mathcal{N},\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right]=\left(\mathcal{N}_{l}-\mathcal{N}_{r}\right)\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\left(\mathcal{N}_{l}-\mathcal{N}_{r}\right)\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\Omega.

Now we use Formula (
154
), yielding

\left(\mathcal{N}_{l}-\mathcal{N}_{r}\right)\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\Omega=\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\left(\mathsf{A}_{l}-\mathsf{A}_{r}\right)\,\Omega=\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{l}-\mathcal{N}_{r}\right)\,\Omega=0,

which proves the result.
∎

Since the number operator on the double Fock space
\mathcal{G}
is given by
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{l}+\mathcal{N}_{r}
, Equations (
153
) and (
154
) imply

(155)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\mathcal{N}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{A}+2\mathsf{C}+2\mathsf{C}^{*}

(156)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\,\mathcal{N}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{A}-2\mathsf{C}-2\mathsf{C}^{*}

with
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}_{l}+\mathsf{A}_{r}=\mathcal{N}+2N-2\dG\!\left(\omega\oplus\overline{\omega}\right)
. This allows us to prove the following bounds.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for any
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}_{k}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq 3^{k}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2N+2k\right)^{k}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq 3^{k}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2N+2k\right)^{k}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Remark 9.1
.

With a similar proof, one obtains

\left\lVert\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1/2}}\leq 3^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2N\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

and so by interpolation, for any
s\in[0,1/2]
,

(157)

\left\lVert\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{s}}\leq 3^{s}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2N\right)^{s}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Proof of Lemma
9.2
.

Since
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\mathcal{N}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
and
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathcal{N}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}
are two positive operators, by doing the sum of the two above identities, we deduce that
\mathsf{A}
is also a positive operator. Therefore, since
\dG\!\left(\omega\oplus\overline{\omega}\right)
is a positive operator, from the definition of
\mathsf{A}
, we obtain

0\leq\mathsf{A}\leq\mathcal{N}+2N,

which implies that for any
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
,
\left\lVert\mathsf{A}^{1/2}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2N\right)^{1/2}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}
. Since
\mathsf{A}
commutes with
\mathcal{N}+2N
, we deduce that

\left\lVert\mathsf{A}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2N\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

On the other hand, by Inequalities (
147a
) and (
147b
) and the fact that
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}=N^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we have

\left\lVert\mathsf{C}^{*}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert uv\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2\right)^{1/2}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+N+2\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

and similarly,
\left\lVert\mathsf{C}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}
. From these inequalities, using the fact that
\mathsf{A}
commutes with
\mathcal{N}
and the fact that
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{C}\left(\mathcal{N}-2\right)
and
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{C}^{*}=\mathsf{C}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+2\right)
, we deduce that for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, by defining
c_{j}:=2N+2j
, we have

\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+c_{j}\right)^{j}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathcal{N}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\\
\leq\left\lVert\mathsf{A}\left(\mathcal{N}+c_{j}\right)^{j}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}+2\left\lVert\mathsf{C}\left(\mathcal{N}+c_{j}-2\right)^{j}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}+2\left\lVert\mathsf{C}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+c_{j}+2\right)^{j}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\\
\leq 3\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+c_{j+1}\right)^{j+1}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

By induction, this implies that for any
(j,k)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}

\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+c_{j}\right)^{j}\left(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathcal{N}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\right)^{k}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq 3^{k}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+c_{j+k}\right)^{j+k}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Taking
j=0
and using the fact that
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
is unitary
\left(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathcal{N}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\right)^{k}=\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathcal{N}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}
, we get

\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}=\left\lVert\left(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\mathcal{N}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\right)^{k}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq 3^{k}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+c_{k}\right)^{k}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

The case of
\mathcal{N}^{k}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
can be handled in the same way.
∎

10.
The Fluctuation Dynamics

With the scaling provided in (
143
), we have that
\rho(x)=N^{-1}\,\omega(x,x)
. Let us define
X_{\omega}(x,y):=N^{-1}\,K(x-y)\,\omega(x,y)
, then this gives us the relation
X_{\omega}=h^{3}\,\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
. Thus, the Hartree–Fock equation (
5
) can be rewritten as follows

(158)

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\omega=\left[{H_{\omega},\omega}\right]\ \text{ with }\ H_{\omega}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\Delta+K*\rho-X_{\omega}.

By
[
11
, Proposition 3.1]
, we know that the dynamics of
\Psi_{\text{fluc}}
satisfies

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\mathsf{U}_{t,s}=\mathsf{G}_{t}\,\mathsf{U}_{t,s}\ \text{ with }\ \mathsf{U}_{s,s}=1\ \text{ for all }s\in\mathbb{R}

and the generator
\mathsf{G}_{t}
is given by

(159)

\mathsf{G}=\dG_{l}\!\left(H_{\omega}\right)-\dG_{r}\!\left(\overline{H_{\omega}}\right)+\mathsf{D}+\mathsf{Q}+\mathsf{Q}^{*}+\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}+\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\,N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K(x-y)\bigg(a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{x})-a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{y}})\,a_{r}(\overline{u_{y}})\,a_{r}(\overline{u_{x}})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt+2\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(v_{x})\,a_{r}(v_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{y})-2\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{y}})\,a_{r}(\overline{v_{y}})\,a_{l}(u_{x})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt+2\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{y})\,a_{l}(v_{y})\,a_{r}(\overline{u_{x}})-2\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\,a_{l}(v_{y})\,a_{r}(\overline{u_{y}})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt+a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{y}})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\,a_{r}(\overline{v_{x}})\,a_{r}(\overline{v_{y}})-a_{l}^{*}(v_{y})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\,a_{l}(v_{x})\,a_{l}(v_{y})\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K(x-y)\,\bigg(a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\,a_{l}(u_{y})-a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{y})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\,a_{l}(v_{y})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt+a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{y}})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\,a_{r}(\overline{u_{y}})-a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{y}})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\,a_{r}(\overline{v_{y}})\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\,N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K(x-y)\bigg(a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{y}})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})-a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{y}})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{y})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

with
u_{x}(y):=u(y,x)
and
v_{x}(y):=v(y,x)
.
\mathsf{D}
contains quartic terms that commute with
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{l}+\mathcal{N}_{r}
, whereas
\mathsf{Q}^{*}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
contain quartic terms that do not commute with
\mathcal{N}
.

10.1.
Bounds on the Fluctuation Dynamics

In this section, we use the uniform in
\hbar
regularity of the solution of the Hartree–Fock equation to estimate the growth of mean number of particles for the fluctuation dynamics.

We fix
p\in[1,2]
with

(160)

p<\mathfrak{b}=\frac{3}{a+1}

and take
1\leq q_{0}<q_{1}\leq\infty
such that

(161)

\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{0}}\right)=\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}.

We choose
T>0
so that the following two quantities are uniformly bounded on
[0,T]
:

(162a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}

\displaystyle:=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q_{0}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q_{1}}}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(162b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathcal{D}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{D}_{q_{1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

with
\mathcal{D}_{q}
defined in Lemma
7.1
and
m=1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n}
with
n>a+1
. The main result of this section is the following inequality.

Proposition 10.1
.

Let
(k_{0},k)\in[0,1/2]\times\mathbb{N}
. Then, for any
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}
and
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}_{t,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}\leq C_{M}\,e^{C_{M}\,\lambda_{\alpha}\,t}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}k}}+\frac{h^{\left(\alpha-1\right)k}}{N^{\frac{k}{2}-k_{0}}}\,t\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k}}\right)

where
\alpha:=\frac{3}{p}-\frac{3}{2}
,
C_{M}=C^{k+k_{0}}\left(1+N^{-\frac{1}{2}}h^{-1}\right)
for some constant
C>0
, and

(163)

\lambda_{\alpha}=C_{p,a,q_{0}}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert h^{-\alpha}\left(1+\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\sup_{[0,T]}\Big(\left\lVert\rho(t)\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}},\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}(t),\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}(t)\Big)

with
p_{a}=\frac{3}{3-2a}
.

Remark 10.1
.

With the cut-off given in Remark
4.2
, one obtains

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}_{t,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}\leq C_{M}\,e^{C_{M}\,\lambda_{R}\,t}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}k}}+\frac{R^{3\alpha k}\,t}{N^{\frac{k}{2}-k_{0}}h^{k}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k}}\right)

with

\lambda_{R}=C_{p,a,q_{0}}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert R^{-3\alpha}\left(1+\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\sup_{[0,T]}(\left\lVert\rho(t)\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}},\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}(t),\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}(t))\,.

To prove Proposition
10.1
, we will first obtain uniform in
\hbar
estimates for the generator (
159
). This is done by proving a series of lemmas. In particular, we will estimate each of the terms of the generator that do not commute with
\mathcal{N}
separately.

10.1.1.
Bounds for
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}

For convenience, let us begin by recalling the following lemma.

Lemma 10.1

(Proposition 4.3 of
[
49
]
)
.

Let
a\in(-1,\tfrac{3}{2})
,
p\in[1,\mathfrak{b})
, and
q_{0},q_{1}
satisfying (
161
). Then, for
n>a+1
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that the estimate

\left\lVert[K_{x},\boldsymbol{\rho}]\right\rVert_{p}\leq C\,h^{1-\frac{3}{\mathfrak{b}}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m\right\rVert_{q_{0}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m\right\rVert_{q_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

holds. Here,
K_{x}
denotes the multiplication operator
K_{x}(y):=K(x-y)
.

Then we have the following result.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
a\in(-1,\tfrac{3}{2})
and
p\in[1,2]
satisfying
p<\mathfrak{b}
. Then, for any
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})\in\mathcal{G}^{2}
, the following inequality holds

(164)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert\left(\tilde{C}_{1}\,h^{3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)}+\tilde{C}_{2}\,N^{\frac{1}{2}}h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}\right)\left\lVert\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\lVert\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}},

where
\tilde{C}_{1}=C\,\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}
and
\tilde{C}_{2}=C\left(Nh^{3}\mathcal{C}_{\infty}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}
for some constant
C>0
depending only on
a,p
and
q_{0}
.

Proof.

Recall the definition of
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
given in Formula (
159
). By the anti-commutation relations (
40
), the products of creation operators in
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
can be written as follows

\displaystyle a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{y}})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})

\displaystyle=a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{y}})

\displaystyle a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{y}})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{y})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})

\displaystyle=a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{y})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{y}}).

Moreover, using the notation (
144
) and the notation
K_{x}(y)=K(x-y)
, we have
\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,K_{x}v\right):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}K(x-y)\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{y}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y
. Therefore, we can rewrite
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
as

(165)

\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}=\frac{1}{2\,N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,K_{x}v\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\delta_{x}v\right)-\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\delta_{x}u\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,K_{x}v\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

Here,
u\,\delta_{x}v
denotes the operator with integral kernel
(u\,\delta_{x}v)(y,z)=u(y,x)\,v(x,z)
.

As in
[
11
, Proof of Proposition 4.3]
, we need to exploit the hidden commutator structure in (
165
) to handle the
\hbar^{-1}
on the left-hand side of inequality (
164
). We begin by using the fact that
u
commutes with
v
to deduce the identity

(166)

u\,K_{x}v=v\,K_{x}u+u\left[{K_{x},v}\right]-v\left[{K_{x},u}\right]=:v\,K_{x}u+c_{x},

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Moreover, the symmetry of
K
allows us to write

(167)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,K_{x}u\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\delta_{x}v\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\delta_{x}u\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,K_{x}v\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

By identities (
166
)–(
167
), we make appear more explicitly the commutator structure

(
165
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,K_{x}u+c_{x}\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\delta_{x}v\right)-\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\delta_{x}u\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,K_{x}v-c_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(c_{x}\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\delta_{x}v\right)+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\delta_{x}u\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(c_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

Again, using the fact that the creation operators anti-commute, we obtain

\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}=\frac{1}{2\,N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})+a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\left[{K_{x},v}\right]-v\left[{K_{x},u}\right]\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

Expanding the product inside the integral gives four terms. We define
\tilde{J}_{1}
and
\tilde{J}_{2}
as the terms with
\left[{K_{x},v}\right]
, and
\tilde{J}_{3}
and
\tilde{J}_{4}
the terms with
\left[{K_{x},u}\right]
. Let us look at
\tilde{J}_{1}
. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we obtain the following bound

\displaystyle\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\tilde{J}_{1}\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Braket{a_{l}(u_{x})\,\Psi_{1}|a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\,\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\right)\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lVert a_{l}(u_{x})\,\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lVert a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\,\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\right)\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The first factor can be written

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lVert a_{l}(u_{x})\,\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{l}(u_{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\Psi_{1}}_{\mathcal{G}}=\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\dG_{l}\!\left(1-\omega\right)\Psi_{1}}_{\mathcal{G}},

which is smaller than
\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\mathcal{N}_{l}\,\Psi_{1}}_{\mathcal{G}}
. To estimate the second factor, we use the fact that

\left\lVert a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}=\left\lVert v_{x}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=N\rho(x)

together with Lemma
8.1
and the fact that
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
to get

\left\lVert a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\right)\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left(N\rho(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\right\rVert_{p}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Combining the above inequalities leads to

\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\tilde{J}_{1}\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}\leq N^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lVert\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\right\rVert_{p}^{2}\rho(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\mathcal{N}\Psi_{1}}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Applying Lemma
10.1
, since
p<\mathfrak{b}
, and the scaling relation (
161
), we get that

(168)

\left\lVert\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\right\rVert_{p}\leq C\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert N^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h^{1+3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}.

Therefore, we finally obtain the inequality

\frac{1}{N\hbar}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\tilde{J}_{1}\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C^{\prime}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}\,h^{3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)}\left\lVert\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\lVert\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}}.

The term
\tilde{J}_{2}
is treated similarly leading to the same bound.

The terms
\tilde{J}_{3}
and
\tilde{J}_{4}
can also be treated in a similar manner. Except in this case, we apply Lemma
7.1
and the fact that
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}=\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}(Nh^{3})^{\frac{1}{2}}
to get

(169)

\left\lVert v\left[{K_{x},u}\right]\right\rVert_{p}\leq C\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert N^{\frac{3}{2}}\,h^{1+3\left(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)}\,\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}.

So, we obtained the claimed bound for
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
.
∎

Remark 10.2
.

In the case of the cut-off potential described in Remark
4.2
, we can take
q_{0}=q_{1}=\infty
and
p=2
in the above inequality, with an extra factor
R^{3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}
, leading to

(170)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert R^{3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left(\tilde{C}_{1}+\tilde{C}_{2}\,N^{\frac{1}{2}}h^{\frac{3}{2}}\right)\left\lVert\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1/2}}\left\lVert\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1/2}}.

More precisely, Inequality (
170
) is a direct consequence of the following estimate

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\left[{K_{R,x},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert R^{3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{\infty}}

which follows directly from the proof of Proposition 4.3 in
[
49
]
.

10.1.2.
Bounds for
\mathsf{Q}^{*}

We label the terms of
\mathsf{Q}^{*}
given in (
159
) by

(171)

\mathsf{Q}^{*}=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}.

Using the fact that the creation operators anti-commute, we get

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K(x-y)\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K(x-y)\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{y})\,a_{l}(v_{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

I_{3}
and
I_{4}
have similar forms with the "
l
" and "
r
" labels interchanged and
(u,v)
replaced by
(\overline{u},\overline{v})
. To reveal hidden commutator structures, which are necessary when estimating
\mathsf{Q}^{*}
uniformly in
\hbar
, we need to further decompose (
171
).

Let us start with the following decomposition lemma.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
\mathsf{Q}^{*}
be as in (
171
). Then, we have the decomposition

I_{1}+I_{2}=J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{12}+I_{12}

where

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}a_{r}^{*}(u_{x})\,a_{l}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\dG_{l}\!\left(u\left[{u,K_{x}}\right]\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\overline{u_{x}})\dG_{l}\!\left(v\left[{v,K_{x}}\right]\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle J_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{u,K_{x}}\right]v+\left[{K_{x},v}\right]u\right)a_{l}^{*}(\omega_{x})\,a_{x,l}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,

and

I_{12}=-\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}a^{*}_{l}(u_{x})\,a^{*}_{r}(\overline{v_{x}})\dG_{l}\!\left(K_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

We have the same splitting for
I_{3}+I_{4}
, interchanging "l" by "r" and replacing
(u,v)
by
(\overline{u},\overline{v})
. Hence, we obtain a decomposition of the form

(172)

\mathsf{Q}^{*}=\left(J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}+J_{12}+J_{34}\right)+(I_{12}+I_{34})=:\tilde{\mathsf{P}}^{*}+\mathsf{P}^{*}.

Proof.

To simplify our computations, we use the Fefferman–de la Llave formula (cf.
[
31
,
41
]
) in its smooth version. For the potential
K
it reads

K(x-y)=\kappa_{a}\int^{\infty}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a+1}{2}}\varphi_{s,z}(x)\,\varphi_{s,z}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

where
\varphi_{s,z}(x)=\varphi_{s}(x-z)=e^{-\pi\left\lvert x-z\right\rvert^{2}s}
and
\kappa_{a}=2^{\frac{3-a}{2}}\frac{\pi^{a/2}}{\Gamma(a/2)}\,\kappa
.
This allows us to rewrite
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
in the following forms

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a+1}{2}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\varphi_{s,z}\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(u\,\varphi_{s,z}\,u\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a+1}{2}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\varphi_{s,z}\,u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(v\,\varphi_{s,z}\,v\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

where
\varphi_{s,z}
is seen as a multiplication operator. Since
u^{2}=1-\omega
, then we have the identity

\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\varphi\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(u\,\varphi\,u\right)=\ \dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\varphi\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(u\left[{\varphi,u}\right]\right)+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{u,\varphi}\right]v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\left(1-\omega\right)\varphi\right)\\
+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\varphi\,u\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\left(1-\omega\right)\varphi\right)

where we used the notation
\varphi=\varphi_{s,z}
. Similarly, since
v^{2}=\omega
, then we have

\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\varphi\,u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(v\,\varphi\,v\right)=\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\varphi\,u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(v\left[{\varphi,v}\right]\right)+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{v,\varphi}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega\,\varphi\right)\\
+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\varphi\,u\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega\,\varphi\right).

Combining the two identities yields

(173)

\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\varphi\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(u\,\varphi\,u\right)+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\varphi\,u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(v\,\varphi\,v\right)\\
=\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\varphi\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(u\left[{\varphi,u}\right]\right)+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\varphi\,u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(v\left[{\varphi,v}\right]\right)\\
+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{\varphi,u}\right]v+\left[{v,\varphi}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega\,\varphi\right)+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\varphi\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\varphi\right).

Thus, using identity (
173
), we can write
I_{1}+I_{2}=J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{12}+I_{12}
with

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\kappa_{a}}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a+1}{2}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\varphi\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(u\left[{u,\varphi}\right]\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\kappa_{a}}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a+1}{2}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(v\,\varphi\,u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(v\left[{v,\varphi}\right]\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle J_{12}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\kappa_{a}}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a+1}{2}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{u,\varphi}\right]v+\left[{\varphi,v}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega\,\varphi\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

and

I_{12}:=-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a+1}{2}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\varphi\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\varphi\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Reversing the Fefferman–de la Llave expansion gives us

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{a}}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a+1}{2}}a^{*}_{l}(u_{x})\,a^{*}_{r}(\overline{v_{x}})\,\varphi(x)\dG_{l}\!\left(u\left[{u,\varphi}\right]\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}a^{*}_{l}(u_{x})\,a^{*}_{r}(\overline{v_{x}})\dG_{l}\!\left(u\left[{u,K_{x}}\right]\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

The same is true for
J_{2}
. Lastly, we have that

\displaystyle J_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{a}}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a+1}{2}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{u,\varphi}\right]v+\left[{\varphi,v}\right]u\right)a_{l}^{*}(\omega_{x})\,\varphi(x)\,a_{x,l}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{u,K_{x}}\right]v+\left[{K_{x},v}\right]u\right)a_{l}^{*}(\omega_{x})\,a_{x,l}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Let us first estimate the
J
terms, which can be treated in a similar manner as in the
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
case. One obtains the following bounds.

Lemma 10.4
.

Assuming the same hypotheses as in Lemma
10.2
. Then, for any
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})\in\mathcal{G}^{2}
, we have the estimates

(174a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|J_{1}\,\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert N^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\lVert\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}}

(174b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|J_{2}\,\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert N^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\lVert\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}}

(174c)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|J_{12}\,\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{12}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert N^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}\,\left\lVert\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\lVert\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}},

where
C_{1}=C\,\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}
,
C_{2}=C\,\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}
, and
C_{12}=C\,\mathcal{C}_{2}\left(\left(Nh^{3}\mathcal{C}_{\infty}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}+\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}\right)
for some constant
C
depending only on
p
and
a
. The same inequalities hold respectively for
J_{3}
,
J_{4}
,
J_{34}
.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
10.1
and the fact that
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
gives us the estimate

\left\lVert u\left[{K_{x},u}\right]\right\rVert_{p}\leq C\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert N\,h^{1+\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}.

Then following the proof of Lemma
10.2
yields inequality (
174a
). Similarly,
by Lemma
10.1
and the fact that
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left(\mathcal{C}_{\infty}\,N\,h^{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we have that

\left\lVert v\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\right\rVert_{p}\leq C\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert N\,h^{1+\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}\,\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}

from which we arrive at inequality (
174b
). Finally, by direct estimation, we see that

\Braket{\Psi_{1}|J_{12}\,\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{1}{N}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lVert a_{l}(\omega_{x})\,\dG^{+}_{r,l}\!\left(v\left[{K_{x},u}\right]-u\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\mathcal{N}_{l}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Then, Inequality (
174c
) follows from Lemma
8.1
and Lemma
10.1
.
∎

Lastly, let us estimate
\mathsf{P}^{*}=I_{12}+I_{34}
.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
p_{a}=\frac{3}{3-2a}
. Then, there exists
C>0
, depending only on
a
, such that for any
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})\in\mathcal{G}^{2}
we have the estimate

\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lvert\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\mathsf{P}^{*}\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{C\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}h}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\mathcal{N}\Psi_{1}}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Braket{\Psi_{2}|\mathcal{N}\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

Let
(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})\in\mathcal{G}^{2}
. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the boundedness of
a^{*}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Braket{\Psi_{1}|I_{12}\Psi_{2}}_{\mathcal{G}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lVert a_{l}(u_{x})\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lVert a_{r}^{*}(\overline{v_{x}})\dG_{l}\!\left(K_{x}\right)\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\mathcal{N}_{l}\Psi_{1}}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho(x)\left\lVert\dG_{l}\!\left(K_{x}\right)\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

where we used
\left\lVert v_{x}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=N\rho(x)
. Moreover, using the fact that

(\dG_{l}\!\left(K_{x}\right)\Psi)^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m})=\kappa\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m})}{\left\lvert x-x_{j}\right\rvert^{a}},

where
\underline{x}_{n}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
,
\underline{y}_{m}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
,
it follows

\left\lVert(\dG_{l}\!\left(K_{x}\right)\Psi)^{(n,m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3(n+m)})}^{2}\leq\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert^{2}n^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{g(y)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{2a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

where we defined
g(x)=\left\lVert\Psi^{(n,m)}(x,\underline{x}_{n-1},\underline{y}_{m})\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{m})}^{2}
. Finally, by the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho(x)\left\lVert(\dG_{l}\!\left(K_{x}\right)\Psi)^{(n,m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3(n+m)})}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert^{2}n^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\frac{\rho(x)\,g(y)}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{2a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq C_{p_{a},a}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert^{2}n^{2}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

where
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{1}}=\left\lVert\Psi^{(n,m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. This yields the desired estimate. The proof for the estimate on
I_{34}
is the same.
∎

10.2.
Proof of Proposition
10.1

To control the growth of the fluctuation dynamics in the
\mathcal{G}_{k}
norms, the strategy consists of splitting the generator
\mathsf{G}
, defined in (
159
), into two parts then solving the problem perturbatively. More precisely, we define the splitting
\mathsf{G}=\tilde{\mathsf{G}}+\mathsf{B}
with

(175a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{G}}

\displaystyle=\dG_{l}\!\left(H_{\omega}\right)-\dG_{r}\!\left(\overline{H_{\omega}}\right)+\mathsf{D}+\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}+\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}+\tilde{\mathsf{P}}+\tilde{\mathsf{P}}^{*}

(175b)

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}+\mathsf{P}^{*}

where we recall
\mathsf{P}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{P}}
are defined by Formula (
172
). The idea is to view
\mathsf{G}
as a small perturbation of
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}
. This view is justifiable since, when
N^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h
is large, the effect of the operator
\mathsf{B}
is small in the following sense.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
2\,j\in\mathbb{N}
and
p_{a}=\frac{3}{3-2a}
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
a
such that

(176)
