\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\mathsf{B}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{j+\frac{3}{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{j}}\leq C\,\frac{2^{j}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}h}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
10.5
. Notice that
\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k}\mathsf{P}^{*}=\mathsf{P}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+3\right)^{k}
, then, by Lemma
10.5
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{P}^{*}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\left\lVert\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq 1}\Braket{\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Psi_{1}|\mathsf{P}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+3\right)^{j+\frac{1}{2}}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{a}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+3\right)^{j+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{N}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{a}\frac{2^{j}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{j+\frac{3}{2}}}.

The estimate for
\mathsf{P}
also follows immediately from Lemma
10.5
, that is,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{P}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\left\lVert\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq 1}\Braket{\mathsf{P}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{-1}\Psi_{1}|\left(\mathcal{N}-1\right)^{j+1}\Psi}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{a}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert(\mathcal{N}-1)^{j+1}\mathcal{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{C_{a}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{j+\frac{3}{2}}}.

This completes the argument.
∎

In light of the above lemma, we define the auxiliary dynamics
\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}
to be the unitary dynamics generated by (
175a
), that is, for any
(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}
satisfies the differential equation

(177)

i\hbar\,\partial_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}\Psi=\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}\Psi\ \text{ with }\ \tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{s,s}\Psi=\Psi

for
\Psi
sufficiently smooth. The existence of
\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}
is proven in Appendix
A
. Let us begin by showing the auxiliary dynamics propagates the
\mathcal{G}_{k}
norm under regularity assumptions on the solution of the Hartree equation.

Proposition 10.2
.

Let
a\in(-1,\tfrac{3}{2})
,
p\in[1,2]
satisfying
p<\mathfrak{b}=\frac{3}{a+1}
, and
\Psi_{t}=\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}\Psi
is a solution to (
177
). Then, for any
k
such that
2k\in\mathbb{N}
, the inequality

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\Psi_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}\leq C_{k}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}\,h^{3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)}+C_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,N^{\frac{1}{2}}h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}\right)\left\lVert\Psi_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)}}

holds for some constants
C_{k}
of the form
C_{k}=C_{p,a,q_{0}}\,C^{k}
and

C_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\left(1+\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}+\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}\right)

where
\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}
are defined by formulas (
162a
) and (
162b
).

Remark 10.3
.

Since
N^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h^{\frac{3}{2}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, then, by Grönwall’s inequality, we deduce that

(178)

\left\lVert\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{k}}\leq e^{C_{t,s}}

where
C_{t,s}=C_{k}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert h^{-\alpha}\left(1+\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}(\tau)+\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}(\tau)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tau
with
\alpha:=\frac{3}{p}-\frac{3}{2}\geq 0
and is
0
if and only if
p=2
.

Proof.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Using the fact that
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}=\tilde{\mathsf{G}}^{*}
, we get

\displaystyle i\hbar\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\Psi_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{k}{2}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Braket{\Psi_{t}|\left[{\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k},\tilde{\mathsf{G}}}\right]\Psi_{t}}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Braket{\Psi_{t}|\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{j-1}\left[{\mathcal{N},\tilde{\mathsf{G}}}\right]\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k-j}\Psi_{t}}_{\mathcal{G}}.

Note that the only terms in
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}
that do not commute with
\mathcal{N}
are
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}
,
\tilde{\mathsf{P}}
, and their adjoints. Since
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}a^{*}_{\sigma}=a^{*}_{\sigma}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\sigma}+1\right)
for
\sigma\in\{r,l\}
, we obtain

\left[{\mathcal{N},\tilde{\mathsf{G}}}\right]=\left[{\mathcal{N},\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}+\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}+\tilde{\mathsf{P}}^{*}+\tilde{\mathsf{P}}}\right]=4\left(\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}-\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}\right)+\tilde{\mathsf{P}}^{*}-\tilde{\mathsf{P}},

which leads to

(179)

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\Psi_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{k}{2}}}^{2}=\frac{2}{\hbar}\im\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Braket{\Psi_{t}|\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{j-1}\left(4\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}+\tilde{\mathsf{P}}^{*}\right)\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k-j}\Psi_{t}}_{\mathcal{G}}\right).

Using again the commutation relation between the number operator and the creation operator, we can balance the power of the number operators appearing on the left and on the right of
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
. More precisely, if
j>\frac{k+1}{2}
, then

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{j-1}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k-j}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+5\right)^{j-\frac{k+1}{2}}\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k-j},

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{j-1}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k-j}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+2\right)^{j-\frac{k+1}{2}}\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{k-j},

and similarly if
j<\frac{k+1}{2}
, using the fact that
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}\left(\mathcal{N}+1\right)^{s}=\left(\mathcal{N}-3\right)^{s}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
. Therefore, applying Lemma
10.2
and Lemma
10.4
on each term of the right-hand side of Equation (
179
) and the fact that
Nh^{3}\mathcal{C}_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}\leq\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left\lVert\Psi_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{k}{2}}}^{2}\leq C^{k}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert C_{p,a,q_{0}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}\,h^{3\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)}+C_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,N^{\frac{1}{2}}h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}}\right)\left\lVert\Psi_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{k}{2}}}\left\lVert\Psi_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{k}{2}+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)}}

which leads to the desired result.
∎

Moreover, by Proposition
9.2
and by weighted interpolation, we deduce that for any
t>0
,
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}_{t}}
is a bounded mapping from
\mathcal{G}_{k}
to
\mathcal{G}_{k}
. More precisely, for any
k\in[0,1/2]
, we have a bound of the form (
157
), and the same bound is valid for
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}
. Therefore, recalling that by definition
\mathsf{U}_{t,s}=\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}_{t}}^{*}\,e^{-i\mathsf{L}(t-s)/\hbar}\,\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}_{s}}
, and since
e^{-i\mathsf{L}(t-s)/\hbar}
commutes with the number operator, we obtain for
k\in[0,1/2]

(180)

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}_{t,s}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}\leq 3\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}+5\,N^{k}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Combining these three inequalities (
178
), (
176
), (
180
), and using the Duhamel formula, we obtain the main result of the section.

Proof of Proposition
10.1
.

Let
\mathsf{B}_{h}:=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\mathsf{B}
. Then the Duhamel formula can be written

\mathsf{U}_{t,0}=\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,0}+(\mathsf{U}\star\mathsf{B}_{h}\tilde{\mathsf{U}})_{t,0}

where we used the notation
\star
for the time convolution of operators

(\mathsf{U}\star\mathsf{V})_{t,s}:=\int_{s}^{t}\mathsf{U}_{t,s^{\prime}}\mathsf{V}_{s^{\prime},s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime},

We now define the iterated time convolution
\mathsf{U}^{(\star k)}
by
\mathsf{U}^{(\star 1)}=\mathsf{U}
for
k=1
and by
\mathsf{U}^{(\star k)}=\mathsf{U}\star\mathsf{U}^{(\star(k-1))}
for
k\geq 2
. With these notations, one can write the following iterated Duhamel’s formula

(181)

\mathsf{U}_{t,0}=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\tilde{\mathsf{U}}\star(\mathsf{B}_{h}\tilde{\mathsf{U}})^{(\star j)})_{t,0}+(\mathsf{U}\star(\mathsf{B}_{h}\tilde{\mathsf{U}})^{(\star k)})_{t,0}\,.

and from Inequality (
180
), we deduce

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}_{t,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left\lVert(\tilde{\mathsf{U}}\star(\mathsf{B}_{h}\tilde{\mathsf{U}})^{(\star j)})_{t,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}\\
+\int_{0}^{t}\left(3\left\lVert(\mathsf{B}_{h}\tilde{\mathsf{U}})^{(\star k)}_{s,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}+5N^{k_{0}}\left\lVert(\mathsf{B}_{h}\tilde{\mathsf{U}})^{(\star k)}_{s,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Since we know from Part
II
that
C_{T}:=\sup_{[0,T]}(\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}},\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}},\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}})
is bounded, we deduce that
C_{t,s}\leq C_{T}\,C^{k_{0}}\,C_{p,a,q_{0}}\left\lvert\kappa\right\rvert h^{-\alpha}\left(1+\mathcal{C}_{\infty}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(t-s\right)=:\lambda_{\alpha}\,C^{k_{0}}\left(t-s\right)
.

From inequalities (
176
) and (
178
), we obtain for any
0\leq s\leq t\leq T

\left\lVert\left(\mathsf{B}_{h}\tilde{\mathsf{U}}\right)_{t,s}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}\leq\frac{2^{k_{0}}\,C\,\lambda_{0}}{M}\,e^{\lambda_{\alpha}\,C^{k}\left(t-s\right)}

where
M=N^{\frac{1}{2}}h
, which leads to

\left\lVert\left(\mathsf{B}_{h}\tilde{\mathsf{U}}\right)^{(\star j)}_{t,s}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}j}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}\leq\frac{\left(C\lambda_{0}\right)^{j}2^{\left(k_{0}+j\right)\left(j+1\right)}}{M^{j}\left(j-1\right)!}\left(t-s\right)^{j-1}e^{\lambda_{\alpha}\,C^{k}\left(t-s\right)}\,.

Hence, for
\mathsf{U}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{U}_{t,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{\left(C\lambda_{0}\right)^{j}2^{\left(k_{0}+j\right)\left(j+1\right)}}{M^{j}\,j!}\,t^{j}e^{\lambda_{\alpha}\,C^{k}\,t}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}j}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\left(2^{k}C\lambda_{0}\right)^{k}}{M^{k}\,k!\,\lambda_{\alpha}}\,t^{k}e^{\lambda_{\alpha}\,C^{k}t}\left(2^{k_{0}\left(k+1\right)}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}k}}+N^{k_{0}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{M}\,e^{C_{M}\,\lambda_{\alpha}\,t}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}k}}+\frac{\left(2^{k}C\right)^{k}\lambda_{0}^{k-1}}{k!}\,t^{k}e^{\lambda_{\alpha}\,C^{k}t}\,\frac{N^{k_{0}}\,h^{3\alpha}}{M^{k}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k}}

where
C_{M}=C^{k+k_{0}}\left(1+\frac{1}{M}\right)
. Observing that
\lambda_{0}=h^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}
, this implies

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}_{t,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}\leq C_{M}\,e^{C_{M}\,\lambda_{\alpha}\,t}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}k}}\\
+\frac{2^{k}C}{k}\frac{\left(2^{k}C\right)^{k-1}\lambda_{\alpha}^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\,t^{k-1}e^{\lambda_{\alpha}\,C^{k}t}\,\frac{N^{k_{0}}\,h^{\alpha k}\,t}{M^{k}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k}}

and using the fact that for
x>0
,
\frac{x^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\leq e^{x}
, replacing the constant
C^{k}+2^{k}C
by
C^{k}
for some other numerical constant
C
in the second exponential and bounding
2^{k}C/k
by
C^{k}
, we can simplify a bit the result and write

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathsf{U}_{t,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{M}\,e^{C_{M}\,\lambda_{\alpha}\,t}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}k}}+C^{k}\,e^{\lambda_{\alpha}\,C^{k}t}\,\frac{N^{k_{0}}\,h^{\alpha k}\,t}{M^{k}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{M}\,e^{C_{M}\,\lambda_{\alpha}\,t}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k_{0}+\frac{3}{2}k}}+\frac{h^{\left(\alpha-1\right)k}}{N^{\frac{k}{2}-k_{0}}}\,t\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k}}\right).

∎

11.
Proofs of Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
3.2

We can now prove our general theorem.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

In order to get the result, we want to apply Proposition
10.1
. Hence, we define

\frac{1}{p_{\alpha}}:=\frac{\alpha}{3}+\frac{1}{2}.

The assumptions
\alpha\in\left[0,1\right]
and
\alpha>a-\frac{1}{2}
are equivalent to say that
p_{\alpha}\in\left[\tfrac{5}{6},2\right]\text{ and }p_{\alpha}<\mathfrak{b}
, and imply that (
80
) is a non-empty condition. Therefore,
p_{\alpha}
satisfies the assumption (
160
). Now we define

(182)

q_{1}:=q\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{1}{q_{0}}:=2\left(\frac{1}{p_{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)-\frac{1}{q_{1}}

so that Formula (
161
) holds with
p=p_{\alpha}
. Assumption (
80
) can be written

(183)

\frac{1}{q_{1}}\in\left[2\left(\frac{1}{p_{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right)-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{p_{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}}\right].

Definition (
182
) and Equation (
183
) together imply that
q_{0}
and
q_{1}
satisfy
2\leq q_{0}<q_{1}\leq\infty
.

Next, we have to check that we have a uniform in
h
bound for the quantity

\sup_{[0,T]}\Big(\left\lVert\rho(t)\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}},\mathcal{D}_{q_{0},q_{1}}(t),\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}(t)\Big)

appearing in the growth rate
\lambda_{\alpha}
defined in (
163
). This is done by using the propagation of regularity results for the Hartree–Fock equation of Part
II
. First, by our initial regularity assumptions and Proposition
6.1
, we deduce that
\left\lVert\rho(t)\right\rVert_{L^{p_{a}}}
is bounded uniformly in
h
and in
t\in[0,T]
for some
T=T_{\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}}
depending on the initial condition of the Hartree–Fock Equation (
5
). Then, by Proposition
6.3
we deduce that
\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\in\mathcal{W}^{1,q}(m_{n})
for any
q\in[2,q_{1})
, and so

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q_{0}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q_{1}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

is uniformly bounded on
[0,T]
. Moreover, by Lemma
6.1
, we obtain

\mathcal{D}_{q,q_{0}}=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q_{0}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\xi}\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{q_{1}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{q_{0},q_{1}}

so
\mathcal{D}_{q,q_{0}}
is also uniformly bounded on
[0,T]
. Therefore, by Proposition
10.1
, we deduce that

(184)

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}_{t,0}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{2p}}}^{2}\leq C_{M}^{2}\,e^{C_{M}\,\lambda\,t/h^{\alpha}}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3k}{2}+\frac{1}{2p}}}^{2}+\frac{h^{2k\left(\alpha-1\right)}}{N^{k-\frac{1}{p}}}\,t^{2}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3k}{2}}}^{2}\right)

with
\lambda
uniformly bounded in
t\in[0,T]
and in the Planck constant
h
. Then, by Proposition
9.1
, the following inequality holds

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C_{p}}{\min(N^{\frac{1}{2}},N\,h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}})}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{1}{2p}}}^{2}.

We conclude the proof by combining Inequality (
184
) with the above inequality.
∎

Next, we prove that from our general Theorem
4.1
, we can deduce our simplified mean-field results, i.e. Theorem
3.2
. To this end, we come back to the setting of density operators over the Fock space by the following Lemma

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}:=\left\lvert\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\Omega)\right\rvert^{2}
as defined in (
77
). Then for any
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}_{s}(\cF)
, that commutes with
\mathcal{N}
, there exists
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}
such that

(185)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\left\lvert\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\,\Psi)\right\rvert^{2}

and

(186)

\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{s}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\leq C_{s}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2N\right)^{s}\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)}

with
C_{s}=12^{s}\left(s+1\right)^{s}
.

Proof.

Let
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\Omega=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}})
. Then
\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rvert=\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}}
, and by the polar decomposition of
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, there exists a unique operator
U_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}
such that

\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}=U_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rvert,

with
\left\lVert U_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\psi\right\rVert_{\cF}=\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{\cF}
if
\psi\in\left(\ker{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\right)^{\perp}
and
\left\lVert U_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\psi\right\rVert_{\cF}=0
if
\psi\in\ker{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}}
(see e.g.
[
65
, Theorem VI.10]
). Then we define

\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}:=U_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\left\lvert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}}\right\rvert,

and
\Phi=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N})
,
\Psi:=\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{*}\Phi
. In particular,
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}\right\rvert^{2}
so Formula (
185
) is satisfied. Now from Lemma
9.2
, we have

\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{s}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}=\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{s}(\Psi-\Omega)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq 3^{s}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+2N+2s+2\right)^{s}\left(\Phi-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

Using the fact that
\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}
is an isometry,
\mathcal{N}_{l}\Phi=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{N}\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N})
,
\mathcal{N}_{r}\Phi=\mathsf{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}\mathcal{N})
and
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}
commutes with
\mathcal{N}
, we deduce that

\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{s}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{s}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)^{s}\left(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\cF)}.

By our choice of
U_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}
, it holds

\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)^{s}\left(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)=U_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\left(\left\lvert\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)^{s}\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}\right\rvert-\left\lvert\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)^{s}\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rvert\right)

with
\left\lVert U_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
. Now recall the Powers–Størmer inequality
[
64
, Lemma 4.1]
which tells that if
A
and
B
are nonnegative operators, then
\tr\!\big(\left\lvert A-B\right\rvert^{2}\big)\leq\tr\!\left(\left\lvert A^{2}-B^{2}\right\rvert\right)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{s}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{s}^{2}\left\lVert\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N}\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)^{s}\right\rvert^{2}-\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)^{s}\right\rvert^{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{s}^{2}\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)^{s}\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\left(\mathcal{N}+N\right)^{s}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\cF)}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

In the setting of Theorem
3.2
, since
a<1/2
, we can take
\alpha=0
in Theorem
4.1
, and the hypothesis for
q
implies that condition (
80
) is satisfied. With this choice, Theorem
4.1
yields for any
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N:1}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{p}}\leq\frac{C\,e^{\lambda\,t}}{\min(N^{\frac{1}{2}},N\,h^{\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}})}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{\frac{3}{2}k_{1}+\frac{1}{2p}}}^{2}\left(1+\frac{h^{-2k_{1}}}{N^{k_{1}-\frac{1}{p}}}\right).

Taking
k=\frac{3}{2}k_{1}+\frac{1}{2p}
, the hypothesis on
k
implies that
\frac{h^{-2k_{1}}}{N^{k_{1}-\frac{1}{p}}}\leq C
. Finally, we use Lemma
11.1
to get

\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}^{2}\leq 2^{k+1}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}+\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{k}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\right)\leq C_{k}\left(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}-\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{1}_{k}(\cF)}\right)

for some
k
dependent constant
C_{k}>0
.
∎

Appendix A
Existence of the Auxiliary Dynamics

The purpose of this appendix is to extend the result on the existence of the auxiliary dynamics for smooth potentials in the interaction picture given in the appendix of
[
11
]
to the case of singular interaction potentials of the form
K(x)=\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{-a}
for
0\leq a\leq 1
.

In this section,
\hbar
will not play any role in our analysis. Therefore, to simplify the presentation, we set
\hbar\equiv 1
. By Formula (
172
), the time-dependent operator
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}
defined in Equation (
159
) can be written

(187)

\tilde{\mathsf{G}}=\dG_{l}\!\left(H_{\omega}\right)-\dG_{r}\!\left(\overline{H_{\omega}}\right)+\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}+\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}+\mathsf{D}+\tilde{\mathsf{P}}+\tilde{\mathsf{P}}^{*}

where
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathsf{D}
are already defined after Equation (
159
) and

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{P}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\bigg(a^{*}_{r}(\bar{v}_{x})\,a_{l}^{*}(u_{x})\dG_{l}\!\left(u\left[{K_{x},u}\right]\right)+a^{*}_{r}(\bar{u}_{x})\,a_{l}^{*}(v_{x})\dG_{l}\!\left(v\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{u,K_{x}}\right]v+\left[{K_{x},v}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega_{x}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+a^{*}_{l}(v_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\bar{u}_{x})\dG_{r}\!\left(\bar{u}\left[{K_{x},\bar{u}}\right]\right)+a^{*}_{l}(u_{x})\,a_{r}^{*}(\bar{v}_{x})\dG_{r}\!\left(\bar{v}\left[{K_{x},\bar{v}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\dG^{+}_{r,l}\!\left(\left[{\bar{u},K_{x}}\right]\bar{v}+\left[{K_{x},\bar{v}}\right]\bar{u}\right)\dG_{r}\!\left(\bar{\omega}_{x}\right)\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

The goal is to show that the operator
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}
generates a unitary dynamics
\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}
in Fock space that satisfies the differential equation

(188)

i\,\partial_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}\Psi=\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}\Psi\ \text{ with }\ \tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{s,s}\Psi=\Psi,

for sufficiently smooth
\Psi\in\mathcal{G}
. To this end, it is convenient to consider the dynamics in the interaction picture. More precisely, define the operator

\widehat{\mathsf{G}}_{t}=-\mathsf{L}_{0}+\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{t}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

where
\mathsf{L}_{0}=\dG_{r}\!\left(\Delta\right)-\dG_{l}\!\left(\Delta\right)
and
\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}=\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t,0}
is the free evolution, i.e.
\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t,s}
solves

i\,\partial_{t}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t,s}\Psi=\mathsf{L}_{0}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t,s}\Psi,

with
\mathsf{U}_{s,s}^{(0)}\Psi=\Psi
. We will show that
\widehat{\mathsf{G}}_{t}
generates a unitary operator
\widehat{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}
in Fock space which in turn allows us to define the auxiliary dynamics by

\tilde{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}:=\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\widehat{\mathsf{U}}_{t,s}\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{s}.

which formally satisfies Equation (
188
).

Since much of the result in this appendix is similar to that of the appendix of
[
11
]
, we will only focus on the part of the result that relies explicitly on the regularity of the potential and refer the reader to
[
11
]
for a more complete proof of the result. Hence, the rest of this section will be devoted to prove that the mapping
t\mapsto\widehat{\mathsf{G}}_{t}\Psi
is Hölder continuous when
\Psi
is sufficiently smooth. More precisely, we define the homogeneous Sobolev-type double Fock space by the norm

(189)

\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}_{k}}:=\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{k-1/2}\dG((-\Delta)^{s})^{1/2}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}.

In particular,
\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{k}}=\left\lVert\mathcal{N}^{k}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}
.
The main proposition of this section is the following result.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\rho}
be a solution to the Hartree–Fock equation with initial condition
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}
satisfying (
39a
), (
39b
), and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho^{\mathrm{in}}(x)\,(1+\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq C
. Then there exists
T>0
and a constant
C_{T}
depending on
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\mathrm{in}}
such that for any
\left(t,s\right)\in[0,T]^{2}
,

\left\lVert\left(\widehat{\mathsf{G}}_{t}-\widehat{\mathsf{G}}_{s}\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left\lvert t-s\right\rvert^{\frac{3-2a}{7}}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{3/2}_{2}}\right).

Remark A.1
.

For a fixed
\hbar
, the global-in-time well-posedness of solutions to the Hartree–Fock equation is a standard result (see for instance
[
21
]
). However, the bounds of the propagated quantity may depend on
\hbar
. In particular, for a general fixed
\hbar
, the constant
C_{T}
in the above proposition may depend on
\hbar
.

Remark A.2
.

We know from Part
II
that the conditions (
39a
) and (
39b
) remain satisfied on
[0,T]
. In particular,
\left\lVert\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{n})}^{2}=\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2n}\right)
is uniformly bounded on
[0,T]
. To see that the third-order spatial moment
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho^{\mathrm{in}}(x)\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3}\right)
remains bounded, one can notice that

i\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3}\right)=\tr\!\left(\left[{\tfrac{\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2}}{2},\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3}}\right]\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)+\tr\!\left(\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3}}\right]\boldsymbol{\rho}\right).

The first term is controlled, using
[
48
, Formula (42)]
, by a term proportional to

\tr\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\left(\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3}+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{3}+1\right)\right).

The second term is zero since

\tr\!\left(\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3}}\right]\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\rho}(x,y)\right\rvert^{2}\frac{\left\lvert y\right\rvert^{3}-\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{3}}{\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert^{a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

is the integral of an anti-symmetric function of
x
and
y
. Then, by the standard Grönwall argument, one obtains the desired result.

It will be convenient to use the fact that the above defined norm (
189
) controls quantities of the form
\left\lVert\dG(A\nabla)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}
as stated in the following lemma.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}
and
\Psi\in\dot{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{1}
. Then
\left\lVert\dG(A\nabla)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{1}}
.

Proof.

Using the fact that
A
is a bounded operator, we obtain

\left\lVert\dG(A\nabla)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\leq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{j\leq n}\left\lVert\nabla_{x_{j}}\Psi^{(n)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\bigg)^{2}.

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and integration by parts, the last factor satisfies

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{j\leq n}\left\lVert\nabla_{x_{j}}\Psi^{(n)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\bigg)^{2}\leq\Braket{\mathcal{N}_{l}\Psi^{(n)}|\dG_{l}\!\left(-\Delta\right)\Psi^{(n)}}_{\mathcal{G}}=\left\lVert\dG_{l}\!\left(-\Delta\right)^{1/2}\mathcal{N}_{l}^{1/2}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}

which is bounded above by
\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{1}}
.
∎

To simplify some of the calculation, it will also be convenient to employ the following lemma.

Lemma A.2
.

For any self-adjoint integral operator
A
on
\mathfrak{h}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we have the identities

(190a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\dG_{l}\!\left(A\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\dG_{l}\!\left(A_{I}\right)

(190b)

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(A\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(A_{I}\right)

where
A_{I}:=e^{-it\Delta}\,A\,e^{it\Delta}
denotes the operator
A
in the interaction picture.

Proof.

By a direct computation, we see that

(191)

\left[{\mathsf{L}_{0},a_{x,l}}\right]=\left[{\dG_{l}\!\left(-\Delta\right),a_{x,l}}\right]=\Delta_{x}a_{x,l}.

Therefore, using the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula

e^{X}Ye^{-X}=Y+\left[{X,Y}\right]+\frac{1}{2!}\left[{X,\left[{X,Y}\right]}\right]+\ldots

and Equation (
191
), one can show the conjugation formula

\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}a_{x,l}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}=e^{it\Delta_{x}}a_{x,l}.

Hence, we arrive at the desired identity

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\dG_{l}\!\left(A\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}A(z_{1},z_{2})\,\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\,a_{z_{1},l}^{*}\,a_{z_{2},r}\,\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}e^{-it\Delta_{z_{1}}}A(z_{1},z_{2})\,e^{it\Delta_{z_{2}}}\,a^{*}_{z_{1},l}\,a_{z_{2},r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{2}.

This establishes (
190a
). The proof of Identity (
190b
) is similar.
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.1
.

To prove Proposition
A.1
, first notice that
\left[{\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t},\dG\!\left(-\Delta\right)}\right]=0
, which using Formula (
187
) allows us to write

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{G}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\Big(\dG_{l}\!\left(V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)-\dG_{r}\!\left(V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\Big)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\left(\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}+\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}+\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\,\mathsf{D}\,\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}+\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\left(\tilde{\mathsf{P}}+\tilde{\mathsf{P}}^{*}\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle=:\mathrm{I}_{t}+\mathrm{II}_{t}+\mathrm{III}_{t}+\mathrm{IV}_{t}.

We shall prove the Hölder continuity of
t\mapsto\widehat{\mathsf{G}}_{t}\Psi
by proving the property for each term
\mathrm{I}_{t}
,
\mathrm{II}_{t}
,
\mathrm{III}_{t}
, and
\mathrm{IV}_{t}
. This is the content of the following lemmas
A.3
,
A.4
,
A.5
,
A.6
. Combining these lemmas leads to the result.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
A.1
, there exists a constant
C_{T}
depending on the initial conditions such that

\left\lVert\partial_{t}\mathrm{I}_{t}\Psi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{G})}\leq C_{T}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{1}}\right).

Proof of Lemma
A.3
.

It suffices to consider the left contribution since the proof for the right contribution is exactly the same. Let us first handle the term with
V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
. Using Identity (
190a
), we see that

\displaystyle i\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left(\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\dG_{l}\!\left(V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\left(\dG_{l}\!\left(\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},-\Delta}\right]\right)+\dG_{l}\!\left(i\partial_{t}\rho*K\right)\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle=:\mathsf{J}_{1}+\mathsf{J}_{2}.

We start by estimating
\mathsf{J}_{1}\Psi
. We rewrite the commutator in
\mathsf{J}_{1}
by using the fact that

\dG_{l}\!\left(\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},-\Delta}\right]\right)=2\dG_{l}\!\left(\nabla V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\cdot\nabla\right)+\dG_{l}\!\left(\Delta V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right).

Then, since
\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}
is unitary and commutes with
\nabla
, we obtain

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\dG_{l}\!\left(\nabla V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\cdot\nabla\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert\nabla V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{1}},

where since
\nabla K\in L^{\mathfrak{b},\infty}
, we have

\left\lVert\nabla V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lvert\nabla K(x-y)\right\rvert\rho(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\leq\left\lVert\nabla K\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b},\infty}}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},1}}.

Similarly, for the second term, by Lemma
8.1
and the fact that
\nabla K\in L^{\mathfrak{b},\infty}
, we have that

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\dG_{l}\!\left(\Delta V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert\Delta V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\leq\left\lVert\nabla K\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b},\infty}}\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},1}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}.

By Proposition
6.1
, the norm of
\rho
in
L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},1}
remains bounded for
t\in[0,T]
. When
\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}\geq 2
, the same holds for
\nabla\rho
. Moreover, since
\hbar=1
,
\left\lVert\nabla\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq C\tr\!\left(\left(1-\Delta\right)\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)
is also bounded on
[0,T]
by Proposition
6.1
, and so
\nabla\rho
is in
L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{p})
for any
p\in[1,4]
. Hence, it follows that

(192)

\left\lVert\mathsf{J}_{1}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{1}}\right).

For the
\mathsf{J}_{2}
term, let us begin by recalling the fact that
\rho
satisfies the equation

\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla\cdot j_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=0

where
j_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\frac{1}{2}\diag\!\left(\boldsymbol{\rho}\,\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{p}\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\right)
is known as the probability current. Similarly as for
\mathsf{J}_{1}
, we have the estimate

(193)

\left\lVert\mathsf{J}_{2}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}=\left\lVert\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\dG_{l}\!\left(\nabla\cdot\left(j_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}*K\right)\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert j_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},1}}\left\lVert\nabla K\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b},\infty}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}.

The term
\left\lVert j_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime},1}}
is bounded as for
\rho
by Proposition (
6.4
) and the kinetic energy of
\boldsymbol{\rho}
.

Now let us handle the exchange term
\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
in term
\mathrm{I}_{t}
. Note that

i\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left(\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\dG_{l}\!\left(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\right)=\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\!\left(\dG_{l}\!\left(\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},-\Delta}\right]\right)+\dG_{l}\!\left(i\partial_{t}\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}=:\mathsf{J}_{3}+\mathsf{J}_{4}.

We start by rewriting the
\mathsf{J}_{3}
term. Observe we have that

\dG_{l}\!\left(\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},-\Delta}\right]\right)=2\dG_{l}\!\left((\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{x}\boldsymbol{\rho}})\cdot\nabla\right)+\dG_{l}\!\left(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right).

The two terms are handled in the same exact manner as before. We will only deal with the second term. By Lemma
8.1
and Inequality (
112
), we have that

(194)

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\dG_{l}\!\left(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{x}\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\leq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}},

and since
\hbar=1
, we have
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{x}\boldsymbol{\rho}=-\sum_{\mathrm{j}=1}^{3}\left[{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}},\left[{\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathrm{j}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]}\right]
and so by Lemma
6.5
,
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{x}\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}\right\rVert_{2}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a+2}\right\rVert_{2}
which remains bounded on
[0,T]
by Proposition
6.1
.
Hence we have the estimate

(195)

\left\lVert\mathsf{J}_{3}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{1}}\right).

For the
\mathsf{J}_{4}
term, we have that

\mathsf{J}_{4}=\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\left(\dG_{l}\!\left(\mathsf{X}_{\left[{-\Delta,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]}\right)+\dG_{l}\!\left(\mathsf{X}_{\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]}\right)-\dG_{l}\!\left(\mathsf{X}_{\left[{\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]}\right)\right)\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}.

To estimate the term with the Laplacian, we proceed as in Inequality (
194
) and use the fact that since
\hbar=1
,
\left[{-\Delta,\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]=\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2}\boldsymbol{\rho}-\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2}
. To estimate the second term, we use Inequality (
112
) to get

\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{a}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]\left(1+\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Then similarly as in Section
6.3
, we write
\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right]m=\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\boldsymbol{\rho}\,m}\right]-\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},m}\right]\boldsymbol{\rho}
and use Proposition
6.5
and Proposition
6.6
with
V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
instead of
E_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
. Similarly, to bound the last term, we use Inequality (
112
) and then Proposition
6.9
. Hence we have the estimate

(196)

\left\lVert\mathsf{J}_{4}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}.

The bound on
\partial_{t}\mathrm{I}_{t}
now follows by combining the inequalities for each part, i.e. (
192
), (
193
), (
195
) and (
196
).
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
A.1
, there exists a constant
C_{T}
depending on the initial conditions such that for any
\left(t,s\right)\in[0,T]^{2}
,

\left\lVert\left(\mathrm{II}_{t}-\mathrm{II}_{s}\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left\lvert t-s\right\rvert^{\frac{3-2a}{7}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{3/2}}.

Proof of Lemma
A.4
.

To estimate term
\mathrm{II}
, it suffices to focus on the first term of
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}
, which we will denote by
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{1}^{*}
. Furthermore, we decompose the singular potential into a long-range part and a singular part as follows

(197)

K=K_{R}^{L}+K_{R}^{S}:=C_{a}\left(\int^{R^{-2}}_{0}s^{\frac{a}{2}-1}\varphi_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s+\int^{\infty}_{R^{-2}}s^{\frac{a}{2}-1}\varphi_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\right).

for some
R
which we will determine shortly, and with
\varphi_{s}(x)=e^{-\pi\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{2}s}
. Consequently, we have the decomposition

N\,\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{1}^{*}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\left(K^{L}_{R}+K^{S}_{R}\right)\!(x-y)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\delta_{x}\,v\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\delta_{y}\,v\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=:\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{1,R}^{L*}+\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{1,R}^{S*}.

For the long-range part, we follow the proof of the bounded potential case as in
[
11
]
and show that
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{1,R}^{L*}
is time differentiable. Applying Lemma
A.2
and the operator identity
e^{-it\Delta}A(x)\,e^{it\Delta}=A(x-2it\nabla)
, we can now rewrite
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{L}_{1,R}
as follows

\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{L*}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{K^{L}_{R}}(y)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u_{I}\,e^{iy\cdot\left(x-2it\nabla\right)}\,v_{I}\right)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u_{I}\,e^{-iy\cdot\left(x-2it\nabla\right)}\,v_{I}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

where
A_{I}:=e^{-it\Delta}A\,e^{it\Delta}
denotes the operator
A
in the interaction picture. To estimate the time derivative of
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{L}_{1,R}
, we make the observation that

i\,\partial_{t}(u_{I}\,e^{iy\cdot(x-2it\nabla)}\,v_{I})=e^{-it\Delta}\left(u\left[{e^{iy\cdot x},-\Delta}\right]v\right)e^{it\Delta}\\
+e^{-it\Delta}\left(\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},u}\right]e^{iy\cdot x}\,v+u\,e^{iy\cdot x}\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},v}\right]\right)e^{it\Delta}.

Applying Lemma
8.1
, we have the estimates

(198)

\begin{split}\left\lVert\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u_{I}e^{iy\cdot(x-2it\nabla)}v_{I}\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}&\leq 2\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}},\\
\left\lVert\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\partial_{t}(u_{I}\,e^{iy\cdot\left(x-2it\nabla\right)}\,v_{I})\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}&\leq C_{T}\left\langle y\right\rangle^{2}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\left\langle\nabla\right\rangle v\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}},\end{split}

where
C_{T}=C\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(1+\left\lVert V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)
is finite, and
\left\langle y\right\rangle^{2}=1+\left\lvert y\right\rvert^{2}
. In particular, it follows from (
198
) that we have the inequality

\left\lVert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{L*}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lvert\widehat{K^{L}_{R}}(y)\right\rvert\left\langle y\right\rangle^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}.

To complete the estimate, we need to compute the
L^{1}
-norm of
\widehat{K^{L}_{R}}
to get the explicit dependence of the constant on
R
. Using the fact that
\widehat{\varphi}_{s}=s^{-\frac{3}{2}}\varphi_{1/s}
, we have

(199)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lvert\widehat{K^{L}_{R}}\right\rvert\left\langle y\right\rangle^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{0}^{R^{-2}}\!\!\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}s^{\frac{a-5}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{s}\left\lvert y\right\rvert^{2}}\left\langle y\right\rangle^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s=\frac{3}{\pi}\left(\tfrac{R^{-\left(a+2\right)}}{a+2}+\tfrac{R^{-a}}{a}\right).

Therefore, provided
R<1
, we obtain the estimate

\left\lVert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left(\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{L*}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{6}{\pi a}R^{-(a+2)}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}

which implies for any
(t,s)\in[0,T]^{2}
,

(200)

\left\lVert\left(\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{L*}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}-\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{s}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{L*}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{s}\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{6}{\pi a}\,R^{-(a+2)}\left\lvert t-s\right\rvert\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}.

For the singular part, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\left\lvert\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{S*}_{1,R}\Psi_{2}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Braket{a_{l}(u_{x})\Psi_{1}|a^{*}_{r}(\overline{v_{x}})\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,K^{S}_{R,x}v\right)\Psi_{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right\rvert\\
\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lVert a_{l}(u_{x})\Psi_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lVert a^{*}_{r}(\overline{v_{x}})\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,K^{S}_{R,x}v\right)\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Applying Lemma
8.1
and the fact that
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
yields

\left\lVert a^{*}_{r}(\overline{v_{x}})\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,K^{S}_{R,x}v\right)\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq(N\rho(x))^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert K_{R,x}^{S}v\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1/2}},

which gives us

\left\lvert\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{S*}_{1,R}\Psi_{2}}\right\rvert\leq C\,N^{\frac{1}{2}}\Braket{\Psi_{1}|\mathcal{N}\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho(x)\left\lVert K^{S}_{R,x}v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\Psi_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1/2}}.

Since
\diag\!\left(v^{2}\right)=N\,\rho
, we see that

\displaystyle\left\lVert K^{S}_{R,x}v\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int^{\infty}_{R^{-2}}s^{\frac{a}{2}-1}\left\lVert\varphi_{s,x}\,v\right\rVert_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=N^{\frac{1}{2}}\int^{\infty}_{R^{-2}}s^{\frac{a}{2}-1}\left(\left\lvert\varphi_{s}\right\rvert^{2}*\rho\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\!(x)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq N^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{R^{-2}}^{\infty}s^{\frac{a}{2}-1}\left\lVert\varphi_{s}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{T}\,N^{\frac{1}{2}}\,R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}.

Hence, by duality, it follows that
\left\lVert\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{S}_{1,R}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{3/2}}
. By a similar argument, one can also show the same inequality for the dual operator
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{S*}_{1,R}
. Therefore,

(201)

\left\lVert\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{S*}_{1,R}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\,N\,R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{3/2}}.

Combining (
200
) and (
201
), we obtain that for any
(t,s)\in[0,T]^{2}
and any
R\in(0,1)
, the following inequality holds

\left\lVert\left(\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}_{1}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}-\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{s}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}_{1}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{s}\right)\Psi\right\rVert\leq C_{T}\left(R^{-(a+2)}\left\lvert t-s\right\rvert+R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\right)\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{3/2}}.

In particular, if
t\neq s
, one can take
R^{\frac{7}{2}}=\frac{\left\lvert t-s\right\rvert}{T}\leq 1
, leading to

\left\lVert\left(\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}_{1}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}-\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{s}\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}^{*}_{1}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{s}\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left\lvert t-s\right\rvert^{\frac{3-2a}{7}}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{3/2}}.

If
t=s
, we can make
R\to 0
to obtain the same inequality.
∎

Next, let us consider the type
\mathrm{III}
terms.

Lemma A.5
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
A.1
, there exists a constant
C_{T}
depending on the initial conditions such that for any
\left(t,s\right)\in[0,T]^{2}
,

\left\lVert\left(\mathrm{III}_{t}-\mathrm{III}_{s}\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left\lvert t-s\right\rvert^{\frac{3-2a}{7}}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{3/2}_{2}}\right).

Proof of Lemma
A.5
.

Let us focus on the first term of
\mathsf{D}
which we denote by
\mathsf{D}_{1}
. The proof of Hölder
continuity of
\mathsf{D}_{1}
is similar to that of
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{1}
. Using (
197
), we decompose
\mathsf{D}_{1}
into two parts

2\,N\,\mathsf{D}_{1}=\mathsf{D}_{1,R}^{L}+\mathsf{D}_{1,R}^{S}.

For the long-range part, we begin by writing

\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\mathsf{D}_{1,R}^{L}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{K^{L}_{R}}(y)\dG_{l}\!\left(u_{I}\,e^{iy\cdot\left(x-2it\nabla\right)}u_{I}\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(u_{I}\,e^{-iy\cdot\left(x-2it\nabla\right)}\,u_{I}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

Using the identity

i\partial_{t}(u_{I}\,e^{iy\cdot\left(x-2it\nabla\right)}\,u_{I})=e^{-it\Delta}\left(\eta\,e^{iy\cdot x}\,u+u\,e^{iy\cdot x}\,\eta+u\left[{e^{iy\cdot x},-\Delta}\right]u\right)e^{it\Delta},

where
\eta=[V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},u]
, and Lemma
8.1
, we deduce the following estimates

(202)

\begin{split}\left\lVert\dG_{l}\!\left(u_{I}\,e^{iy\cdot(x-2it\nabla)}u_{I}\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}&\leq\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}\\
\left\lVert\dG_{l}\!\left(\partial_{t}(u_{I}\,e^{iy\cdot(x-2it\nabla)}\,u_{I})\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}&\leq\left(\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lvert y\right\rvert^{2}\right)\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}+\left\lvert y\right\rvert\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{1}}.\end{split}

By the above inequalities (
202
) and Formula (
199
), provided
R\in(0,1)
, we get an estimate of the form

\left\lVert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\left(\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\mathsf{D}^{L}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{a}\left(1+\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)R^{-(a+2)}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{2}}\right).

To handle the singular part, we begin by writing
u=1-w
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{S}_{1,R}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K^{S}_{R}(x-y)\,a^{*}_{x,l}\,a^{*}_{y,l}\,a_{l}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K^{S}_{R}(x-y)\,a^{*}_{l}(w_{x})\,a^{*}_{l}(w_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K^{S}_{R}(x-y)\,a^{*}_{x,l}\,a^{*}_{l}(w_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}K^{S}_{R}(x-y)\,a^{*}_{l}(w_{x})\,a^{*}_{y,l}\,a_{l}(u_{y})\,a_{l}(u_{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=:\mathsf{I}_{1}+\mathsf{I}_{2}+\mathsf{I}_{3}+\mathsf{I}_{4}.

To estimate
\mathsf{I}_{1}
, we begin by observing that

(\mathsf{I}_{1}\Psi)^{(n,m)}\!\left(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m}\right)=\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq n}K^{S}_{R}(x_{i}-x_{j})\left(\bar{u}^{(x_{j})}\bar{u}^{(x_{k})}\Psi^{(n,m)}\right)\!\left(\underline{x}_{n},\underline{y}_{m}\right)

where
u^{(x_{j})}
is the operator acting on the variable
x_{j}
and
\underline{x}_{n}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
. Defining
g(x,y):=\left\lVert\bar{u}^{(x)}\bar{u}^{(y)}\Psi^{(n,m)}\!\left(x,y,\dots\right)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3\left(n+m-2\right)})}
, it follows from the triangle inequality and the anti-symmetry of
\Psi
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathsf{I}_{1}\Psi)^{(n,m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq n\left(n-1\right)\left(\iint_{\left\lvert z\right\rvert\leq R}\frac{\left\lvert g(x+z,x)\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{2a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq n^{2}R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lVert\frac{g_{R,x}(z)}{\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{a}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{z}(B_{1})}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

where
g_{R,x}(z)=g(x+zR,x)
and
B_{1}
is the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Now let
T_{B_{1}}
be the bounded extension operator

\displaystyle T_{B_{1}}

\displaystyle:H^{a}({B_{1}})\to H^{a}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

\displaystyle\forall x\in B_{1},(T_{B_{1}}g)(x)

\displaystyle=g(x)

which exists as proved for example in
[
20
, Theorem IX.7]
when
a\in\mathbb{N}
. One can proceed by interpolation when
a\in\mathbb{R}
. Then one has

\left\lVert\frac{g_{R,x}(z)}{\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{a}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{z}(B_{1})}=\left\lVert\frac{T_{B_{1}}g_{R,x}(z)}{\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{a}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{z}(B_{1})}\leq\left\lVert\frac{T_{B_{1}}g_{R,x}(z)}{\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{a}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

and by Hardy–Rellich’s inequality

\left\lVert\frac{T_{B_{1}}g_{R,x}(z)}{\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{a}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\left\lVert\left(-\Delta\right)^{\frac{a}{2}}T_{B_{1}}g_{R,x}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C_{T_{B_{1}}}\left\lVert g_{R,x}\right\rVert_{H^{a}(B_{1})}.

For
\mathsf{I}_{1}
, this leads to

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathsf{I}_{1}\Psi)^{(n,m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,n^{2}R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left(\iint_{\left\lvert z\right\rvert\leq 1}\left\lvert(-\Delta)^{\frac{a}{2}}g_{R,x}(z)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert g_{R,x}(z)\right\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,n^{2}\left(\iint_{\left\lvert z\right\rvert\leq R}\left\lvert\left(-\Delta\right)_{z}^{\frac{a}{2}}g\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert R^{-a}g\right\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Now by Hölder’s inequality and by Sobolev’s embedding, for any
\alpha>0
and any
f\in H^{\alpha}
,

\int_{\left\lvert z\right\rvert\leq R}\left\lvert f(z)\right\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2p^{\prime}}}^{2}\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{B_{R}}\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq C\,R^{2\alpha}\left\lVert(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2},

with
p=\frac{3}{2\alpha}
. In particular, taking
f=\left(-\Delta\right)_{z}^{\frac{a}{2}}g
and
\alpha=\frac{3}{2}-a
and taking
f=R^{-a}g
and
\alpha=\frac{3}{2}
we obtain for
\mathsf{I}_{1}

\left\lVert(\mathsf{I}_{1}\Psi)^{(n,m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\,n^{2}\,R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert(-\Delta)^{3/4}_{x}g\right\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Using the fact that
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
, we can control the
L^{2}
norm on the right-hand side of the above inequality by

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(-\Delta\right)_{x}^{3/4}g\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\left(\bar{u}^{(y)}\left(-\Delta\right)_{x}^{3/4}\bar{u}^{(x)}\Psi^{(n,m)}\right)\!\left(x,y,\dots\right)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3\left(n+m\right)})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\left(\left(-\Delta\right)^{3/4}\bar{u}\right)^{(x)}\Psi^{(n,m)}\left(x,\dots\right)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3\left(n+m\right)})}

and using the fact that
\bar{u}=1-\bar{w}
, we finally obtain

\left\lVert\mathsf{I}_{1}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\frac{C}{N}\,R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{2}}+\left\lVert\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{\frac{3}{2}}w\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\right).

The other
\mathsf{I}_{i}
terms are less singular and treated in the same way, leading to

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\mathsf{D}^{S}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\,R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{N}\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{3/2}_{2}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\right).

By the same argument as in the case of
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{1}
, we see that
\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\mathsf{D}_{1}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi
is also Hölder continuous in time.
∎

Finally, let us handle type
\mathrm{IV}
terms.

Lemma A.6
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
A.1
, there exists a constant
C_{T}
depending on the initial conditions such that for any
\left(t,s\right)\in[0,T]^{2}
,

\left\lVert\left(\mathrm{IV}_{t}-\mathrm{IV}_{s}\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left\lvert t-s\right\rvert^{\frac{3-2a}{7}}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{2}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\right).

Proof of Lemma
A.6
.

For this case, it suffices to consider

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u\,\delta_{x}\,v\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(u\left[{K_{x},u}\right]\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle J_{12}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{K_{x},u}\right]v+\left[{v,K_{x}}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

Following the same routine as before, we decompose the operators into a long-range part and a singular part using Formula (
197
). Again, we will denote the decomposition by
J_{1,R}^{L}+J_{1,R}^{S}
and likewise for
J_{12}
. Applying Lemma
A.2
, we can now rewrite
J^{L}_{1,R}
as follows

\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}J^{L}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{K^{L}_{R}}(y)\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(u_{I}\,e^{-iy\cdot(x-2it\nabla)}v_{I}\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(u_{I}\left[{e^{-iy\cdot(x-2it\nabla)},w_{I}}\right]\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

Since we have that

i\,\partial_{t}\!\left(u_{I}\left[{e^{-iy\cdot(x-2it\nabla)},w_{I}}\right]\right)=e^{-it\Delta}\left(\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},u}\right]\left[{e^{-iy\cdot x},w}\right]\right)e^{it\Delta}\\
+e^{-it\Delta}\left(u\left[{\left[{e^{iy\cdot x},-\Delta}\right],w}\right]+u\left[{e^{-iy\cdot x},\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},w}\right]}\right]\right)e^{it\Delta}

then by Lemma
8.1
, since
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
, we have the estimate

(203a)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\dG_{l}\!\left(u_{I}\left[{e^{-iy\cdot(x-2it\nabla)},w_{I}}\right]\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}

(203b)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\dG_{l}\!\left(\partial_{t}\!\left(u_{I}\left[{e^{-iy\cdot(x-2it\nabla)},w_{I}}\right]\right)\right)\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{T}\left\langle y\right\rangle^{2}\left\lVert\left\langle\boldsymbol{p}\right\rangle^{2}w\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}.

where
\mathcal{C}_{T}=C\sup_{[0,T]}\!\left(1+\left\lVert V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)
. In particular, by inequalities (
198
), (
203
), and (
199
), we have that

\left\lVert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}J^{L}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\,R^{-(a+2)}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}.

The singular part follows from Lemma
10.2
and Remark
10.2
. More precisely, we have that

(204)

\left\lVert\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}J^{S}_{1,R}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\,R^{\frac{3}{2}-a}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}.

Repeating the argument of
\tilde{\mathsf{Q}}_{1}
shows that
\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}J_{1}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}\Psi
is Hölder continuous in time.

Lastly, let us estimate the operator
J_{12}
. We begin by writing

(205a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}J_{12}\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{K_{x,I},w_{I}}\right]v_{I}\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega_{x,I}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(205b)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{K_{x,I},v_{I}}\right]u_{I}\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega_{x,I}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

It suffices to handle Term (
205b
) since Term (
205a
) can be treated in a similar manner. Taking its time-derivative yields

\displaystyle i\,\partial_{t}\eqref{eq:def_J12_term2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(i\partial_{t}(\left[{K_{x,I},v_{I}}\right]u_{I})\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega_{x,I}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{K_{x,I},v_{I}}\right]u_{I}\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(i\partial_{t}\omega_{x,I}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=:\mathsf{I}_{5}+\mathsf{I}_{6}.

Let us first consider
\mathsf{I}_{6}
. Notice, we have the identity

i\,\partial_{t}\omega_{x,I}=e^{-it\Delta}\left(2\,\nabla_{1}\omega_{x}\cdot\nabla_{1}+\Delta_{2}\omega_{x}+\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\omega}\right]_{x}\right)e^{it\Delta}.

In particular, we can write

\displaystyle\mathsf{I}_{6}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{K_{x},v}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(2\,\nabla_{1}\omega_{x}\cdot\nabla\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{K_{x},v}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\Delta_{x}\omega_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{K_{x},v}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\omega}\right]_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle=:\mathsf{J}_{1}+\mathsf{J}_{2}+\mathsf{J}_{3}.

To bound
\mathsf{J}_{1}
, it suffices to estimate the following quantity

(206)

\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\left[{K_{x},v}\right]u)(z_{1},z_{2})\,\nabla_{1}\omega(x_{n},x)\cdot\nabla\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}

where
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\ldots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{n}
and
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{2}
. Let us also break the commutator, that is,

(
206
)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\frac{v(z_{1},z)\,u(z,z_{2})}{\left\lvert x-z_{1}\right\rvert^{a}}\,\nabla_{1}\omega(x_{n},x)\cdot\nabla\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\frac{v(z_{1},z)\,u(z,z_{2})}{\left\lvert x-z\right\rvert^{a}}\,\nabla_{1}\omega(x_{n},x)\cdot\nabla\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}.

Since
u=1-w
where
w
is a Hilbert–Schmidt operator, we will focus on the identity part. Using the fact that
\omega=v^{2}
and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we see that

\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{v(z_{1},z_{2})}{\left\lvert x-z_{1}\right\rvert^{a}}\,\nabla_{1}\omega(x_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{x_{n}}\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}.\\
=\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\frac{v(z,x)}{\left\lvert x-z_{1}\right\rvert^{a}}\,v(z_{1},z_{2})\nabla_{1}v(x_{n},z)\cdot\nabla_{x_{n}}\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}\\
\leq\sup_{z_{1}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\rho(x)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\left\lvert x-z_{1}\right\rvert^{a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\nabla_{1}v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{z}}\left\lVert\nabla\Psi^{(n)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})},

since
\left\lVert v_{x}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}=\rho(x)^{1/2}
. Note that by Young’s and Hölder’s inequalities,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\rho(x)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\left\lvert x-z_{1}\right\rvert^{a}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq C\left\lVert\rho^{1/2}\right\rVert_{L^{\frac{3}{3-a}}}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho(x)\left\langle x\right\rangle^{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

provided
k>3-2a
. Hence, we have the estimate

\left\lVert\mathsf{J}_{1}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{2}^{1}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\right).

The other two terms
\mathsf{J}_{2}
and
\mathsf{J}_{3}
can be handled in the same manner since
v
is sufficiently smooth and
\left\lVert V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
and
\left\lVert\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{y}}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\rho}\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2+a}\right\rVert_{2}
are bounded. Thus, it follows that

\left\lVert\mathsf{I}_{6}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left(\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{2}^{1}}+\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\right).

Lastly, we handle the
\mathsf{I}_{5}
term. Since we have that

\displaystyle i\,\partial_{t}\!\left(\left[{K_{x,I},v_{I}}\right]u_{I}\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-it\Delta}\left(\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},u}\right]\right)e^{it\Delta}

\displaystyle\qquad+e^{-it\Delta}\left(\left[{\left[{\Delta,K_{x}}\right],v}\right]u+\left[{K_{x},\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},v}\right]}\right]u\right)e^{it\Delta}

then we can write

\displaystyle\mathsf{I}_{5}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{K_{x},v}\right]\left[{-V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}+\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},w}\right]\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{K_{x},\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},v}\right]}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathsf{U}^{(0)*}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\dG^{+}_{l,r}\!\left(\left[{\left[{\Delta,K_{x}}\right],v}\right]u\right)\dG_{l}\!\left(\omega_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathsf{U}^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle=:\mathsf{J}_{4}+\mathsf{J}_{5}+\mathsf{J}_{6}.

Term
\mathsf{J}_{4}
and
\mathsf{J}_{5}
can be estimated in the same manner as in the previous case, since
\left[{-V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}+\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},w}\right]
is a bounded operator and
\left[{V_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}-\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}},v}\right]
is a Hilbert–Schmidt operator. Hence, it suffices to estimate
\mathsf{J}_{6}
.

To bound
\mathsf{J}_{6}
, it suffices to estimate the following quantity

(207)

\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left[{\Delta K_{x}+2\nabla K_{x}\cdot\nabla,v}\right]\!(z_{1},z_{2})\,\omega(x_{n},x)\,\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}.

In the case
a=1
, we have that

(
207
)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(v\,\delta_{x})(z_{1},z_{2})\,\omega(x_{n},x)\,\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{x-z_{1}}{\left\lvert x-z_{1}\right\rvert^{3}}\cdot\nabla_{1}v(z_{1},z_{2})\,\omega(x_{n},x)\,\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}.

For the first term, we have

\left\lVert v(z_{1},z_{2})\,\omega(x_{n},z_{2})\,\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}\\
\leq\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{y}}\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{y}}\left\lVert\Psi^{(n)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}\leq C\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\omega\left\lvert\boldsymbol{p}\right\rvert^{2}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\Psi^{(n)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}.

For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{x-z_{1}}{\left\lvert x-z_{1}\right\rvert^{3}}\cdot\nabla_{1}v(z_{1},z_{2})\,\omega(x_{n},x)\,\Psi^{(n,m)}(\underline{x}_{n-1},x_{n},\underline{y}_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{z}_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{z_{1}}\!\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\rho(x)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\left\lvert x-z_{1}\right\rvert^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\nabla_{1}v\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\Psi^{(n)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{x}_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\underline{y}_{n})}.

where the first integral term is controlled by
\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{L^{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The case when
0<a<1
is similar, except that when
a\leq 1/2
, we need to estimate the last quantity with moments in
x
. Thus, it follows that

\left\lVert\mathsf{I}_{5}\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{T}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}

which completes the proof.
∎
